1.The risk-free rate, one of the three CAPM inputs, should be estimated from the current, not forecast, interest rate.69

Introduction
Q.PLEASE STATE YOUR NAME AND PROFESSIONAL AFFILIATION.
A.My name is Mark E. Ellis. I am an economic and financial consultant. My business address is 8595 Nottingham Place, La Jolla, CA 92037.
Q.ON WHOSE BEHALF ARE YOU TESTIFYING?
A.I am testifying on behalf of Georgia Interfaith Power and Light (GIPL).
Q.DO YOU CERTIFY UNDER PENALTY OF PERJURY THAT, TO THE BEST OF YOUR KNOWLEDGE, THE TESTIMONY YOU WILL GIVE IN THIS PROCEEDING IS TRUE AND CORRECT?
A.Yes.
Q.PLEASE SUMMARIZE YOUR EDUCATION AND PROFESSIONAL WORK EXPERIENCE.
A.I graduated from Harvard University with a Bachelor of Science in Mechanical and Materials Sciences and Engineering and from the Massachusetts Institute of Technology with a Master of Science in Technology and Policy.
I have over 25 years of professional experience in the energy industry. Before starting my consulting practice in 2020, I led the strategy function at Sempra Energy for fifteen years. My responsibilities included developing and implementing the enterprise-wide cost of capital estimation process. This critical corporate finance function entailed thorough and ongoing research of the academic and practitioner literature on the historical cost of capital and the various cost of capital estimation methodologies and models; creating a process to estimate, quarterly, the forward-looking, risk-adjusted cost of capital for Sempra’s portfolio of companies spanning a variety of geographies and lines of business; and calibrating the results against historical data and reputable, objective third-party estimates.
Previously, I held various positions in strategy, project development, and engineering with McKinsey, ExxonMobil, Southern California Edison, and Sanyo Electric. Last year, I provided expert testimony on behalf of The Utility Reform Network (TURN) before the California Public Utilities Commission in PG&E’s application (A.21-20-04-23) for a $7.5-billion wildfire cost securitization. Earlier in 2022, I provided expert testimony on behalf of The Protect Our Communities Foundation (PCF) before the California Public Utilities Commission in the three California utilities’ application (A.21-08-013 et seq.) to suspend the Cost of Capital Mechanism (CCM). I am currently working on or have completed other utility regulatory cases in New Hampshire, Hawaii, Georgia, and Wisconsin. Attachment MEE-1 contains more detail on my background.
Summary of Conclusions
Q.WHAT IS THE PURPOSE OF YOUR TESTIMONY IN THIS PROCEEDING?
A.I have been asked by GIPL to assess Georgia Power Company’s (GPC) test year 2023 cost of capital application to analyze and calculate the return on equity (ROE) and equity ratio that are (1) adequate to ensure that GPC, from 2023 to 2025, earns a return on its investments “commensurate with returns on investments in other enterprises having corresponding risk,” and (2) “sufficient to assure confidence in the financial integrity of the enterprise, so as to maintain its credit and to attract capital.”
Q.GENERALLY, WHAT CONCLUSIONS DO YOU REACH FROM YOUR ANALYSIS AND CALCULATIONS?
A.The Commission can substantially reduce the authorized ROE requested by Georgia Power, and thereby customer costs (by a billion dollars per year), while still enabling Georgia Power to attract debt and equity investment capital, fairly compensate investors for risk, and maintain Georgia Power’s current credit rating.
Q.WHAT ARE YOUR RECOMMENDED AUTHORIZED ROE AND EQUITY RATIO?
A.I recommend an authorized return on equity (ROE) equal to Georgia Power’s cost of equity (COE) and paired with an equity ratio that, together with my recommended ROE, minimizes customer costs while maintaining Georgia Power’s financial integrity, fairly compensating debt and equity investors for risk, and improving Georgia Power’s credit rating. My recommended authorized ROE is 5.54% at an equity ratio of 61.7%.
Summary of Findings
Georgia Power’s cost of capital testimony employs flawed models and assumptions that systematically produce upwardly biased ROE estimates.
Q.THERE IS A SIGNIFICANT DIFFERENCE BETWEEN YOUR RECOMMENDATIONS AND GEORGIA POWER’S RECOMMENDED ROE AND EQUITY RATIO. HOW DO YOU EXPLAIN THAT?
A.The divergence arises from differences in both the models and input assumptions used to determine our respective ROE and equity recommendations.
Georgia Power’s cost of capital expert, Mr. James Coyne, uses four models in developing his recommended return on equity (ROE). Two of those models, the Risk Premium Analysis (RPA) and Expected Earnings Analysis (EEA), both suffer from a severe, invalidating conceptual flaw: they are based on utilities’ authorized or forecast return on equity, not their actual cost of equity (COE), or the “the return to the equity owner … commensurate with returns on investments in other enterprises having corresponding risks.” Thus, these two models are not commonly used in finance outside of utility regulatory proceedings because they do not actually estimate the cost of equity. These models are akin to developing a nutrition recommendation based on what people actually eat, not on what they should eat to maintain a healthy weight.
The other two models used by Mr. Coyne – the discounted cash flow model (DCF) and capital asset pricing model (CAPM) – are widely used throughout finance to estimate the cost of capital. But Mr. Coyne’s implementations of each suffer from numerous flaws which bias his results upward.
In implementing the DCF, Mr. Coyne uses unnecessarily long trailing price histories to calculate the dividend yield, which bias his figures upward, and he assumes demonstrably unrealistic, economically impossible long-term dividend growth rates.
In implementing the CAPM, he uses an interest rate forecast long and widely known to be systematically upwardly biased. He cherry-picks a beta calculation methodology that does not reflect current investor risk perceptions and applies the “Blume” adjustment that is not valid for utilities, both of which upwardly bias his results. He fails to examine other, more robust beta estimation methodologies, investigate whether the pandemic-related changes in market conditions and investor perceptions of utility risk were temporary or have been sustained, or compare his results to the long-term history of utility betas.
Mr. Coyne’s chosen methodology does not reflect the wide range of ways beta could be estimated, each of which could produce dramatically different results. As Nobel laureate Fischer Black, one of the pioneers of empirical testing of beta and the CAPM, famously admonished, “Watch out for data mining!” – running an analysis several different ways but reporting only the outcomes that support one’s conclusions.
To estimate the CAPM market risk premium, Mr. Coyne uses the same flawed implementation of the DCF used for his proxy group, again producing an economically impossible result.
Finally, Mr. Coyne fails to adjust his ROE estimates for differences in equity ratio among the proxy group members, and between the proxy group average and Georgia Power.
Mr. Coyne’s capital structure recommendation is similarly deficient. While the testimony of Georgia Power’s credit expert, Mr. David M. Fetter, refers to key metrics used in assessing credit quality, Mr. Coyne does not provide any analysis or calculations demonstrating how ROE affects those metrics, and how the impact of ROE on those metrics influences his proposed equity ratio. Regulators in other states have authorized ROEs that are substantially lower than those requested by Georgia Power, with comparable or lower equity ratios, without adversely impacting their utilities’ credit ratings, suggesting the Commission can substantially reduce the ROE requested by Georgia Power, and thereby customer costs, while maintaining its credit rating.
More rigorous, fact-based analysis of Georgia Power’s COE and credit metrics yields a recommended ROE roughly 50% lower and an equity ratio slightly higher than Georgia Power’s proposal.
Q.PLEASE SUMMARIZE THE MAIN FINDINGS OF YOUR ROE ANALYSES FOR GEORGIA POWER.
A.Like Mr. Coyne, I use the DCF and CAPM, two models that are widely used throughout finance, to estimate the cost of capital. Unlike Mr. Coyne, I am careful to use realistic and rigorously supported assumptions about long-term dividend growth rates, current interest rates, and risk profiles and premia.
As I explain in Section III.A below, the equity ratio required to maintain any given level of credit quality depends on the ROE. Consequently, the ROE and equity ratio must be determined jointly. Instead of Mr. Coyne’s crude equity ratio peer group comparison, which does not consider the most important metrics of credit quality, I model the inter-relationships between key credit metrics, ROE, and equity ratio to arrive at the optimal equity ratio that minimizes customer costs while meeting the return and credit quality requirements of both equity and debt investors.
Figure 1 summarizes the key findings of my review of Mr. Coyne’s analysis, the modifications required to correct its deficiencies, the resulting COE estimates, and my ROE and equity ratio recommendations. More rigorous, fact-based, and accurate analyses result in a substantially lower recommendation for Georgia Power’s ROE: 5.54%, roughly one half of Mr. Coyne’s recommended 11.00%, and a slightly higher equity ratio, 61.7% vs. Mr. Coyne’s 56%.
Based on my analysis of Georgia Power’s general rate case filings, its proposed combined rate of return on both debt and equity, grossed up for taxes, accounts for more than 25% of its revenue requirement. My recommended ROE and equity ratio would reduce Georgia Power customer costs by approximately 11%, over $1 billion, per year.

Organization of testimony
Q.How is your testimony organized?
A.First, I review a few key conceptual issues related to the cost of capital and capital structure. Next, I provide a detailed assessment of Mr. Coyne’s cost of equity estimation methodology and implementation. For the DCF and CAPM, the two of Mr. Coyne’s four cost of equity models that are valid, I explain various modifications to his methodology and assumptions to correct for the deficiencies in Mr. Coyne’s analyses and then provide the resulting COE estimates. I then apply the capital structure concepts reviewed in the first part of my testimony to determine the optimal equity ratio that minimizes customer costs while satisfying the demands of equity and debt investors. Finally, I estimate the potential savings to customers from adopting my recommended ROE and equity ratio instead of Mr. Coyne’s proposal.
Confusion between the rate of return on capital and cost of capital has led to excessive authorized returns.
Rate of return on capital and cost of capital are not the same: rate of return on capital is a financial performance metric, whereas cost of capital is the measure of economic cost described in the Hope case.
Q.WHAT IS THE OBJECTIVE IN SETTING A UTILITY’S AUTHORIZED RATE OF RETURN?
A.The authorized rate of return is the amount of money, expressed as a percentage of capital invested, that a utility is allowed to recover in customer rates to compensate debt and equity investors for assuming the risks of investing in the utility 
The Supreme Court provides the guiding ratemaking principles in its 1944 Hope Natural Gas case, in which it directs ratemakers to arrive at “just and reasonable rates” by a “balancing of the investor and the consumer interests.” Consumer interests are straightforward: they want to minimize costs and rates. Investors include holders of both the utility’s debt and equity. A utility’s debt investors expect to receive their contractual interest payments. Debtholders therefore have a “legitimate concern with the financial integrity of the company whose rates are being regulated,” i.e., the utility’s credit quality and anticipated ability to fulfill its obligations to debtholders. Equity investors expect a fair return, defined in Hope as “commensurate with returns on investments in other enterprises having corresponding risks.”
Q.How does the rate of return on capital differ from the cost of capital?
A.The rate of return on capital, often shortened to “rate of return,” is an accounting metric of financial performance, calculated by dividing the value returned to investors – e.g., interest, net income – by the amount of capital invested. The cost of capital is the return investors expect on their investment. It is referred to as a “cost” because it reflects what investors demand in return for assuming the risk of the investment and, therefore, what companies must pay for that investment. The rate of return on each form of capital, whether calculated retrospectively or estimated prospectively, may or may not equal its respective cost of capital.
Q.How does the authorized rate of return on capital differ from the rate of return and the cost of capital?
A.The authorized rate of return refers to a specific use of this accounting metric to determine a utility’s revenue requirement and customer rates. According to long-established ratemaking principles, which will be explained in more detail below, the authorized rate of return should be set equal to the cost of capital. Figure 2 summarizes the differences between these three sets of metrics for debt, equity, and the combined return.

Q.What principles do you use to determine your recommended authorized rate of return?
A.As the National Association of Regulatory Utility Commissioners (NARUC) has explained, “Fundamental financial concepts demonstrate that the fair rate of return to use in ratemaking for a utility is its cost of capital in order to achieve the proper balance between customers and investors.” Mr. Coyne has also acknowledged, in ROE testimony in other jurisdictions, “that the allowed rate of return should equal the cost of capital.” The objective in setting a utility’s authorized rate of return should, therefore, be to set the rate of return on each source of capital – debt and equity – as close as possible to the actual cost of each source of capital.
Q.Does the approach to calculating cost of equity differ from the approach to calculating cost of debt?
A.Yes. The cost of outstanding debt can be directly determined from its contractual interest rates. Similarly, the cost of debt expected to be issued can be accurately determined from known interest rate indexes for debt of comparable credit quality, such as Moody’s utility bond indexes, which, in turn, are based on interest rates directly observed in the market. In contrast, the cost of equity, both existing and to-be-issued, cannot be directly observed and must be estimated using various models.
Q.Why is the distinction between the cost of capital and rate of return important?
A.The cost of capital and rate of return (on capital) are entirely different concepts. The rate of return is a financial performance metric. The cost of capital is an economic concept. Nonetheless, they are frequently referred to interchangeably in utility regulatory proceedings, perhaps in part because finance professionals commonly refer to the cost of capital as the expected return (on capital).
The muddling of the difference between the cost of capital and the rate of return is not just of semantic concern, particularly for the cost of equity, which must be estimated using various models rather than directly observed (like the cost of debt).
This confusion between the cost of capital and the return on capital has infiltrated some of the models commonly used in utility cost of capital proceedings to estimate the cost of equity. These models’ apparent influence on regulatory decisions, though, does not make them correct or mean they provide a suitable basis for estimating the cost of equity.
Models that rely exclusively on historical or forecast utility rates of return on equity, without reference to utilities’ actual cost of equity, should be rejected outright. Mr. Coyne uses two such models, one relying exclusively on historical authorized ROEs, the other on forecast ROEs. These models incorporate no information about the actual cost of equity and are therefore inherently flawed and produce invalid results. Consistent with the fairness principle described by NARUC, and Mr. Coyne’s own statements, only models that estimate the cost of equity should be used to determine the authorized ROE.
Multiple, diverse sources of evidence demonstrate that utilities’ authorized ROEs far exceed their cost of equity.
Q.Do authorized ROEs reflect the actual cost of equity?
A.No. Substantial, robust evidence suggests that authorized ROEs for nearly all U.S. utilities exceed their cost of equity. Georgia Power’s ROE follows this national pattern. Below, I provide three diverse analyses that compellingly demonstrate, individually and collectively, the substantial gap between authorized ROEs and utilities’ actual cost of equity: expected equity return forecasts produced by investment professionals, utility market-to-book ratios, and the increasing spread between authorized ROEs and interest rates.
Investment firms’ expected return forecasts for the U.S. equity market as a whole – which is riskier, on average, than utilities – are consistently lower than utilities’ authorized ROEs.
Q.Are there other public sources for cost of equity estimates outside utility regulatory proceedings?
A.Utility cost of capital proceedings are not the only purpose for which expected returns on equity are estimated. Investment firms, such as JP Morgan, BlackRock, and T. Rowe Price, regularly publish capital market assumption reports (CMAs) – expected return forecasts for various asset classes. Figure 3 summarizes a survey of U.S. equity market return forecasts published by over thirty firms in 2021.
The CMA forecasts shown in Figure 3 are grouped by assumed investment horizon: less than ten years, ten years (the most common), and more than ten years. The average across the longer-term 10-year and more-than-ten-year horizons, 5.7%, is approximately 40% less than the average ROE authorized for regulated utilities throughout the United States in 2021, 9.5%. Not a single one of the forty-two expected return forecasts that I reviewed is as high as the average ROE yielded by any of Mr. Coyne’s models.
CMA equity market return forecasts are a relevant and useful benchmark for utility ROEs because U.S. utilities, including Georgia Power, are lower-risk – both historically and prospectively – than the market, and therefore investors have lower return expectations for utilities than for the market. The fact that authorized utility ROEs are so much higher than the expected returns on the overall market, which is higher risk, is a compelling indicator that authorized ROEs far exceed utility investors’ expected returns, i.e., utilities’ actual cost of equity.

Figure 3. U.S. equity market expected returns
Nominal, geometric


Market-to-book ratios reveal that utilities’ cost of equity is substantially lower than authorized ROEs.
Q.What is a market-to-book ratio?
A.“Market” value refers to the price one must pay to purchase a share of a company’s stock at any given time. “Book” value refers to the net equity invested in a company. In general, market value reflects the discounted value of a company’s future cash flows, while book value reflects the historical investment in the company. The market-to-book ratio (M/B) is a commonly used financial metric that indicates the amount of shareholder value added in excess of shareholders’ return expectations.
Q.How does a market-to-book ratio relate to returns?
A.It is a well understood financial principle that a market-to-book ratio greater than 1.0 indicates the company is expected to earn a return on its investment in excess of the actual cost of capital. A positive net present value (NPV), i.e., the value of a company minus its investments, is the signature indicator of a rate of return that exceeds the cost of capital. NPV is equal to investment multiplied by (M/B – 1.0), so M/B exceeding 1.0 indicates that NPV is positive.
Q.What do utility stock market-to-book ratios reveal about ROEs relative to utilities’ cost of equity?
A.It has long been recognized that utilities’ market-to-book (M/B) ratios provide insight into the relationship between authorized return and the true cost of capital. Legendary regulatory economist Alfred Kahn called attention to this phenomenon over fifty years ago in his 1970 classic, The Economics of Regulation: Principles and Institutions:
[T]he sharp appreciation in the prices of public utility stocks, to one and half and then two times their book value during this period, reflected … a growing recognition that the companies in question were in fact being permitted to earn considerably more than their cost of capital. … The source of the discrepancy between market and book value has been that commissions have been allowing r’s [returns on equity] in excess of k [market cost of equity]; if instead they had set r equal to k, or proceeded at some point to do so … the discrepancy between market and book value … would have disappeared, or would never have arisen.
Kahn was referring to the period of the late 1940s to 1965, but the observation that utilities trade above book value is equally valid today. As seen in Figure 4, the utility sector average M/B ratio has exceeded 1.0 for nearly thirty years and, except for a short period after the global financial crisis, has exceeded 1.5 since 1995.
Figure 4. Utility sector average market-to-book ratio
Year-end

The average of Mr. Coyne’s proxy group members’ M/B ratios, listed in Figure 4, is even higher, at 2.1. As Kahn observed, the utility sector trading at 1.5 to 2.0 times book value for decades clearly demonstrates that utilities have once again been “permitted to earn considerably more than their cost of capital.”

Another commonly referenced textbook on utility regulation, Kolbe, Read, and Hall’s The Cost of Capital: Estimating the Rate of Return for Public Utilities, recommends using a M/B ratio of 1.0 as a “guide for regulators” in setting the cost of capital:
The market-to-book ratio expresses the market value of the firm’s outstanding common stock to the book value of its equity. If the two are equal the expected return on the book will equal the expected return on the market value of the company, which in turn will equal the cost of capital for a company of that degree of risk.
Kahn and Kolbe, et al., drew their conclusions from the basic financial concept of net present value (NPV) discussed above. Because utilities trade at a premium to book value (i.e., invested capital), they have positive NPVs – prima facie evidence that they are earning more than their cost of capital.
Q.In practical, dollars-and-cents terms, what does it mean for a utility to have a market value that is higher than its book value?
A.In practical terms, this means that, for every dollar of equity a utility invests, shareholders receive back not just their investment plus a reasonable return, which would be the case when M/B = 1.0, but additional value equivalent to their equity investment multiplied by (M/B – 1.0). At current M/B ratios near 2.0, authorized ROEs effectively double the value of utilities’ equity investments, on top of returning their cost of equity. Such high returns are not necessary to attract capital and needlessly increase customer costs.
Q.ARE YOU ABLE TO CALCULATE GEORGIA POWER’S MARKET-TO-BOOK RATIO?
A.Georgia Power is not publicly traded, so its market value is not directly observable. Nonetheless, The Southern Company’s market-to-book ratio, averaging 2.82 over the month of September 2022, provides a reasonable estimate.
Q.Is Mr. Coyne aware of the relationship between the market-to-book ratio and returns?
AYes. Mr. Coyne has acknowledged that a market-to-book ratio greater than 1.0 necessarily implies that investors’ expected return, i.e., the cost of equity, is less than their authorized return. In a recent cost of capital proceeding in California, Mr. Coyne testified to the following principles:
“The terms ‘expected return to investors’ and ‘cost of capital’ are essentially the same.”
“The allowed rate of return should equal the cost of capital,” the NARUC fairness principle.
“A typical [investor] expectation is the utility will earn its allowed return [on book equity].” Mr. Coyne’s Expected Earnings Analysis is predicated on this assumption, and the Value Line reports from which the EEA draws its input assumptions explicitly state that the reports’ ROE forecasts are based on allowed returns.
“[I]nvestors can’t buy shares at book value … They [investors] can only buy them [shares of utility stocks] at market value.” 
“[M]arket-to-book values [of] electric and gas utilities all hover in around 2.0; although there is variation between companies. It’s been a long-term trend towards increasing the market-to-book ratio for utilities.”
It “would be highly unusual” to find “any company in [Mr. Coyne’s proxy group] sample that does not have a market value greater than the book value.”
“When market-to-book ratio is greater than 1.0 that means that investors are willing to pay more than book value for the same stream of cash flows.”
“[W]hen an investor pays more for the same stream of cash flows, that necessarily implies a lower return.”
The logical conclusions from Mr. Coyne’s testimony are that (1) investors’ expected returns on book equity are based on authorized ROEs (as can be seen in Figure 5, which shows the Value Line ROE data used in Mr. Coyne’s EEA; the averages, which range from 10.0% to 10.7%, are in line with the national average ROE authorized for electric utilities in 2020-22, 9.4%); (2) investors pay more than book value when they invest in utilities; (3) investors’ expected return on their investment, i.e., the cost of equity, must be less than the authorized ROE; and (4) according to NARUC’s fairness principle, under which the ROE should be set equal to the COE, historical, current, and forecast ROEs, because they systematically exceed the cost of equity, can provide no basis for determining the appropriate authorized ROE.
[Remainder of page intentionally left blank.]

Authorized ROEs and interest rates have diverged without a corresponding increase in utilities’ risk profile.
Q.Why do regulators approve authorized ROEs in excess of utilities’ actual cost of equity?
A.A mathematical model called the Pólya urn can provide insight into why regulators have continued to approve authorized ROEs in excess of utilities’ actual cost of capital. Historical return on equity decisions can be thought of as balls in an urn. To decide on a new case, the regulator draws a ball from the urn. The ball is then replaced, along with a new ball – representing the current ROE decision – with the same value. This process of sampling-with-replacement-plus-duplication has a self-reinforcing property sometimes called the rich-get-richer or Matthew effect.
Of course, this model is over-simplified because regulators look at other information besides past authorized ROEs. The basic model can be modified to include additional balls in the urn representing new information, such as the estimated current cost of equity. Nonetheless, as long as regulators look at, much less rely on, past ROEs, changes in authorized ROEs will lag changes in the current true cost of equity.
The basic utility regulatory model and risk profile have not changed significantly for decades, so the utility equity risk premium – the spread of the cost of capital over risk-free government interest rates – has remained stable. The Pólya urn model predicts that, in a market in which interest rates and, assuming a relatively stable utility equity risk premium, utilities’ cost of equity have been trending downward for decades, authorized ROEs will consistently exceed the actual cost of equity, and the spread will widen over time.
The data confirm the Pólya urn model’s prediction of such a widening spread between authorized ROEs and the actual cost of equity. Figure 6 shows the quarterly average authorized ROE for all U.S. utilities, the 30-year Treasury rate, and their difference. While interest rates have declined steadily since the mid-1980s, authorized ROEs have not kept pace. As a result, the ROE-Treasury spread has more than tripled, from approximately 2.3% in the early 1980s to 7.1% in the two years through September 2022. It can be estimated from the Pólya urn model described above that, even under conservative assumptions, regulators, on average, assign no more than a 25% weight to the current cost of equity and at least 75% to recent ROEs. No evidence suggests that utilities’ risk profile has systematically increased over this period, so setting ROEs higher and higher relative to utilities’ actual cost of equity unnecessarily raises rates and costs to customers.
Figure 6. Quarterly average authorized ROE and 30-year Treasury rate

Others have made similar observations about the growing divergence between authorized ROEs and utilities’ actual COEs. In a study published in 2019 exploring potential explanations, Carnegie Mellon researchers David Rode and Paul Fischbeck concluded:
It would appear that regulators are authorizing excessive returns on equity to utility investors and that these excess returns translate into tangible profits for utility firms.…In the end, we may observe simply that what regulators should do, what regulators say they’re doing, and what regulators actually do may be three very different things.
An analogy can be drawn between estimating the cost of equity and developing a nutrition recommendation. Looking at actual authorized ROEs to estimate the required ROE is akin to developing a calorie intake recommendation based on how much people actually eat, not what they need to maintain a healthy weight.
These nationwide trends have been even more pronounced for Georgia Power.
Q.Do these national trends apply to Georgia Power?
A.Yes. Authorized ROEs for Georgia Power have been set substantially higher than the national average authorized ROE for the last two decades, as seen in Figure 7, which shows the difference between GPC’s authorized ROEs and the corresponding quarterly national average electric utility authorized ROE in GPC’s last five cost of capital authorizations. On average, GPC’s authorized ROEs have been 0.8% higher than the national electric utility average.

[Remainder of page intentionally left blank.]

Figure 7. GPC and average U.S. electric utility authorized ROE since 2000


ROE and capital structure are interrelated and cannot be determined separately.
ROE and capital structure are interrelated through ROE’s impact on cash flow.
Q.Does the capital structure impact customer costs?
A.Yes. Capital structure refers to the share of a utility’s investment that is funded by debt and equity. Because equity generally has a higher cost than debt, assuming no change in authorized ROE, a higher equity ratio tends to increase customer costs.
Q.Does the capital structure impact a utility’s credit quality?
A.Yes. A primary determinant of a company’s credit quality – its anticipated ability to repay its debts – is the amount of debt outstanding relative to the total amount of capital, both debt and equity, invested in the company. In general, a higher equity ratio tends to improve a utility’s credit quality. Equity ratio is not the only determinant of credit quality, though. As will be explained in more detail below, credit quality is also determined by the amount of cash available to service the debt.
Q.Please explain the relationship between ROE and capital structure.
A.The testimony of Georgia Power’s credit expert, Mr. Steven Fetter, reveals that ROE and capital structure are inextricably linked.
Mr. Fetter describes the importance of the ratio of funds from operations (FFO) to debt in rating agencies’ assessments of utility credit quality. FFO and similar cash flow metrics measure the cash available to pay debt interest and principal. What he fails to clarify in his written testimony is that net income is a key component of FFO. Net income, in turn, is the product of rate base, equity ratio, and ROE. Consequently, ROE and equity ratio are key determinants of FFO. During cross-examination, Mr. Fetter acknowledged the relationship between capital structure, ROE, and credit quality:
Q.  [Ms. Whitfield]: On page 14, you talk about key quantitative 
measures. Specifically, you reference FFO to debt and debt to EBITDA as two core financial ratios that are considered by credit agencies.
A.  [Mr. Fetter]: Yes.
Q.  Do both of those ratios include inputs that are affected by 
both capital structure and the return on equity?
A.  Well, it certainly factors in the equity/debt split because in
one, the debt divides from the bottom and in the other, the debt is divided into by the EBITDA. As far as whether ROE gets factored into this, FFO – ROE ends up resulting in revenues and FFO is a certain measure and EBITDA is an earnings measure. And so ROE would impact those two items.
In the context of the regulatory objective of setting a capital structure that appropriately balances customer and investor interests, as ROE increases, the amount of debt in the capital structure can also increase while still maintaining the utility’s credit quality; similarly, as the ROE declines, the equity ratio would need to increase to maintain the same creditworthiness.
Curiously, Mr. Coyne’s capital structure analysis makes no reference whatsoever to any credit metrics. Instead, he bases his equity ratio recommendation, 56%, solely on a peer comparison.
Q.How should a utility’s capital structure be determined?
A.The appropriate capital structure can be determined more rigorously by using the analytical methods and metrics employed by credit rating agencies and as described by Mr. Fetter. The level of debt that can be accommodated in the capital structure will vary with ROE, because funds from operation (FFO), a key determinant of a utility’s creditworthiness, is based on net income, and net income is based on ROE, for any given credit rating and its corresponding FFO/debt ratio.
Figure 8 illustrates the relationships between equity ratio, ROE, and FFO/debt. The horizontal axis is the equity ratio; the vertical axis is FFO/debt. The light orange horizontal band represents the range of FFO/debt that corresponds to the utility’s desired credit rating. The dark orange arcing lines correspond to different levels of authorized ROE, with increasing line thickness representing increasing ROE. Holding the equity ratio constant, FFO/debt declines as the ROE is reduced (moving down from a thicker ROE line to a thinner line). But the decline in FFO/debt when ROE is reduced can be reversed by increasing the equity ratio (moving along the thinner ROE line up and to the right). Any number of combinations of ROE and equity ratio can meet the level of FFO/debt needed to maintain the utility’s credit rating. A higher ROE requires less equity to maintain the same FFO/debt and credit rating; a lower ROE can maintain the same FFO/debt and credit rating if it is paired with a higher equity ratio.
Figure 8. Illustrative relationships between equity ratio, ROE, and FFO/debt

Many observers see utilities’ healthy credit ratings and low cost of debt and conclude that the best way to reduce customer costs is to increase the amount of debt in the capital structure. As explained above, current authorized ROEs far exceed utilities’ actual cost of equity, so ample scope exists for the Commission to reduce Georgia Power’s ROE without adversely affecting its ability to raise equity. At typical utility credit ratings, savings from a lower ROE, after grossing up for taxes, generally more than make up for the incremental cost of any additional equity required in the capital structure. For example, based on analysis of data provided in Moody’s October 2021 Georgia Power credit opinion, every 1.0% reduction in ROE reduces total customer costs by approximately 2.0%, even after accounting for the approximately 2.0% increase in equity ratio needed to maintain Georgia Power’s current cash flow-to-debt ratio.
Total customer costs can be reduced by decreasing the ROE while increasing the equity ratio to maintain the utility’s creditworthiness (i.e., its cash flow-to-debt) because the trade-off is not one-for-one. Net income accounts for about 50% of GPC’s FFO; other items, such as depreciation and amortization, account for the rest. Consequently, a relatively large reduction in net income due to a sharp cut in ROE would reduce FFO by roughly half as much in percentage terms. In addition, ROE is grossed-up for taxes, which are not included in FFO, so the savings to customers from a lower ROE is amplified. Rather than “leverage up,” i.e., reduce the equity ratio and increase debt, at current ROEs to reduce rates, it is more cost-effective to use any spare credit capacity to reduce the utility’s ROE, or even to increase the equity ratio if necessary to maintain a target credit rating, than to increase debt.
Reducing ROE and increasing the equity ratio has the additional benefit of reducing debt-to-capitalization, another key metric used by rating agencies to assess credit quality.
Georgia Power’s capital structure proposal does not address the interaction between ROE and capital structure.
Q.Does Georgia Power’s capital structure proposal take into account the interaction between ROE and capital structure?
A.As Mr. Coyne acknowledged during cross examination, he developed his capital structure recommendation without incorporating the interrelationship between credit quality, ROE, and equity ratio. Neither Mr. Coyne nor Mr. Fetter provides any analysis or calculations demonstrating how FFO/debt interacts with ROE and how that interaction influences GPC’s proposed equity ratio. As a result, how much GPC’s proposed ROE and/or the equity ratio could be reduced to lower customer costs while still maintaining its desired investment-grade credit rating cannot be assessed from the testimony and data GPC has provided.
GPC’s analysis should have included a detailed analysis of the relationships between ROE, equity ratio, and creditworthiness and the impact of different combinations of equity ratio and ROE on customers, lenders, and shareholders so that the Commission could examine the ability of Georgia Power to obtain sufficient capital while minimizing customer costs. The Commission should require Georgia Power to provide a detailed analysis of the relationships between ROE, equity ratio, and creditworthiness so that the Commission possesses the facts it needs to come to fact-based conclusions about Georgia Power’s authorized capital structure.
Georgia Power’s ROE and equity ratio can be modified to reduce customer costs without adversely affecting GPC’s ability to raise capital.
Q.Can Georgia Power’s proposed ROE and/or equity ratio be modified in a way that reduces customer rates while maintaining their creditworthiness?
A.Evidence from other states suggests both the ROEs and equity ratios proposed by Georgia Power can be reduced while maintaining their creditworthiness. Figure 9 summarizes recent utility rate cases in which the authorized ROE was 9% or less. All of the equity ratios are below the 56% proposed by Georgia Power, yet all of the companies listed in Figure 9 below nonetheless also have comparable or better credit ratings, maintaining their ability to attract capital.

Mr. Coyne employs flawed models and assumptions that systematically produce upwardly biased ROE estimates for Georgia Power.
Mr. Coyne’s methodology relies, in part, on conceptually flawed models.
Q.How does Mr. Coyne estimate Georgia Power’s ROE?
A.Mr. Coyne employs four different models to estimate Georgia Power’s ROE: (1) constant-growth discounted cash flow model (CG DCF); (2) capital asset pricing model (CAPM); (3) risk premium analysis (RPA); and (4) expected earnings analysis (EEA). In the CG DCF, CAPM, and EEA, Mr. Coyne estimates ROEs for a number of proxy group companies and bases his recommended ROEs on the proxy group average.
Q.What is your overall assessment of Mr. Coyne’s approach?
A.Although two of Mr. Coyne’s models, the CG DCF and CAPM, are widely used by financial professionals and utility cost of capital experts to estimate the cost of equity, his implementations are deeply flawed. They rely on unrealistic, systematically upwardly biased assumptions that invalidate their results. I describe these deficiencies in more detail in Sections IV.C and IV.E below.
The other two models, RPA and EEA, while frequently used by utility cost of capital experts, are not commonly used elsewhere in finance outside utility regulatory proceedings, and both suffer a severe, invalidating conceptual flaw.
Q.What is the invalidating conceptual flaw in the RPA and EEA?
A.Both the RPA and EEA confuse the cost of equity and the return on equity, estimating the cost of equity from historic or forecast returns on equity. The RPA consists of a linear regression model of historical quarterly-average authorized ROEs against historical quarterly-average 30-year Treasury yield. Mr. Coyne’s EEA estimates the cost of equity using ROE forecasts provided by investment research firm Value Line. The primary drivers of Value Line’s ROE forecasts for the proxy group companies, in turn, are the companies’ most recent authorized ROEs.
Both the RPA and EEA are essentially models of past ROE decisions, not the actual cost of equity. By design, therefore, they repeat the historic overearnings arising from the excess ROEs authorized in the past. As explained in Section II.A above, the cost of equity and the return on equity are two entirely different concepts; there is no basis to assume that they will or should be equal. In fact, as explained in Section II.B above, authorized ROEs have diverged dramatically from utilities’ actual cost of equity, as reflected in the disparity between authorized ROEs and substantially lower forecast returns for the U.S. equity market as a whole, despite the latter’s higher risk; utility market-to-book ratios exceeding 1.0 for decades; and the growing divergence between average authorized ROEs and interest rates. Basing a utility’s authorized ROE on historically authorized ROEs without any reference to the actual cost of equity, as both the RPA and EEA do, merely perpetuates these errors.
A simple calculation illustrates the EEA’s conceptual flaw in equating ROE and COE. ROE is the ratio of earnings to the book value of equity. But investors cannot buy shares at book value; they must pay market value. The market value of the stocks Mr. Coyne chose to include in his peer group tend to trade at a significant premium over book value, currently an average of 2.1x. Mathematically, if investors pay more than book value for the same stream of earnings, their expected return, i.e., the cost of equity, must be less than the ROE calculated using the book value. For companies earning just their cost of equity, the expected return, i.e., the cost of equity, can be estimated by dividing earnings per share (EPS) by equity book value per share (BVPS). EPS can be expressed as the product of ROE and book value per share, so COE can similarly be expressed as ROE divided by the market-to-book ratio. COEs so calculated are dramatically lower than Mr. Coyne’s EEA estimates. For example, Value Line’s forecast ’25-‘27 ROE for Alliant Energy Corporation is 11.0%, and as of September 30, 2022, Alliant’s market-to-book ratio was 2.17, so investors’ expected return would be only approximately 11.0/2.17 = 5.07%. As Kahn explained, and Mr. Coyne has acknowledged (see Section II.B.2 above), M/B ratios greater than 1.0 imply COE is less than ROE. It is simply mathematically impossible for COE to equal ROE when M/B is not equal to 1.0, yet Mr. Coyne’s RPA and EEA are nonetheless premised on this mathematical impossibility.
FERC has also recognized the flaws in the RPA and EEA. In Opinion No. 569 (November 2019), FERC rejected the use of both the RPA and EEA to estimate the cost of equity. With regard to the EEA, FERC explained “that relying on the Expected Earnings model would not satisfy the requirements of Hope”:
In particular, we find that the record does not support departing from our traditional use of market-based approaches to determine base ROE. Under the market-based approach, the Commission sets a utility’s ROE to equal the estimated return that investors would require in order to purchase stock in the utility at its current market price. In Hope, the Supreme Court explained that “the return to the equity owner should be commensurate with returns on investments in other enterprises having corresponding risks.” In order to determine this, we must analyze the returns that are earned on “investments in other enterprises having corresponding risks,” but investors cannot invest in an enterprise at book value and must instead pay the prevailing market price for an enterprise’s equity. As a result, the expected return on a utility’s book value does not reflect “returns on investments in other enterprises” because book value does not reflect the value of any investment that is available to an investor in the market, outside of the unlikely situation in which market value and book value are exactly equal. Accordingly, we find that relying on the Expected Earnings model would not satisfy the requirements of Hope.
FERC initially rejected the RPA for similar reasons:
[T]he Risk Premium model is likely to provide a less accurate current cost of equity estimate than the DCF model or CAPM because it relies on previous ROE determinations, whose resulting ROE may not necessarily be directly determined by a market-based method, whereas the DCF and CAPM methods apply a market-based method to primary data. For example, previous ROE determinations may not involve an explicit determination as to whether an ROE is just and reasonable, but instead focused on whether to allow an ROE incentive adder or were approving a preexisting RTO-wide ROE for a new RTO member. Similarly, many previous ROE determinations used in the Risk Premium model were the product of rate case settlements. Such settlements often involve compromises on a variety of issues present in a rate case, of which the appropriate ROE is only one. Consequently, such settlements could include ROEs that are not representative of the market cost of equity because the ROEs were negotiated above or below that market cost of equity in order to form an overall settlement package, together with negotiated outcomes on other issues, that were acceptable to the parties.
Although FERC subsequently reinstated the RPA in Opinion No. 569-A (May 2020), the D.C. Circuit Court of Appeals found that FERC’s reinstatement of the RPA was arbitrary and capricious and has vacated Opinion 569-A.
In the same California cost of capital proceeding referenced in Section II.B.2 above, Mr. Coyne acknowledged that his Risk Premium Analysis and Expected Earnings Analysis are not based on utilities’ cost of equity:
[Remainder of page intentionally left blank.]
Q.Isn’t it true that two of your models, the risk premium analysis, RPA, and the expected earnings analysis, EEA, are not based on the utilities’ actual cost of equity?
A.Yeah.
Q.The RPA is based on historical authorized ROEs; correct?
A.It’s based on an historic relationship between allowed return by regulatory commissions and Treasury yields.
Q.The EEA is based on forecast ROEs; correct?
A.Yes. That’s based on a forecast of ROEs.
Despite his acknowledgement that ROE systematically exceeds COE, Mr. Coyne’s RPA and EEA nonetheless estimate COE based on historical and forecast ROEs – in direct contradiction to the NARUC fairness principle that he has endorsed. For these reasons, the results of Mr. Coyne’s EEA and RPA models should be rejected outright.
Mr. Coyne fails to account for differences in equity ratios among the proxy group members.
Q.How does the equity ratio affect the cost of equity?
A.All else equal, a lower equity ratio tends to raise the cost of equity. This can be understood intuitively. The cash generated by a business is pledged to holders of its debt and equity, with debtholders having first priority. As the equity ratio declines, a smaller share of the cash goes to equity owners. To the extent there is uncertainty in the cash generated, it is amplified by a lower equity ratio, increasing the riskiness of those cash flows. This increased risk is reflected in a higher cost of equity.
The companies in Mr. Coyne’s proxy group have different equity ratios, and their average, 53%, is different from the 56% he proposes for Georgia Power, so their costs of equity cannot be used to estimate Georgia Power’s under his proposed equity ratio. In addition, Mr. Coyne compares the equity ratios on a book basis; this is incorrect. When determining the cost of capital, the market value of equity should be used.
I will revisit adjusting the COE model results for differences in equity ratios among the proxy group members, and between the proxy group average and Georgia Power, in Section V.A below.
Mr. Coyne’s DCF model uses upwardly biased dividend yield calculations and unrealistically extrapolates analyst earnings growth forecasts into perpetuity, producing economically impossible results.
Q.What is your assessment of Mr. Coyne’s DCF model?
A.Mr. Coyne uses the constant-growth version of the DCF model (CG DCF) in his analysis, with a growth rate equal to equity analysts’ consensus 3-to-5-year EPS growth rate estimates. The key shortcoming in his implementation of the CG DCF is the assumption that dividends can grow at analysts’ estimated EPS growth rates into perpetuity. This economically impossible assumption upwardly biases his results dramatically.
Q.Please describe the constant-growth DCF model.
A.The constant-growth DCF is based on the well-known and widely used mathematical formula for the value of a growing perpetuity stream of cash flows. It assumes a single, constant rate of cash flow growth. In the constant-growth DCF model used by Mr. Coyne and many other modelers, the cash flows are expected dividends, and the perpetuity value formula can be expressed as:

where  refers to the current market value (stock price), , the current dividend (typically four times the most recent quarterly payment), , the forecast perpetuity growth rate, and , the cost of equity. Rearranging terms, the cost of equity can be expressed as a function of the dividend yield,  (), and growth rate:

In some implementations of the CG DCF, the first-year dividend yield is calculated by multiplying the current yield by , instead of , to account for the quarterly, not annual, payment of dividends. Mr. Coyne uses this approach. Typically, the cost of equity is estimated for each member of the proxy group, with the mean or median reflecting the cost of equity for the target company. Mr. Coyne uses the mean.
The general DCF model, which can allow for varying growth rates over time, is a particularly apt representation of stock returns because its assumptions realistically reflect several key features of share prices and expected returns. First, the DCF model’s perpetual cash flow stream assumption mirrors equity’s claim on a firm’s cash flows into perpetuity. Second, the assumption of steady growth in dividends over time reasonably reflects their much greater stability relative to other potential measures of profitability, like earnings or cash flow. Third, the resulting single discount rate into perpetuity is consistent with the no-arbitrage principle of finance. If investors expected higher (lower) returns in the future, they would impute that into the price today and bid up (down) the price accordingly, such that near-term and long-term returns roughly equilibrate.
But the constant growth version of the DCF used by Mr. Coyne is not well-suited for estimating the cost of equity for a utility or any other stock, for two reasons. First, it is not realistic to assume that a utility will maintain its current 3-to-5-year growth rate into perpetuity. At any given time, the 3-to-5-year growth rate will deviate from its long-term trend due to any number of factors, such as weather; economic conditions; new capital projects; regulatory, tax, and other policy changes; and unforeseen events like the Covid-19 pandemic. Second, the results of the CG DCF are particularly sensitive to the perpetuity growth rate assumption. The inaccuracy introduced by assuming a relatively short-term growth rate will be sustained forever invalidates the results of Mr. Coyne’s CG DCF. Mr. Coyne’s use of analyst estimates, a source widely known to be upwardly biased, for his growth rate assumption further invalidates his results.
Mr. Coyne’s dividend yield calculations use unnecessarily long trailing price histories, introducing upward bias to his estimates.
Q.How does Mr. Coyne calculate the current dividend yield?
A.To calculate the dividend yield, Mr. Coyne divides four times the most recent quarterly dividend by the trailing average share price over 30, 90, and 180 trading days.
While it is advisable to use a multi-day average of the share price to reduce the effect of any day-to-day price fluctuations that are not reflective of investors’ long-term expectations, all three of Mr. Coyne’s averages are unnecessarily long. Because share prices have a general tendency to trend upward over time, the longer the backward-looking averaging period, the lower the share price will tend to be, introducing upward bias into the dividend yield. Averaging the most recent month of data, approximately 21 trading days, better balances the competing objectives of mitigating the potential short-term volatility cited by Mr. Coyne and reflecting current investor expectations. 
Mr. Coyne’s perpetuity growth rate is based on analysts’ 3-to-5-year growth rate forecasts, producing economically impossible results.
Q.How does Mr. Coyne estimate each peer utility’s perpetuity growth rate?
A.While estimating the current dividend yield is fairly straightforward (although, as just explained, the potential for bias exists even there), estimating the perpetuity growth rate is more subjective. Mr. Coyne uses analysts’ consensus 3-to-5-year estimated earnings-per-share (EPS) growth rate provided by Value Line, Thomson First Call (as reported by Yahoo! Finance), and Zacks.
The DCF is a model of dividends, not earnings, so it would be preferable to use explicit dividend-per-share (DPS) forecasts. While some analysts, such as Value Line, provide DPS forecasts, they are less common, so EPS forecast are typically used instead. 
Q.Is it reasonable to assume analysts’ consensus growth rates into perpetuity in a DCF model?
A.No. There are several problems with using analysts’ estimates for the perpetuity growth rate. A wealth of academic research over decades has found that analyst forecasts tend to be optimistic. Several other observations and analyses demonstrate the unreasonableness of using analysts’ 3-to-5-year EPS growth rate estimates for the perpetuity dividend growth rate in the constant-growth DCF model.
Q.What is your first observation or analysis that demonstrates the unreasonableness of using analysts’ 3-to-5-year EPS growth rate estimates for the perpetuity dividend growth rate in the CG DCF?
A.It is economically impossible for analyst earnings growth forecasts to be sustained even for one decade, much less into perpetuity. Figure 10 compares the forecast aggregate earnings of the U.S. publicly-traded companies for which analysts provide EPS growth forecasts to forecast U.S. GDP. Currently, these companies’ combined earnings are equal to roughly 8% of U.S. GDP. Yet if analysts’ growth projections were correct, they would exceed total U.S. GDP in just six years.
Figure 10. U.S. stock market forecast earnings vs. forecast GDP

The Research Foundation of CFA Institute has made a similar critique of projecting analysts’ estimates beyond their forecast horizon:
Consensus long-term earnings growth estimates routinely exceed sustainable GDP growth. The current consensus growth rate for earnings on the S&P 500, according to the Zacks Investment Research survey, is 10 percent, which, if we assume a consensus inflation expectation of 2-3 percent, corresponds to 7-8 percent real growth. Real earnings growth of 8 percent is six times the real earnings growth of the past century, however, and three times the consensus long-term GDP growth rate. This growth is not possible.
Mr. Coyne himself has acknowledged the potential unreasonableness of assuming analyst growth rates into perpetuity in his CG DCF model, testifying in a recent rate case, “the Multi-Stage DCF can be used when there is concern that short-term growth rates may not be sustainable over the longer-term.”
Q.WHAT IS YOUR SECOND OBSERVATION OR ANALYSIS THAT DEMONSTRATES THE UNREASONABLENESS OF USING ANALYSTS’ 3-TO-5-YEAR EPS GROWTH RATE ESTIMATES FOR THE PERPETUITY DIVIDEND GROWTH RATE IN THE CG DCF?
A.Mr. Coyne is correct that “[o]ver the long term … dividend growth can only be sustained by earnings growth,” and EPS and DPS do tend to have similar growth rates over extended, multi-decade periods of time. Nonetheless, EPS growth rates are poor proxies for DPS growth over the 3-to-5-year horizon of analysts’ EPS forecasts. This is observed in both forecast and historical data.
Few analysts provide both DPS and EPS projections, but Value Line, one source of Mr. Coyne’s growth estimates, does provide both EPS and DPS growth forecasts, and its EPS and DPS projections vary considerably. Figure 11 compares Value Line’s forecast EPS and DPS growth rates for the members of Mr. Coyne’s proxy group. On average, the DPS growth rate is lower, 5.5% vs 6.1% for EPS; EPS overestimates expected dividend growth, introducing upward bias into Mr. Coyne’s DCF results. More importantly, as shown in Figure 11, a cross-plot of Value Line’s EPS and DPS growth rate estimates, the correlation between the two sets of numbers is low (R2 coefficient of 0.23).

[Remainder of page intentionally left blank.]

Figure 11. Value Line forecast EPS and DPS forecast growth rates

Historically, the variation between EPS and DPS is even greater. Figure 12 compares historical 5-year EPS and DPS growth rates for the S&P 500 from January 1988 through March 2022. Historically, average EPS growth has also been greater than DPS, 7.0% vs. 5.7%, and the correlation between EPS and DPS is even lower than for the forecast growth – R2 coefficient of just 0.05.

[Remainder of page intentionally left blank.]

Figure 12. Historical S&P 500 EPS and DPS 5-year growth rates

In both the historical and projected data, the average growth rate for EPS is higher than for DPS, seemingly contradicting Mr. Coyne’s assertion that “earnings per share [and] dividends per share … grow at the same constant rate.” But EPS and DPS do, in fact, grow at roughly the same compound rate over the long term. Nonetheless, analysts’ 3-to-5-year forecast horizons are not long-term, and simply because earnings are more volatile than dividends, the figure reported for EPS growth over analysts’ 3-to-5-year forecast period will tend to be higher than DPS, as both the historical growth rates in Figure 12 and the forecast growth rates in Figure 11 demonstrate.
This is due to the mathematical relationship between compound, or geometric, and arithmetic average growth rates. An illustrative example demonstrates the principle. Suppose both EPS and DPS both grow at a compound average growth rate (CAGR) of 5% over 25 years, but they take different paths to get there. DPS grows at the same 5% every year, or 27.6% over each 5-year period. Annual EPS growth varies between -5% and +15% over each 5-year period, say 15% in the first 5 years, followed by -5%, 0%, 6%, and 10% over the next four 5-year periods. The average of the DPS growth rates over each of the 5-year periods is 5.0%, but the average of the EPS growth rates is 5.2%. The effect is relatively small, but it is another source of upward bias introduced by using EPS growth rates as a proxy for DPS growth.
For the two reasons observed in both forecast and historical data – overestimation on average and low correlation – EPS is a poor proxy for DPS, and it is inappropriate to assume that analysts’ relatively short-term EPS growth estimates reasonably reflect investors’ DPS growth expectations into perpetuity.
Q.WHAT IS YOUR THIRD OBSERVATION OR ANALYSIS THAT DEMONSTRATES THE UNREASONABLENESS OF USING ANALYSTS’ 3-TO-5-YEAR EPS GROWTH RATE ESTIMATES FOR THE PERPETUITY DIVIDEND GROWTH RATE IN THE CG DCF?
A.EPS growth rate horizons are likely not compatible with the CG DCF’s forecast horizon. The starting time periods of analysts’ estimates are not specified with precision. S&P explains of its estimates:
Long Term Growth Rate (LTG) is a compound annual growth rate based on current and projected EPS values provided directly by the analysts. … Most analysts define LTG as an estimated average rate of earnings growth for the next 3-5 years. The exact time frame differs from broker to broker. Since the analysts providing LTG may differ from the analysts providing fiscal year estimates and the variation in time periods of 3-5 years, it is not possible to reconcile LTG with fiscal year estimates.
The starting points for Yahoo! Finance’s estimates are similarly unknown:
[A]s most analysts do not provide the basis of the calculation of their growth rates, the estimates collected are assumed to include a combination of past and future years with at least one future period included, and are calculated on a compounded annual growth rate (CAGR) basis.
Value Line does specify the starting point and forecast horizon of its estimates. Nonetheless, Value Line’s growth rate forecast horizons are virtually certain not to be consistent with the dividend yield used in the CG DCF, i.e., the end of the last trading day of the share price averaging period. The starting period for the Value Line growth estimates used by Mr. Coyne, for example, is ’19-’21, the midpoint of which, June 2020, is almost three years before the first dividend payment in his DCF model.
EPS can vary significantly from one year to the next, typically much more than the annual variation in DPS. Without knowing the forecast period, it is not possible to determine whether the estimate reflects the growth rate over the three to five years, much less the rate into perpetuity, from the starting point of the CG DCF. Following a year of poor performance, for example, expected growth would be elevated, potentially significantly above what could be sustained long-term.
The incompatibility of the growth estimates can be clearly observed in the data itself. For example, Value Line’s growth rate for Edison International is reported as “not meaningful” because the company reported a loss in 2018, the first year of its growth forecast period. Yahoo! Finance’s and Zacks’s growth rates are both positive, presumably because the starting point of their growth rate forecasts are years in which Edison International reported a profit.
Q.WHAT IS YOUR FOURTH OBSERVATION OR ANALYSIS THAT DEMONSTRATES THE UNREASONABLENESS OF USING ANALYSTS’ 3-TO-5-YEAR EPS GROWTH RATE ESTIMATES FOR THE PERPETUITY DIVIDEND GROWTH RATE IN THE CG DCF?
A.The expected returns produced by a CG DCF model assuming DPS grows into perpetuity at analysts’ 3-to-5-year EPS growth rates are inconsistent with analysts’ own expected return forecasts.
In addition to their EPS and DPS growth rates, Value Line publishes a variety of other forecasts, including for share prices. These forecasts can be used to estimate Value Line’s own expected return for each company, which can be compared to the CG DCF results using Value Line’s dividend yield and EPS growth rate forecasts in Mr. Coyne’s model. Figure 13 compares the results of the two models. The horizontal axis is the COE estimated using Mr. Coyne CG DCF and Value Line’s yield and EPS growth rate assumptions; the vertical axis is COE implied by Value Line’s own dividend and price forecasts.
On average, the CG DCF COE estimates (9.6%) are significantly higher than the COE implied by Value Line’s price and dividend forecast (8.1%). More importantly, there is virtually no correlation between the two sets of model results (R2 = 0.0). A CG DCF COE based on Value Line’s dividend yield and EPS growth bears almost no resemblance to the COE implied in its own dividend and price forecast. The analysts that provide 3-to-5-year EPS growth forecasts clearly do not assume those rates apply to dividends or will be sustained into perpetuity, as Mr. Coyne does. For Mr. Coyne’s proxy group, Value Line assumes DPS will grow more slowly, on average, than EPS beyond its forecast horizon.
Figure 13. COE based on Value Line CG DCF and price and DPS forecast IRR

Q.WHAT IS YOUR FIFTH OBSERVATION OR ANALYSIS THAT DEMONSTRATES THE UNREASONABLENESS OF USING ANALYSTS’ 3-TO-5-YEAR EPS GROWTH RATE ESTIMATES FOR THE PERPETUITY DIVIDEND GROWTH RATE IN THE CG DCF?
A.Analyst earnings (and, by assumption, dividend) growth forecasts tend to be higher than the companies’ long-term historical results, additional evidence that analysts’ 3-to-5-year growth forecasts are not sustainable into perpetuity and are therefore unreasonable assumptions in a CG DCF model.
Figure 14 compares the proxy group average growth forecasts to their historical 30-year (1992-2022) DPS compound annual growth rates (CAGR). On average, forecast rates are approximately 4% higher, in both nominal (as reported) and real (inflation-adjusted) terms, than the historical average. The ~4% difference between historical and forecast growth highlights the unreasonableness of assuming analysts’ estimates into perpetuity.
Figure 14. Proxy group average historical and Coyne forecast dividend per share 
growth
Percent

Q.GIVEN THE NUMEROUS SHORTCOMINGS OF ANALYSTS’ FORECASTS, SHOULD THEY BE USED AT ALL IN DCF MODELS?
A.Discounted cash flow models can be a robust approach to estimating expected returns and are widely used throughout finance. The key shortcoming of the constant-growth version of the DCF model – assuming a relatively short-term growth rate into perpetuity – can be easily remedied by assuming that analysts’ estimated growth rates apply only for a limited period, after which they converge toward a market- or sector-average terminal growth rate in a multi-stage DCF model (MS DCF), which I describe in more detail in IV.D below. Despite the various deficiencies in analysts’ estimates even in the short-term, they are viewed as the best available estimates of near-term investor expectations. That said, relatively little weight should be placed on them in estimating the cost of equity, and the MS DCF model can weight them more appropriately. 
Mr. Coyne’s flawed DCF results should be disregarded.
Q.WHAT IS YOUR CONCLUSION REGARDING MR. COYNE’S DCF RESULTS?
A.Mr. Coyne’s DCF model results should be disregarded completely. While, in principle, a constant-growth DCF model could be used to estimate the cost of equity, Mr. Coyne’s implementation is deeply flawed. Its dividend yield calculations use excessively long historical price data series, introducing upward bias into the results. More critically, Mr. Coyne’s DCF unrealistically assumes analysts’ EPS growth estimates are valid for forecasting dividends into perpetuity. Several analyses demonstrate the invalidity of this assumption: the economic impossibility of sustaining EPS growth forecasts even for one decade, much less into perpetuity; the low correlation between analysts’ EPS and DPS growth forecasts; the unknown starting period for analyst growth forecasts and therefore likely inconsistency with the DCF model’s starting period; the inconsistency between the CG DCF results and analysts’ own implied expected return estimates; and the wide disparity between analyst forecasts and utilities’ long-term historical DPS growth rates.
The Multi-Stage DCF should be used instead of the CG DCF because it allows more realistic cash flow projections, yielding more accurate results.
The multi-stage DCF model enhances the CG DCF by allowing different dividend growth rates over time.
Q.What is the multi-stage DCF model?
A.The multi-stage DCF model (MS DCF) enhances the CG DCF by allowing different dividend growth rates over time. As we saw previously, analysts’ estimated 3-to-5-year growth rates are too high to be sustained in perpetuity, and may be biased. But analyst estimates should not be ignored completely. Analysts’ estimated 3-to-5 year growth rates provide useful information about the relative expected growth across companies. Over the long term though, it is reasonable to assume investors expect growth rates, in real terms, to revert to their long-term historical trends. The MS DCF explicitly models different growth rates over time.
The MS DCF can incorporate any number of stages. For equity valuation, a three-stage model is commonly used, in which the initial stage uses analysts’ estimates over their 3-to-5-year forecast horizon, and the terminal stage uses the long-term real historical growth rate plus current long-term inflation expectations. In between lies a transition phase, typically 5 to 15 years, in which the growth rate is the simple average of the initial and terminal rates. The MS DCF model can be expressed as:

where  is the current dividend yield; , , and  are the initial, transition, and terminal growth rates, respectively (where );  and  are the initial and transition stage durations; and  is the cost of equity such that the equation is true. Substantial precedent exists for the MS DCF model, in both its two- and three-stage forms, in both corporate finance and regulatory contexts.
In my implementation of the MS DCF, I assume an initial growth stage of three years – the low end of analysts’ EPS growth rate forecast horizon, to mitigate the effect of their upward bias – and a 10-year transition. To account for the quarterly distribution of dividends, I convert the reported rates to quarterly and multiply the number of periods in the initial and transition phases by 4. The dividend yield is the most recent quarterly dividend divided by the average price over December 2021.
The MS DCF’s initial growth rate can be estimated from analysts’ EPS growth forecasts.
Q.HOW DO YOU ESTIMATE THE INITIAL GROWTH RATE FOR THE MS DCF?
A.I use an average of analysts’ EPS growth forecasts from S&P Global Market Intelligence (S&P GMI), Yahoo! Finance, and Zacks.
The MS DCF’s terminal growth rate can be estimated from expected inflation, based on utilities’ long-term historical dividend growth.
Q.HOW DO YOU ESTIMATE THE TERMINAL GROWTH RATE FOR THE MS DCF?
A.The terminal growth rate is intended to reflect a sector-wide dividend growth rate toward which all stocks in the peer group are expected to converge over the long term. Figure 15 shows real utility-sector dividend, price, and book value per share from 1927 through 2020. While there have been periods of growth and decline, the long-term trend for both has been in line with inflation for over 90 years. Based on this long-term history, the terminal growth rate in the MS DCF for the GPC proxy group is assumed to be equal to long-term inflation.
Figure 15. Utility sector real dividend and book value per share, 1927-2021
1927=1.0

For the market as a whole, long-term real DPS growth has tracked GDP per capita, about 1.9% per year. At any given time, some sectors grow faster, some slower. The technology and healthcare industries, for example, have sustained DPS growth rates higher than the market average for decades. Utilities are a mature industry, though, and end-use demand for electricity, gas, and water has grown more slowly than GDP for decades, so it is not unreasonable for utility companies’ per-share dividend growth to lag the market as whole. The long-term track record of essentially zero real dividend growth further highlights the unreasonableness of Mr. Coyne’s assumption that analyst growth forecasts can be sustained into perpetuity.
Q.HOW DO YOU ESTIMATE EXPECTED LONG-TERM INFLATION?
A.For expected long-term inflation, I use Treasury-TIPS spreads. TIPS are Treasury Inflation-Protected Securities, which provide investors a return equivalent to inflation plus the quoted TIPS yield. The difference in yield between Treasurys and TIPS of equal maturity is a current measure of the market’s forward-looking inflation expectation over the life of the bonds.
The MS DCF uses inflation for the terminal, not initial or transition, growth rate, so inflation into perpetuity is estimated at the end of the transition phase, not from today. I use the expected inflation, , rate over the period from 20 to 30 years from now, as implied by the difference in the 30-year and 20-year Treasury-TIPS spreads:

Using average Treasury yields for the month of September 2022, the long-term inflation estimate is 1.45%.
The MS DCF produces COE estimates substantially lower than Mr. Coyne’s CG DCF.
Q.WHAT ARE YOUR MS DCF COE RESULTS?
A.Figure 16 summarizes the MS DCF results for the GPC proxy group. The average COE for the GPC proxy group is 6.0% – substantially lower than Mr. Coyne’s corresponding CG DCF average results of 8%-10%.
