Q.DO YOU USE THE MS DCF ELSEWHERE IN YOUR ANALYSIS?
A.Yes. I use it as one of two methods to estimate the market risk premium for the CAPM.
Mr. Coyne makes unrealistic, upwardly biased assumptions for all three inputs into his CAPM.
Q.WHAT IS THE CAPITAL ASSET PRICING MODEL?
A.Mr. Coyne’s analysis incorporates another well-known COE model, the capital asset pricing model (CAPM). It estimates the cost of equity, , from the formula:

where  is the risk-free rate (typically a long-term U.S. Treasury),  is the expected return on the market, and  is a measure of risk of the company in question relative to the market. The market risk premium (MRP), the difference between the market return and the risk-free rate, , reflects the additional return investors require as compensation for taking on equity market risk. The CAPM is a simple model of the fundamental financial risk-reward trade-off: investors demand higher returns as risk increases.
The source of Mr. Coyne’s risk-free rate assumption has a long and widely known track record of upwardly biased interest rate forecasts.
Q.WHAT RISK-FREE RATE DOES MR. COYNE USE IN HIS CAPM?
A.Mr. Coyne uses the 30-year Treasury (T30). Because the models estimate the expected return on equity, which is a claim on cash flows into perpetuity, the longest-term rate available should be used. The 30-year Treasury is the longest-term risk-free rate, so the T30 is an appropriate term. Mr. Coyne calculates his CAPM COE estimates using both recent historical average and forecast Treasury rates.
Q.DO YOU HAVE ANY CONCERNS WITH MR. COYNE’S RISK-FREE RATE ESTIMATES?
A.Yes, I have two concerns. First, as with the average price calculation for the dividend yield in his DCF model, Mr. Coyne’s historical T30 averaging period, 30 days, is too long. Curiously, Mr. Coyne provides no explanation for the use of different averaging periods in the two different models. One month of trading data, typically 20-22 days, is sufficient. Nonetheless, the difference in trailing averaging periods for interest rates generally does not introduce any bias in the results because, unlike stock prices, which tend to exhibit an upward trend over time, daily movements interest rates are random. 
Q.WHAT IS YOUR SECOND CONCERN WITH MR. COYNE’S RISK-FREE RATE ESTIMATES?
A.My second concern involves his use of a forecast rate. Using a forecast rate creates inconsistencies with the time horizon of the DCF, which is estimated as of today (or, more precisely, as of the end of the trailing price averaging period). The mathematical formula for the present value of a periodic time series upon which the DCF is based discounts the stream of future cash flows to a “time zero” one period before the first payment. The resulting discount rate is as of that time zero. The first payment in the DCF model is typically assumed to occur one time step from today; therefore, the rate determined by the DCF model is as of today. Using an interest rate expected at some future date in the CAPM produces a COE as of that future date, not today, and that COE is not directly comparable to the DCF’s COE.
Ignoring the consistency concern, even if we did want to use a forecast rate, in general, commonly available interest rate forecasts are no better predictors of future interest rates than the current market rate. Most importantly, though, I am particularly concerned about Mr. Coyne’s chosen source for his interest rate forecast, Blue Chip Financial Forecasts (BCFF). BCFF’s 30-year Treasury forecast has an exceptionally poor track record.
Q.HOW ACCURATE HAVE THE BLUE CHIP FINANCIAL FORECASTS 30-YEAR TREASURY FORECASTS BEEN IN THE PAST/HISTORICALLY?
A.BCFF has a multi-decade track record of producing systematically upwardly-biased forecasts, and the errors have only increased over time. Figure 17 compares the BCFF forecasts used by Mr. Coyne – the average of the next five quarters and years one to five – to their corresponding future average realized rates, going back to BCFF’s first long-range forecast in December 1996. The chart shows that BCFF’s 30-year Treasury forecasts have consistently overestimated future rates, and the forecast errors have tended to increase over time. 
Figure 17. BCFF 30-year Treasury forecast vs. average realized rate


Q.HOW DOES MR. COYNE JUSTIFY HIS USE OF FORECASTS WITH SUCH A POOR TRACK RECORD?
A.Mr. Coyne cites a Massachusetts regulatory decision to argue that “Blue Chip Financial Forecasts is widely relied on by investors and provides a useful proxy for investor expectations for the risk-free rate.” BCFF may be relied upon by some investors, but no basis exists for Mr. Coyne’s claim that BCFF forecasts represent a reasonable proxy for investor expectations. BCFF has no more than a hundred thousand subscribers, less than 0.1% of the hundreds of millions of investors who are exposed to Treasury rates through direct investments or as a benchmark for other investments. Although utility cost of capital experts routinely argue that these forecasts represent the “market’s view,” 0.1% in no way represents the market. The hundreds of millions of market participants respond to all kinds of information, and the small slice of the market used by Mr. Coyne, via BCFF, does not represent an adequate or reasonably proxy.
Mr. Coyne’s argument also implicitly assumes that investors rely only on BCFF forecasts, to the exclusion of all other ways that investors might develop their expectations; that they rely on BCFF’s forecasts as-is, with no adjustment for their historical inaccuracy; and that investors’ only use of the forecasts is for investment decisions. None of Mr. Coyne’s assumptions is true. The consistent errors in BCFF forecasts are well-known; the Congressional Budget Office has issued public reports on BCFF’s interest rate forecasting errors for nearly twenty years. Many investors undoubtedly take BCFF’s forecasts “with a grain of salt” and inform their decisions with other forecasts and information. Finally, BCFF reports include dozens of other forecasts, as well as commentary and analysis. Investors might “rely” on the reports for that other content, not BCFF’s interest rate forecasts, per se.
Q.SUPPOSE WE IGNORED THE CONCERN YOU EXPRESSED ABOUT CONSISTENCY WITH THE DCF AND WANTED A FORECAST RISK-FREE INTEREST RATE ASSUMPTION FOR THE CAPM. WHAT SHOULD WE USE?
A.It turns out that current interest rates generally provide an unbiased forecast of future rates. Figure 18 is a cross-plot of the 20-year Treasury rate one year ahead against the current rate. Current interest rates account for approximately 91% of the variation in future interest rates; for rates two years in the future, current interest rates explain 83% of the variation in future interest rates, and for rates in three years, current interest rates explain 79%. Regardless of the forecast horizon, the current rate is unbiased – exhibiting no tendency to be systematically too high or too low. Similar predictive validity is obtained for 30-year Treasury and corporate bonds.
Figure 18. Twenty-year Treasury rate, one year in the future vs. current
January 1925-September 2022

More intuitively, markets are forward-looking. If investors expect interest rates to rise, their expectations will be incorporated into current yields. Consider the alternative. Suppose an investor expects the yield on the 30-year Treasury to rise from its current ~3% to 4% over the next six months. There is an inverse relationship between a bond’s value and a bond’s yield; when the yield rises, the value falls, and vice versa. An investor who expects bond yields to rise would not buy a bond today, because to do so would be to invest expecting a loss; better not to buy the bond at all. But market participants do buy at the current 3%, implying that the market overall does not expect rates to rise in the future. Current yields are the best predictor of future yields, especially for longer-term bonds.
The predictive validity of current rates has been acknowledged among utility cost of capital experts. Reviewing the academic research, Roger Morin, author of the frequently cited practitioner text, New Regulatory Finance, concludes, “The literature suggests that on balance, the bond market is very efficient in that it is difficult to consistently forecast interest rates with greater accuracy than a no-change [from the current interest rate] model.”
BCFF’s consistently poor track record, the high predictive validity of current interest rates, and economic intuition are consistent with an extensive body of research on the superiority of simple prediction models to both more complex models and expert judgment.
Current interest rates are the most accurate and unbiased publicly available estimates for future interest rates that I am aware of. Conveniently, using the current rate also entirely skirts the potential concern about horizon inconsistency with the DCF.
Mr. Coyne cherry-picks his beta calculation methodology, ignoring the wide variety of valid potential approaches and best practice for choosing among them.
Q.HOW DOES MR. COYNE ESTIMATE THE BETA IN HIS CAPM MODEL?
A.Mr. Coyne uses beta estimates from two different financial data providers, Value Line and Bloomberg. He uses Value Line’s betas as reported. Mr. Coyne overrides Bloomberg’s default settings and uses only a subset of the beta estimates Bloomberg provides, such that the calculation methodology and resulting beta estimates he reports for Bloomberg are very similar to Value Line’s.
Importantly, Mr. Coyne selects two nearly identical beta calculation methodologies among the multitude of methodologies commonly used by practitioners and academics, specifically the two that currently tend to produce the highest results.
Q.HOW DO BLOOMBERG AND VALUE LINE ESTIMATE THEIR BETAS?
A.Both Bloomberg and Value Line estimate beta from the slope of a linear regression model of a stock’s returns against the returns of the market overall. Both calculate “raw” betas from a regression of trailing weekly, price-only returns of the stock in question against the corresponding market returns. Both also report “Blume-adjusted” betas – a weighted-average of approximately 2/3 of the raw beta and 1/3 of the market average of 1.0 – to correct for an empirically observed tendency for betas, on average, to regress toward the market mean over time that I discuss in more detail below. Other details of their respective methodologies differ, which is why their beta estimates are not exactly the same even after Mr. Coyne’s modifications to Bloomberg’s defaults. Figure 19 summarizes the key elements of Value Line’s and Bloomberg’s beta estimation methodologies. 
Figure 19. Value Line and Bloomberg beta estimation methodologies

Q.MR. COYNE WRITES, “[I]T IS IMPORTANT TO EMPHASIZE THAT BETA COEFFICIENTS ARE CALCULATED OVER A FIVE-YEAR PERIOD.” IS FIVE YEARS OF WEEKLY RETURNS WITH THE BLUME ADJUSTMENT THE ONLY WAY TO CALCULATE BETA?
A.No. Academic studies commonly use five years of monthly returns, without the Blume adjustment. Other financial data providers, including some used by Mr. Coyne elsewhere in his analysis, calculate beta using different trailing histories, return frequencies, and without the Blume adjustment. Yahoo! Finance and Zacks – data sources for some of Mr. Coyne’s other cost of capital analyses – use five years of monthly returns and are unadjusted, like many academic studies. S&P Global Market Intelligence (S&P GMI), another data source frequently cited by Mr. Coyne, reports 1- and 3-year betas using daily returns, also without the Blume adjustment.
Bloomberg’s default trailing history is 2 years. Bloomberg allows users to override several of its calculation parameters, such as the trailing history, market index, and return frequency. Bloomberg also reports both raw and adjusted beta by default. Mr. Coyne apparently overrode Bloomberg’s default trailing history parameter and simply ignored its raw beta estimates to arrive at the Bloomberg betas used in his CAPM, which are nearly identical to those provided by Value Line.
Figure 20 lists recent betas from S&P GMI, Yahoo! Finance, and Zacks for Mr. Coyne’s GPC proxy group. The average betas are 0.43 and 0.76 for S&P GMI using 1 and 3 years of daily returns, and 0.48 for Yahoo! Finance and Zacks, both using 5 years of monthly returns. All four sources’ beta estimates are lower than Mr. Coyne’s 0.85-0.88 figures.

Q.WHY DO BLOOMBERG AND OTHER FINANCIAL DATA PROVIDERS USE DIFFERENT BETA CALCULATION METHODOLOGIES, PROVIDE MULTIPLE ESTIMATES, AND ALLOW USERS TO OVERRIDE THEIR DEFAULTS?
A.Beta is intended to be a forward-looking measure of relative risk, so it is inherently uncertain. It cannot be measured directly (like an interest rate) and is usually estimated from historical data, as the slope of the regression of the returns of a stock against the returns of the market over a recently-ended historical period. Estimates based on historical data generally reasonably reflect future expectations, because most companies’ risk profiles change slowly over time. Slowly changing risk profiles prove particularly true for the relatively stable and predictable utility sector.
But if a dramatic change in the market or in individual stocks occurs, as in the pandemic-related market turmoil of early 2020, that change will influence the beta estimate for as long as the period of change is included in the trailing data used in the beta calculation, even if investors’ risk perceptions have returned to their level prior to the dramatic change. Analysts should always examine whether the change in market conditions was temporary or is sustained. Mr. Coyle’s analysis suffers from his failure to examine whether the pandemic-related change in market conditions was temporary or has been sustained. 
Q.HOW CAN WE DETERMINE WHETHER THE CHANGE IN INVESTORS’ RISK PERCEPTIONS WAS TEMPORARY OR HAS BEEN SUSTAINED?
A.Determining whether the change in investors’ risk perception was temporary or has been sustained is typically done by examining how betas calculated using different amounts of trailing data and returns calculated at different frequencies – for example, daily, weekly, or monthly – have changed over time.
Q.IS THE NEED TO ESTIMATE BETA USING DIFFERENT METHODOLOGIES WELL KNOWN?
A.Yes. Utility cost of capital expert witnesses Michael Vilbert and Bente Villadsen have written about the trade-offs of different methodologies, highlighting the need to consider shorter calculation intervals in the wake of abrupt disruptions such as was experienced first during and then immediately after the pandemic-driven bout of market turmoil in early 2020:
The choices for the interval for the return data and the length of the beta estimation window involve trade-offs between obtaining more observations through the choice of a longer window and/or more frequent return data, ensuring that no structural change has occurred during the estimation window, and avoiding problems due to insufficient trading activity. … Balancing these considerations, economists typically recommend estimating beta using daily, weekly, or monthly returns over the most recent 2- to 5-year period, with weekly being the more common, except if there are reasons to think that the industry might be subject to recent changes in systematic risk so that the use of a more recent data window is desirable.
The need to examine beta using different calculation methodologies is also reflected in data providers’ offerings. As explained previously, Bloomberg allows users to easily override its default beta calculation parameters. S&P GMI, in addition to reporting betas calculated using 1 and 3 years of trailing data, provides its users with spreadsheet models that allow them to modify all of its beta calculation parameters.
In using both Value and Bloomberg betas, Mr. Coyne implicitly acknowledges that no single beta calculation methodology necessarily reflects current investor expectations. But the differences in the Value Line and (modified) Bloomberg beta methodologies he uses are immaterial. Their different market indexes – NYSE Composite for Value Line, S&P 500 for Bloomberg – are nearly perfectly correlated (0.96 correlation), and their slightly different Blume adjustment weights yield differences in beta of 2% or less across the range of typical raw utility betas (0.4-1.0). Mr. Coyne’s chosen methodologies hardly reflect the wide range of ways beta could be calculated, each of which could produce dramatically different results. As Nobel laureate Fischer Black, one of the pioneers of empirical testing of beta and the CAPM, famously admonished, “Watch out for data mining!” – running an analysis several different ways but reporting only the outcomes that support one’s conclusions.
Q.WHICH METHODOLOGICAL DIFFERENCES ACCOUNT FOR THE MOST VARIATION IN BETA ESTIMATES?
A.The largest potential sources of variation in beta estimates arise from their trailing return history duration, return calculation frequency, and Blume adjustment parameters.
Q.HOW DOES THE DURATION OF TRAILING RETURN HISTORY AFFECT BETA ESTIMATES?
A.Following bouts of high market volatility, such as was experienced in the early days of the pandemic in February and March 2020, betas will be affected as long as the trailing history includes the volatile period, even if market conditions have stabilized. For example, S&P GMI reports unadjusted betas calculated from both 1 and 3 years of daily returns. As of July 27, 2022, the average 1-year beta for the proxy group, which does not include the volatile period, was 0.28; the average 3-year beta, which does include the volatile period, was 0.72. For comparison, on the same day, Yahoo! Finance’s and Zacks’s group average 5-year unadjusted betas using monthly returns were 0.45.
Figure 21 plots the raw beta for the entire utility sector using 1, 2, and 5 years of weekly returns from June 1926 through May 2022. At any given time, beta can be very sensitive to the trailing history used. As of the end of August 2022, the betas using the 1-, 2-, and 5-year trailing histories were 0.51, 0.47, and 0.71, respectively.
[Remainder of page intentionally left blank.]
Figure 21. Utility sector raw beta – trailing return history sensitivity
July 1926-August 2022

In general, betas calculated using longer return histories tend to be the more stable over time, as the effect of any short-term period of volatility is reduced by longer surrounding periods of stability. Because utility cost of capital proceedings seek a relatively long-term estimate, it might be tempting to conclude that a longer trailing history is preferred in estimating a utility’s beta for purposes of authorizing an ROE. As Vilbert and Villadsen astutely recommend, though, “if there are reasons to think that the industry might be subject to recent changes in systematic risk … the use of a more recent data window is desirable.” The current 5-year beta of 0.71, driven by the market turmoil of early 2020, is higher than its historical average since 1955 of approximately 0.55. The 1- and 2-year betas, at 0.51 and 0.47, respectively, are in-line with their long-term historical averages. After the brief period of unusual market volatility in early 2020 not seen since the World War II era, investor perceptions of utility risk quickly settled to their pre-pandemic levels. Because of their shorter trailing histories, the 1- and 2-year betas are more reflective of current investor sentiment.
Figure 21 also betrays Mr. Coyne’s claim that “the recent movement in betas captures a trend that began five years ago but has been accelerated by the recent market conditions” and that “[t]he substantial increase in Beta coefficients for the proxy group companies represents a significant departure from how investors have typically viewed utilities relative to the broader market and is not just COVID-19 related.” Regardless of the duration of the trailing return used, utility sector betas were in a decade-plus-long decline before the pandemic. Once the early-pandemic period of market turmoil fell out of the trailing historical data, as with the 1- and 2-year betas, betas returned to their pre-pandemic levels.
The sharp declines in the 1- and 2-year betas in Figure 21 make it clear that Mr. Coyne’s elevated 5-year weekly betas are not valid indicators of current investor expectations but purely artifacts of the inclusion of a transitory and short-term market anomaly.
Q.HOW DOES THE FREQUENCY OF RETURN CALCULATION AFFECT BETA ESTIMATES?
A.Figure 22 plots the raw beta for the entire utility sector using 5 years of monthly, weekly, and daily returns from June 1926 through May 2022. As with the trailing history, at any given time, beta can be very sensitive to the return calculation frequency used. As of the end of August 2022, the betas using the monthly, weekly, and daily trailing histories were 0.50, 0.71, and 0.66, respectively. In general, betas tend to be more stable at higher return calculation frequencies. For any given trailing history duration (e.g., 1, 2, or 5 years), shorter return frequencies generate more data for use in the beta calculation: weekly returns generate approximately four times more data than monthly; daily approximately five times more than weekly. As the return frequency increases, any extreme data points are averaged with a larger number of “typical” data points, which tends to mitigate abrupt changes in beta over time.
This finding would tend to recommend using shorter return frequencies, due to their greater stability over time. But the choice of return frequency should reflect the time horizon of the analysis in which the CAPM-derived cost of equity will be used. Utility cost of capital proceedings seek to estimate a cost of equity that applies over a multi-year period. This consideration recommends a longer calculation frequency.
Figure 22. Utility sector 5-year raw beta – return calculation frequency sensitivity
July 1926-August 2022

Q.HOW DO OTHER BETA CALCULATION PARAMETERS IMPACT THE VARIATION IN BETA ESTIMATES?
A.Even a beta calculation parameter as seemingly arbitrary as the day of the week on which weekly returns are calculated can materially affect the beta estimate. Figure 23 shows the 5-year trailing beta, i.e., raw Value Line-equivalent, with returns calculated on each weekday. Currently, Friday yields the highest beta, 0.78, but simply changing the calculation day to Tuesday reduces the beta to 0.54, 31% lower. This day-of the-week return calculation effect is only partially mitigated by averaging multiple utilities. The stock prices of individual companies within the sector tend to move together on any given day, so weekly returns calculated on the same day will tend to be similar.
This finding highlights the deficiency in Mr. Coyne’s analysis in failing to examine alternative beta calculation methodologies and the potential arbitrariness of his selected methodologies. It also further highlights the need for caution in using the mechanically calculated betas provided by Value Line, Bloomberg, or other financial data providers, particularly betas calculated using weekly returns, without examining how they’ve changed over time or comparing them to long-term historical averages.
Figure 23. Utility sector 5-year weekly raw beta – return calculation day sensitivity
July 1926-August 2022

Q.YOU LISTED THE BLUME ADJUSTMENT AS A THIRD SOURCE OF VARIATION IN BETA ESTIMATES. WHAT IS THE ORIGIN OF THE BLUME ADJUSTMENT?
A.The Blume adjustment is based on an analysis conducted by Wharton professor Marshall Blume in the early 1970s. Analyzing beta-sorted portfolios, he found a tendency for betas, on average, to regress toward the market average beta, 1.0, from one time period to the next. Based on this finding, some providers of beta estimates report adjusted betas that are a weighted average of the raw estimate and the market mean. The most common weighting is 2/3 on the raw beta, 1/3 on the market beta (1.0):
.
For stocks with raw betas below 1.0, like most utilities historically, the effect of the adjustment is to increase the beta one-third of the way toward 1.0. For example, a stock with a raw beta of 0.4 would have an adjusted beta of 2/3 x 0.4 + 1/3 = 0.6. For its adjusted beta, Bloomberg uses the common 2/3 and 1/3 weights. Value Line’s weights are similar: 0.67 and 0.35, respectively. Value Line also rounds to the nearest 0.05.
As Vilbert and Villadsen note, “analysts have different views on whether to use raw or adjusted betas,” hence the reporting of unadjusted betas by Bloomberg, S&P GMI, Yahoo! Finance, and Zacks.
Q.IS THE BLUME ADJUSTMENT VALID FOR UTILITIES?
A.No, it is not. The Blume adjustment is based on an observation of the tendency of betas, on average, to regress toward 1.0. But not every stock exhibits this tendency. Blume did not investigate whether and how this tendency might vary across stocks with different characteristics.
Rutgers professor Richard Michelfelder investigated the validity of the beta adjustment specifically for utility stocks and found no evidence of the average tendency observed by Blume. This can be clearly seen in Figure 24, which shows the same 7-year monthly beta used by Blume in his original analysis for the entire utility sector going back to 1926. Since the 1950s, the beta for the utility sector as a whole has tended to regress toward 0.50-0.60, not 1.0.
[Remainder of page intentionally left blank.]
Figure 24. Utility sector 7-year monthly raw beta
July 1926-August 2022

Blume speculated as to why betas, on average, tend to regress toward 1.0 over time. High-beta firms tend to be newer and smaller; as they mature and grow, they become more risk-averse. In contrast, low-beta firms tend to run out of low-risk investment opportunities and must accept more risk to stay in business. Neither of these causal explanations applies to utility operating companies, like Georgia Power and the publicly traded members of the GPC proxy group. They are large and mature, and their investments tend to have consistently low risk profiles over time. These attributes combine to keep utilities’ betas sustainably and significantly below 1.0.
Empirical analysis specifically investigating utility betas, grounded in sound economic reasoning, demonstrates that utility betas do not have a tendency to regress toward the market average and therefore should not be Blume-adjusted.
Q.WHAT ARE YOUR CONCLUSIONS ABOUT MR. COYNE’S CHOICE OF BETA ESTIMATION METHODOLOGY?
A.The variation in the three most recent beta estimates in Figure 21 suggests we should not unthinkingly use the most recent trailing betas from Value Line, Bloomberg, or any other data provider. It’s important to keep in mind that all methodologies are intended to produce estimates of investors’ future expectations. The elevated current 5-year betas used by Mr. Coyne are artifacts of arbitrary choices of calculation period; there is no reason to believe they reflect investors’ current long-term expectations.
The seven-week bout of market volatility in early 2020 was an anomaly, and investor perceptions of utility risk quickly returned to their pre-pandemic levels. The 5-year return history used in Mr. Coyne’s beta estimates does not accurately reflect current investor sentiment.
The betas used by Mr. Coyne are inflated because they are (1) Blume-adjusted and (2) incorporate an anomalous period that does not reflect investors’ current, long-term expectations.
Mr. Coyne estimates his market risk premium using a constant-growth DCF model with the same flawed perpetuity growth assumption as his proxy group DCF analysis.
Q.HOW DOES MR. COYNE ESTIMATE THE THIRD ASSUMPTION IN THE CAPM, THE MARKET RISK PREMIUM?
A.Mr. Coyne estimates the market risk premium (MRP) using the same constant-growth discounted cash flow model he uses for the proxy group in his DCF analysis. As detailed in Section IV.C.2 above, Mr. Coyne’s fatally flawed implementation of the CG DCF erroneously assumes analysts’ 3-to-5-year EPS growth estimates can be sustained into perpetuity, a deficiency of which he is aware. This assumption is invalid for several reasons, perhaps the most compelling of which is that it is simply economically impossible for the market to sustain analysts’ forecast growth rates for even a decade, much less forever. Since 1926, U.S. stock market dividend growth has averaged 4.9% (1.9% in real terms) – roughly 40% of the 12.4%-14.2% range assumed by Mr. Coyne. The market has never sustained 12.4% dividend growth for even seven years, much less into perpetuity – certainly raising a legitimate “concern that short term growth rates may not be sustainable over the longer-term.” Mr. Coyne nonetheless ignores his own advice and uses the constant growth DCF to estimate his CAPM MRP. Consequently, Mr. Coyne’s MRP results beggar belief: at 11.8%-12.5%, they are roughly 2.5 times the long-term historical average of approximately 5%.
Mr. Coyne’s flawed CAPM results should be disregarded.
Q.WHAT IS YOUR OVERALL ASSESSMENT OF THE CAPM AND MR. COYNE’S IMPLEMENTATION OF IT?
A.The CAPM is conceptually sound and one of the most widely used COE models in corporate finance. But Mr. Coyne’s implementation choices – a forecast, not current, risk-free interest rate; a cherry-picked adjusted beta that is not reflective of current market conditions or utilities’ long term risk profile; and a flawed MRP model – yields systematically upwardly-biased results. His flawed CAPM results should be disregarded.
Implementing the CAPM with more rigorously estimated assumptions produces substantially lower COE estimates.
Q.PLEASE EXPLAIN YOUR IMPLEMENTATION OF THE CAPM.
A.There are three components to the CAPM: the risk-free rate, beta, and the market risk premium. For the risk-free rate I use the current, not forecast, 30-year Treasury rate.
As explained above in Section IV.E.2, beta is the most subjective of the three CAPM parameters, and no single calculation methodology produces the most reasonable estimates in all market conditions. Based on my review of the latest research literature, I use the betas provided by Yahoo! Finance, calculated using five years of monthly returns. Five-year monthly betas are consistent with cost of capital proceedings’ objective of estimating a multi-year cost of equity, and their current values strike a reasonable balance between current market sentiment and the long-term historical average for utilities.
I estimate the market risk-premium from the average of the long-term historical trend and a forward-looking estimate calculated using the MS DCF.
The risk-free rate, one of the three CAPM inputs, should be estimated from the current, not forecast, interest rate.
Q.HOW DO YOU ESTIMATE THE RISK-FREE RATE?
A.Like Mr. Coyne, I use the 30-year Treasury. I use the most recent full-month average 30-year Treasury rate, for September 2022, of 3.56%. As explained above in Section IV.E.1, current market interest rates provide an unbiased estimate of future rates and are generally superior to publicly available “expert” forecasts.
Betas calculated using five years of monthly returns are consistent with the objective of a cost of capital proceeding and strike an appropriate balance between recent market conditions and utilities’ long-term historical risk profile.
Q.HOW DO YOU ESTIMATE BETA?
A.As explained in Section IV.E.2 above, no single, widely used approach to estimating beta exists. Beta estimates can vary substantially depending, in particular, on the historical trailing period used, return calculation frequency, and adjustment for long-term trend reversion. I use 5-year monthly betas such as those provided by Yahoo! Finance and Zacks and commonly used in academic studies. Five-year betas using monthly returns are consistent with cost of capital proceedings’ objective of estimating a multi-year expected cost of equity. The current average of 0.48 for the GPC proxy group, shown in Figure 20, strikes an appropriate balance between the long-term historical range 0.5-0.6 and current subdued investor perceptions of risk, as reflected in 1-year betas using daily returns of approximately 0.4.
The market risk premium estimate should reflect both current market conditions and the long-term historical trend.
Q.WHY CAN’T WE JUST LOOK UP THE MRP LIKE, SAY, WE CAN LOOK UP A STOCK PRICE OR INTEREST RATE?
A.The market risk premium is the difference between investors’ expectations of future stock returns and the risk-free (interest) rate. While interest rates are directly observable, expected future market returns are not. The MRP must therefore be estimated. A historical average is a useful check for reasonableness, but the cliché, “past performance is no guarantee of future results,” applies. Instead, analysts use a variety of models and input assumptions to estimate the MRP.
Q.HOW DO YOU ESTIMATE THE MARKET RISK PREMIUM?
A.I use the average of two methods, one historical, the other forward-looking.
Q.HOW DO YOU ESTIMATE THE HISTORICAL MARKET RISK PREMIUM?
A.I use the long-term historical difference in the average real total returns on the market and long-term Treasury bond.
Figure 25 shows the long-term historical real returns on the market and 20-year Treasury bonds, as well as the implied MRP, from June 1926 through August 2022. Over the last 96+ years, stocks have outperformed 20-year Treasurys by 4.83% per year.
Figure 25. Market, 20-year Treasury, and MRP real total return index
June 1926=1.0 (log scale)

The historical MRP is calculated using 20-year Treasury data because that is the most extensive Treasury bond data set available. Because I use the 30-year Treasury in my CAPM analysis, though, the premium is reduced by the current difference in the inflation-adjusted 20- and 30-year Treasurys (TIPS), 0.08%, for a 30-year real MRP of 4.75%. Adjusted for the 30-year inflation rate estimated from the Treasury-TIPS spread, 2.24%, the nominal MRP is 4.86%.
Q.HOW DO YOU ESTIMATE THE FORWARD-LOOKING MRP?
A.I apply the same multi-stage DCF model I use for the GPC proxy group to the market as a whole, represented by the S&P 500 Index, and subtract the current 30-year Treasury.
Q.HOW DO YOU ESTIMATE THE CURRENT DIVIDEND YIELD FOR THE S&P 500 INDEX?
A.I use the same methodology I use for the proxy group members: the most recent dividend paid, through September 30, 2022, divided by the average price of the index over the month of September 2022. The current annualized yield is 1.73%.
Q.HOW DO YOU ESTIMATE THE INITIAL GROWTH RATE FOR THE S&P 500 INDEX?
A.I use the weekly estimate provided in S&P’s weekly S&P 500 earnings and estimate report. I use this source because it is publicly available, well-known, frequently updated, and produced by the party with the most intimate knowledge of the index. As of September 30, 2022, S&P’s estimate for the S&P 500 Index’s 3-to-5-year growth rate is 13.6%.
Q.HOW DO YOU ESTIMATE THE TERMINAL GROWTH RATE FOR THE S&P 500 INDEX?
A.Many analysts incorrectly assume long-term dividend growth equal to nominal GDP growth. Historically, per-share payout growth, whether measured as dividends or dividends plus net share buybacks, has tracked GDP per capita. I assume a terminal growth rate based on forecast real long-term per-capita GDP plus the current market forecast for long-term inflation, estimated as described in Section IV.D.3.
For long-term per-capita GDP growth, I use the average of the most recent long-term CPI-adjusted forecasts from three government agencies: the Congressional Budget Office (CBO), the Energy Information Administration (EIA), and the Social Security Administration (SSA). I use the compound annual growth rate from 2042 to remove any near-term transitory effects, such as post-Covid economic recovery, and to align with the time period used to estimate long-term inflation (years 21 through 30 from today).
TIPS payouts are tied to CPI, so the Treasury-TIPS spread provides a forecast of consumer price inflation. In contrast, real GDP forecasts are deflated by the GDP deflator, which reflects the prices of all domestic expenditures, including by businesses and government. For consistency with the CPI forecast derived from the Treasury-TIPS spread, which reflects only the prices paid by consumers, I use each agency’s nominal GDP forecast deflated by its CPI forecast, rather than its GDP deflator forecast. Figure 26 summarizes the three agencies’ real long-term per-capita GDP forecasts.

The average of the CBO, EIA, and SSA (the agencies) CPI-deflated long-term per-capita GDP growth rates is 1.26%. Adding the same long-term inflation expectation, 1.57%, used to estimate the terminal growth rate in the proxy group MS DCF in Section IV.D.3 above gives a nominal rate of 2.85%. The corresponding average of the CBO, EIA, and SSA long-term per-capita nominal GDP growth rates is 3.64%. I use the market-implied long-term inflation rate rather than the agencies’ for two reasons. First, although all three forecasts are the agencies’ most recent, they are stale in comparison to the September 2022 average Treasury rates used to estimate inflation. Second, as demonstrated by the analysis of BCFF forecasts, market-derived data are generally considered less biased and more accurate indicators of investor expectations than expert forecasts.
Q.WHAT IS YOUR FORWARD-LOOKING MRP?
A.The S&P 500 MS DCF yields a forecast return of 6.43%. This result is consistent with the buy-side equity return forecasts summarized in Figure 3, which average 5.9% over the 10+-year horizon. Subtracting the current T30, 3.57%, gives an MRP of 2.87%. 
Q.AND YOUR COMBINED MRP?
A.The average of my historical (4.86%) and forward-looking (2.87%) MRPs is 3.86%.
Implementing the CAPM with more rigorously estimated parameters yields COE estimates approximately one-third of Mr. Coyne’s values.
Q.WHAT ARE THE RESULTS OF YOUR CAPM ANALYSIS?
A.The Yahoo! Finance and Zacks 5-year monthly betas for the GPC proxy group, listed in Figure 20, are both 0.48. The corresponding COE is 5.41%, approximately 40% of the 13.55% average of Mr. Coyne’s estimates.
Q.RECENT UTILITY BOND YIELDS ARE CLOSE TO, AND EVEN EXCEED, YOUR MS DCF AND CAPM RESULTS, 5.97% AND 5.41%, RESPECTIVELY. SHOULDN’T THE PREMIUM OVER BOND YIELDS BE GREATER?
A.To compare my COE results to utility bond yields, we must use the monthly average yield that corresponds to the proxy group’s average credit-rating, Baa1, for September 2022, the averaging period of the inputs to my MS DCF and CAPM. Moody’s provides several widely referenced utility bond yield indexes, but not specifically for Baa1-rated utility bonds. Nonetheless, the Baa1 yield can be estimated by interpolating between Moody’s Baa and A monthly average utility bond yields for September 2022, 2/3 x 5.61% + 1/3 x 5.28% = 5.50% – higher than my CAPM result of 5.41%.
But a utility COE estimate – an expected return on equity – cannot be directly compared to the corresponding utility bond yield. Reported bond yields are yields to maturity, assuming no default. Default risk for bonds with a Baa1 rating reduces their expected returns by approximately 0.46%. Similarly, bonds are not as liquid as stocks and attract a liquidity premium of approximately 0.33%. Deducting the default and liquidity premia from the Baa1 utility bond yield to maturity reduces it to approximately 4.74%, 1.24% and 0.67% lower than my MS DCF and CAPM COE estimates, respectively.
There is no hard-and-fast rule for what the spread of equity returns over the corresponding cost of debt should be. My MS DCF and CAPM results are consistent with my forward-looking MRP and beta estimates, third-party forecasts for total market returns, and the widely recognized low risk profile of utilities.
Mr. Coyne’s flotation cost adjustment is not analytically sound and is therefore unwarranted.
Q.Mr. Coyne adds a flotation cost adjustment to his ROE model results. Is a flotation cost adjustment warranted?
A.No. A flotation cost adjustment is not warranted. Mr. Coyne cites Roger Morin’s New Regulatory Finance in support of adding a flotation cost adjustment, but the application of the flotation cost adjustment provided in Morin’s book is conceptually flawed. The math relied on by Mr. Coyne for his recommendation shows that shareholders make money from the issuance of new stock, so flotation cost adjustments are especially inappropriate. 
Mr. Morin’s flotation cost adjustment assumes that the market price of the equity issued is equal to book value. But, as shown in Figure 4 above, the average market-to-book ratio of the GPC proxy group is 2.1. As implied by the very formula provided by Mr. Morin, when the market price is greater than book value, new equity issuance is accretive to existing shareholders – that is, a share of the proceeds of the new issuance accrues to them.
Morin derives the flotation cost adjustment from the formula for the extended DCF model under the assumption of continuous external stock financing:
,
where  is the earnings retention ratio,  is ROE,  is the annual rate of new equity issuance (expressed as a share of existing book equity), and  is the accretion factor, equal to . He then adjusts  for flotation costs, , so that . But as long as  exceeds , i.e., the reciprocal of the market-to-book ratio, existing shareholders gain from new share issuance. Mr. Coyne estimates flotation costs to be 2.47%, so  = 0.98, much greater than the reciprocal of the GPC proxy group average M/B ratio, 1/2.1 = 0.48.
Q.Under the NARUC fairness principle, ROE should equal the actual cost of equity. This would imply that the market-to-book ratio equals 1.0, in which case new equity issuance would not be accretive. If the Commission adheres to the NARUC fairness principle, should ROE be adjusted for flotation costs?
A.No. Even if the M/B ratio is equal to 1.0, there is no reason to adjust the ROE for new equity issuance costs.
Purchasers of the newly issued equity know that a portion of proceeds will cover transaction costs, and the offering price reflects this information. There is no need to compensate new investors for issuance costs, which they knowingly pay when they purchase new shares, with an adjustment to the ROE. Similarly, existing shareholders know that the company may issue new equity over time. These costs, and their potential effect on expected returns, are likewise reflected in investors’ willingness to buy, hold, or sell shares that determines share prices.
Mr. Coyne’s flotation cost adjustment is small and is overwhelmed by the imprecision in his models. At 0.08%, it amounts to less than 2% of the 8.99%-13.55% range of Mr. Coyne’s base ROE results. To add a flotation cost adjustment is akin to the elementary school student, who, upon being asked what they learned during a field trip to the science museum, proudly says, “Earth is 4.5 billion years and [glancing at a clock] six hours and forty-three minutes old.”
Mr. Coyne’s flotation cost adjustment is conceptually flawed and not materially significant. It should be disregarded.
Georgia Power’s ROE and equity ratio should optimally balance customer and investor interests.
COE model results must be adjusted for differences in equity ratio.
Q.In Section IV.B above, you discussed adjusting COE model results for differences in equity ratios among the proxy group members. How do you do that?
A.The MS DCF and CAPM yield levered costs of equity. To account for differences in capital structure among the proxy group members, the COE results are unlevered to estimate the cost of equity assuming 100% equity financing. The unlevered cost of equity, , is typically expressed as an adjustment to beta in the CAPM:
,
where the unlevered beta, , is expressed in terms of the levered equity beta, :
.
For consistency and comparability, I apply the same methodology – unlevering relative to the risk-free rate, not the company’s cost of debt – to the MS DCF model results:


,
where  and  refer to debt and equity, respectively. Best practice is to use market, not book, values for both debt and equity as market reflects investors’ actual exposure; they buy and sell securities at market value, not book. Market values for the debt carried by the proxy group members are not readily available, though, so book value is assumed.

The variation in the unlevered COE estimates is much lower than in the levered COEs, with approximately half the standard deviation (0.25 vs 0.57) and range (1.00 vs. 1.95). The underlying businesses of the proxy group members are very similar, so their risk profiles and corresponding overall costs of capital are expected to be similar as well. Their equity ratios vary considerably, though, from 42% to 74%, which introduces variation in their levered costs of equity. This variation due to differences in equity ratios makes levered COEs an inappropriate basis for determining the ROE of a target company, like Georgia Power, which will likely have a different equity ratio.
Q.How do you use the proxy group average unlevered COE to determine your recommendation for Georgia Power’s ROE, which is levered?
A.The unlevered COE is “relevered” using the same formula described above, the terms of which can be rearranged as:
.
In Section III above, I explained that the equity ratio depends on ROE. ROE, in turn, depends on the equity ratio. They can be determined jointly, in an iterative calculation process that is easily performed in common spreadsheet software like Microsoft Excel or Google Sheets.
ROE and equity ratio should be optimized to minimize customer costs while meeting investor requirements.
Q.In Section III.A above, you provided an illustrative analysis of the inter-relationships between equity ratio, ROE, and credit quality. Can you conduct that analysis specifically for Georgia Power?
A.Yes. Such an analysis can be conducted for Georgia Power using data provided in Moody’s most recent credit update.
Figure 28 applies the analysis illustrated in Figure 8 above to the financial data provided in Moody’s October 2021 credit update for Georgia Power. As before, different levels of ROE are represented by the upward curving lines. As the equity ratio increases along the horizontal axis, so does the cash flow-to-debt ratio, depicted on the vertical axis. Here, cash flow from operations (CFO) is used instead of FFO, for consistency with Moody’s preferred metric. Black dashed horizontal lines have been added at the CFO/debt levels that correspond to potential target credit ratings: Georgia Power’s current Baa1 (XXX% CFO/debt) and an upgrade to A3 (XXX%).
The dark gray dot on the upper of the two ROE arcs represents Georgia Power’s proposed 11.00% ROE and 56% equity ratio. The corresponding CFO/debt ratio is indicated by the height of the dot on the vertical axis, XXX% – well above the XXX% required to maintain Georgia Power’s current Baa1 credit rating. Either or both of Georgia Power’s proposed ROE and equity ratio can be reduced to lower customer costs while still satisfying investor demands.
The lower of the two ROE arcs represents Georgia Power’s 4.78% unlevered (100% equity) COE. As discussed previously, the corresponding levered ROE will increase as the equity ratio declines. To maintain Georgia Power’s current Baa1 rating would require an equity ratio of 59.2%, slightly higher than GPC’s proposal, and an ROE of 5.62%. If instead a higher A3 credit rating is targeted, the equity ratio and ROE would need to be 61.7% and 5.54%, respectively. The trade-off, in terms of customer costs, associated with these higher equity ratios will be quantified below.
The two white dots immediately below GPC’s proposal illustrate scenarios in which an equity ratio constraint is imposed, here equal to GPC’s proposed 56%. To maintain target credit ratings of Baa1 or A3, the ROE would need to be increased to 7.18% and 8.36%, respectively. The trade-off, in terms of customer costs, associated with an equity ratio constraint will also be quantified below.

[Remainder of page intentionally left blank.]

Figure 28. Relationship between equity ratio, ROE, and credit quality for Georgia 
Power

An optimized ROE and equity ratio can significantly reduce customer costs while meeting the demands of both equity and debt investors.
Q.How would an optimized ROE and equity ratio impact customer costs?
A.Figure 29 compares the revenue requirement (pre-tax) weighted average rate of return for the five scenarios depicted in Figure 28. The ROE optimized for a Baa1 rating, 5.62%, is 49% lower than Mr. Coyne’s proposed ROE of 11.00% (line 2). Even with the higher 59.2% equity ratio, after grossing up for taxes, the revenue requirement ROR, 5.92%, is 40% lower than GPC’s 9.83% (lines 6-7). If a higher A3 credit rating is targeted, the additional cost of the required higher 61.7% equity ratio is almost completely offset by the lower levered ROE of 5.54%, and the revenue requirement ROR increases only slightly to 5.95%.
Figure 29 also shows the average annual total revenue requirement under each of the five scenarios (line 8). Based on analysis of Georgia Power’s general rate case filings, its proposed combined rate of return for both debt and equity, the latter grossed up for taxes, accounts for more than 25% of its revenue requirement. The total revenue requirement under GPC’s proposal averages $9.14 billion per year from 2023 through 2025. Optimized ROEs and equity ratios, under either the Baa1 or A3 target credit rating, reduce total customer costs by over 11% (line 10) or more than $1.0 billion per year (line 9).
An equity ratio constraint would impose fairly substantial costs on customers, increasing the revenue requirement ROR to 6.96% and 7.84% under Baa1 and A3 credit rating targets, respectively – an increase of nearly 100 and 200 basis points relative to the corresponding minimum-cost scenarios. While total customer costs are lower than under GPC’s proposal, they are only 50% to 70% of the reduction without an equity ratio constraint. These results further demonstrate the flaw in the “leverage up” (reduce the equity ratio) argument described in Section III.A above. It is much more cost effective to use any spare credit capacity to reduce ROE than to increase debt.

Q.What are the implications of an optimized ROE and equity ratio on residential customer bills?
A.Average residential customer bills can be estimated from Georgia Power’s total retail base rate revenue and sales of electricity forecast for the 12 months ending July 31, 2023, and Budget 2022 Load and Energy Forecast customer count projection. Assuming the 11% cost reduction under either of the optimized ROE/equity ratio scenarios applies uniformly across customer classes, Georgia Power residential customers would save approximately $135 per year.
Q.What are your ROE and equity ratio recommendations for Georgia Power?
A.I recommend the A3 target credit rating scenario for Georgia Power, 5.54% ROE and 61.7% equity ratio, for several reasons. Both the A3 and Baa1 scenarios are consistent with the Supreme Court’s guidance provided in Hope Natural Gas to set “just and reasonable rates” through a “balancing of the investor and the consumer interests.” In contrast, Mr. Coyne’s recommendation and the two equity ratio-constrained scenarios are overly generous to investors at the expense of customers and therefore fail the Hope standard.
The A3 and Baa1 scenarios have the same estimated cost to customers over the 2023-25 timeframe. This is largely because the analysis assumes the improved credit rating does not materially reduce Georgia Power’s average cost of debt during the 2023-25 timeframe. Beyond 2025, though, the average cost of debt would be materially lower under the A3 scenario, reducing customer costs relative to the Baa1 scenario. Figure 30 summarizes my COE analysis and ROE and equity ratio recommendations.

Q.If the Commission adopts your ROE and equity ratio recommendations, how would The Southern Company’s shareholders be affected?
A.ROEs in excess of the cost of equity resembles a zero-sum game, with the interests of shareholders and customers in direct opposition. If the Commission adopted my recommended ROE and equity ratio, I would expect The Southern Company share price to experience a one-time downward adjustment as investors reset their expectations about future returns. Southern would still be able to access the equity markets, because Georgia Power’s ROE, based on its actual cost of equity, i.e., investors’ expected return, would be sufficient to satisfy investors’ demands. Investors who purchase shares after the one-time adjustment would earn returns comparable to the return current Southern shareholders expect.
I said that ROE above the cost of equity resembles a zero-sum game between shareholders and customers. It is not quite zero-sum between these two parties, though, because there is a third party: the tax authorities. ROE is grossed-up for taxes; to the extent ROE is reduced, taxes are also reduced. In aggregate, a lower ROE provides approximately one-third more benefit to customers than shareholders lose in foregone profit. As shown in Figure 31, of the $1.04-billion reduction in revenue requirement return under the A3 scenario (Figure 29, line 9), the decline in Georgia Power’s net income accounts for $740 million; $190 million is foregone taxes, of which approximately $150 million (77.5%) is Federal.
Figure 31. Interest, ROE, and income tax revenue requirement under GPC proposal and A3 minimum cost scenario
$ billion

It is important to recognize that Hope established that regulators are not obligated to maintain utility stock market valuations, and that such an obligation would make a nonsense of regulators’ consumer protection mandate:
Ratemaking is indeed but one species of price-fixing. The fixing of prices, like other applications of the police power, may reduce the value of the property which is being regulated. But the fact that the value is reduced does not mean that the regulation is invalid. It does, however, indicate that “fair value” is the end product of the process of ratemaking, not the starting point, as the Circuit Court of Appeals held. The heart of the matter is that rates cannot be made to depend upon “fair value” when the value of the going enterprise depends on earnings under whatever rates may be anticipated.
The impact on Southern’s stock price should not factor at all into the Commission’s determination of the appropriate ROE and equity ratio.
Q.Does this conclude your testimony?
A.Yes.

 5             A.      Summary of Conclusions

 6   Q. WHAT IS THE PURPOSE OF YOUR TESTIMONY IN THIS PROCEEDING?
 7   A. I have been asked by GIPL to assess Georgia Power Company’s (GPC) test year 2023 cost of
 8         capital application to analyze and calculate the return on equity (ROE) and equity ratio that are
 9         (1) adequate to ensure that GPC, from 2023 to 2025, earns a return on its investments
10         “commensurate with returns on investments in other enterprises having corresponding risk,”
11         and (2) “sufficient to assure confidence in the financial integrity of the enterprise, so as to
12         maintain its credit and to attract capital.” 1
13   Q. GENERALLY, WHAT CONCLUSIONS DO YOU REACH FROM YOUR ANALYSIS
14         AND CALCULATIONS?
15   A. The Commission can substantially reduce the authorized ROE requested by Georgia Power,
16         and thereby customer costs (by a billion dollars per year), while still enabling Georgia Power
17         to attract debt and equity investment capital, fairly compensate investors for risk, and maintain
18         Georgia Power’s current credit rating.
19   Q. WHAT ARE YOUR RECOMMENDED AUTHORIZED ROE AND EQUITY RATIO?
20   A. I recommend an authorized return on equity (ROE) equal to Georgia Power’s cost of equity
21         (COE) and paired with an equity ratio that, together with my recommended ROE, minimizes
22         customer costs while maintaining Georgia Power’s financial integrity, fairly compensating
23         debt and equity investors for risk, and improving Georgia Power’s credit rating. My
24         recommended authorized ROE is 5.54% at an equity ratio of 61.7%.

 1            B.       Summary of Findings

 2                     1.         Georgia Power’s cost of capital testimony employs flawed models and
 3                                assumptions that systematically produce upwardly biased ROE
 4                                estimates.

 5   Q. THERE               IS     A     SIGNIFICANT           DIFFERENCE               BETWEEN    YOUR
 6        RECOMMENDATIONS AND GEORGIA POWER’S RECOMMENDED ROE AND
 7        EQUITY RATIO. HOW DO YOU EXPLAIN THAT?
 8   A. The divergence arises from differences in both the models and input assumptions used to
 9        determine our respective ROE and equity recommendations.
10            Georgia Power’s cost of capital expert, Mr. James Coyne, uses four models in developing
11        his recommended return on equity (ROE). Two of those models, the Risk Premium Analysis
12        (RPA) and Expected Earnings Analysis (EEA), both suffer from a severe, invalidating
13        conceptual flaw: they are based on utilities’ authorized or forecast return on equity, not their
14        actual cost of equity (COE), or the “the return to the equity owner … commensurate with
15        returns on investments in other enterprises having corresponding risks.” 2 Thus, these two
16        models are not commonly used in finance outside of utility regulatory proceedings because
17        they do not actually estimate the cost of equity. These models are akin to developing a nutrition
18        recommendation based on what people actually eat, not on what they should eat to maintain a
19        healthy weight.
20            The other two models used by Mr. Coyne – the discounted cash flow model (DCF) and
21        capital asset pricing model (CAPM) – are widely used throughout finance to estimate the cost
22        of capital. But Mr. Coyne’s implementations of each suffer from numerous flaws which bias
23        his results upward.
24            In implementing the DCF, Mr. Coyne uses unnecessarily long trailing price histories to
25        calculate the dividend yield, which bias his figures upward, and he assumes demonstrably
26        unrealistic, economically impossible long-term dividend growth rates.
27            In implementing the CAPM, he uses an interest rate forecast long and widely known to be
28        systematically upwardly biased. He cherry-picks a beta calculation methodology that does not
29        reflect current investor risk perceptions and applies the “Blume” adjustment that is not valid


      2
          Id. (emphasis added).

                                    Mark E. Ellis Direct Testimony, on behalf of GIPL
                                                GPSC Docket No. 44280
                                                          Page 3

                                   PUBLIC DISCLOSURE VERSION
                                            (October 21, 2022 Errata)
 1       for utilities, both of which upwardly bias his results. He fails to examine other, more robust
 2       beta estimation methodologies, investigate whether the pandemic-related changes in market
 3       conditions and investor perceptions of utility risk were temporary or have been sustained, or
 4       compare his results to the long-term history of utility betas.
 5           Mr. Coyne’s chosen methodology does not reflect the wide range of ways beta could be
 6       estimated, each of which could produce dramatically different results. As Nobel laureate
 7       Fischer Black, one of the pioneers of empirical testing of beta and the CAPM, famously
 8       admonished, “Watch out for data mining!” 3 – running an analysis several different ways but
 9       reporting only the outcomes that support one’s conclusions.
10           To estimate the CAPM market risk premium, Mr. Coyne uses the same flawed
11       implementation of the DCF used for his proxy group, again producing an economically
12       impossible result.
13           Finally, Mr. Coyne fails to adjust his ROE estimates for differences in equity ratio among
14       the proxy group members, and between the proxy group average and Georgia Power.
15           Mr. Coyne’s capital structure recommendation is similarly deficient. While the testimony
16       of Georgia Power’s credit expert, Mr. David M. Fetter, refers to key metrics used in assessing
17       credit quality, Mr. Coyne does not provide any analysis or calculations demonstrating how
18       ROE affects those metrics, and how the impact of ROE on those metrics influences his
19       proposed equity ratio. Regulators in other states have authorized ROEs that are substantially
20       lower than those requested by Georgia Power, with comparable or lower equity ratios, without
21       adversely impacting their utilities’ credit ratings, suggesting the Commission can substantially
22       reduce the ROE requested by Georgia Power, and thereby customer costs, while maintaining
23       its credit rating.

 1                    2.      More rigorous, fact-based analysis of Georgia Power’s COE and credit
 2                            metrics yields a recommended ROE roughly 50% lower and an equity
 3                            ratio slightly higher than Georgia Power’s proposal.

 4   Q. PLEASE SUMMARIZE THE MAIN FINDINGS OF YOUR ROE ANALYSES FOR
 5        GEORGIA POWER.
 6   A. Like Mr. Coyne, I use the DCF and CAPM, two models that are widely used throughout
 7        finance, to estimate the cost of capital. Unlike Mr. Coyne, I am careful to use realistic and
 8        rigorously supported assumptions about long-term dividend growth rates, current interest rates,
 9        and risk profiles and premia.
10            As I explain in Section III.A below, the equity ratio required to maintain any given level
11        of credit quality depends on the ROE. Consequently, the ROE and equity ratio must be
12        determined jointly. Instead of Mr. Coyne’s crude equity ratio peer group comparison, which
13        does not consider the most important metrics of credit quality, I model the inter-relationships
14        between key credit metrics, ROE, and equity ratio to arrive at the optimal equity ratio that
15        minimizes customer costs while meeting the return and credit quality requirements of both
16        equity and debt investors.
17            Figure 1 summarizes the key findings of my review of Mr. Coyne’s analysis, the
18        modifications required to correct its deficiencies, the resulting COE estimates, and my ROE
19        and equity ratio recommendations. More rigorous, fact-based, and accurate analyses result in
20        a substantially lower recommendation for Georgia Power’s ROE: 5.54%, roughly one half of
21        Mr. Coyne’s recommended 11.00%, and a slightly higher equity ratio, 61.7% vs. Mr. Coyne’s
22        56%.
23            Based on my analysis of Georgia Power’s general rate case filings, its proposed combined
24        rate of return on both debt and equity, grossed up for taxes, accounts for more than 25% of its
25        revenue requirement. 4 My recommended ROE and equity ratio would reduce Georgia Power
26        customer costs by approximately 11%, over $1 billion, per year.

 1              C.      Organization of testimony

 2   Q. HOW IS YOUR TESTIMONY ORGANIZED?
 3   A. First, I review a few key conceptual issues related to the cost of capital and capital structure.
4          Next, I provide a detailed assessment of Mr. Coyne’s cost of equity estimation methodology
5          and implementation. For the DCF and CAPM, the two of Mr. Coyne’s four cost of equity
6          models that are valid, I explain various modifications to his methodology and assumptions to
7          correct for the deficiencies in Mr. Coyne’s analyses and then provide the resulting COE
8          estimates. I then apply the capital structure concepts reviewed in the first part of my testimony
9          to determine the optimal equity ratio that minimizes customer costs while satisfying the
10         demands of equity and debt investors. Finally, I estimate the potential savings to customers
11         from adopting my recommended ROE and equity ratio instead of Mr. Coyne’s proposal.
12   II.        CONFUSION BETWEEN THE RATE OF RETURN ON CAPITAL AND COST OF
13              CAPITAL HAS LED TO EXCESSIVE AUTHORIZED RETURNS.

14              A.      Rate of return on capital and cost of capital are not the same: rate of return
15                      on capital is a financial performance metric, whereas cost of capital is the
16                      measure of economic cost described in the Hope case.

17   Q. WHAT IS THE OBJECTIVE IN SETTING A UTILITY’S AUTHORIZED RATE OF
18         RETURN?
19   A. The authorized rate of return is the amount of money, expressed as a percentage of capital
20         invested, that a utility is allowed to recover in customer rates to compensate debt and equity
21         investors for assuming the risks of investing in the utility
22              The Supreme Court provides the guiding ratemaking principles in its 1944 Hope Natural
23         Gas case, in which it directs ratemakers to arrive at “just and reasonable rates” by a “balancing
24         of the investor and the consumer interests.” 6 Consumer interests are straightforward: they want
25         to minimize costs and rates. Investors include holders of both the utility’s debt and equity. A
26         utility’s debt investors expect to receive their contractual interest payments. Debtholders
27         therefore have a “legitimate concern with the financial integrity of the company whose rates
28         are being regulated,” 7 i.e., the utility’s credit quality and anticipated ability to fulfill its


     6
         320 U.S. 591, 603.
     7
         Id.

                                  Mark E. Ellis Direct Testimony, on behalf of GIPL
                                              GPSC Docket No. 44280
                                                        Page 7

                                      PUBLIC DISCLOSURE VERSION
                                               (October 21, 2022 Errata)
1              obligations to debtholders. Equity investors expect a fair return, defined in Hope as
 2             “commensurate with returns on investments in other enterprises having corresponding risks.” 8
 3   Q. HOW DOES THE RATE OF RETURN ON CAPITAL DIFFER FROM THE COST OF
 4             CAPITAL?
5    A. The rate of return on capital, often shortened to “rate of return,” is an accounting metric of
 6             financial performance, calculated by dividing the value returned to investors – e.g., interest,
 7             net income – by the amount of capital invested. The cost of capital is the return investors expect
 8             on their investment. It is referred to as a “cost” because it reflects what investors demand in
 9             return for assuming the risk of the investment and, therefore, what companies must pay for that
10             investment. The rate of return on each form of capital, whether calculated retrospectively or
11             estimated prospectively, may or may not equal its respective cost of capital.
12   Q. HOW DOES THE AUTHORIZED RATE OF RETURN ON CAPITAL DIFFER FROM
13             THE RATE OF RETURN AND THE COST OF CAPITAL?
14   A. The authorized rate of return refers to a specific use of this accounting metric to determine a
15             utility’s revenue requirement and customer rates. According to long-established ratemaking
16             principles, which will be explained in more detail below, the authorized rate of return should
17             be set equal to the cost of capital. Figure 2 summarizes the differences between these three sets
18             of metrics for debt, equity, and the combined return.

 2   Q. WHAT PRINCIPLES DO YOU USE TO DETERMINE YOUR RECOMMENDED
 3         AUTHORIZED RATE OF RETURN?
 4   A. As the National Association of Regulatory Utility Commissioners (NARUC) has explained,
 5         “Fundamental financial concepts demonstrate that the fair rate of return to use in ratemaking
 6         for a utility is its cost of capital in order to achieve the proper balance between customers and
 7         investors.” 9 Mr. Coyne has also acknowledged, in ROE testimony in other jurisdictions, “that
 8         the allowed rate of return should equal the cost of capital.” 10 The objective in setting a utility’s
 9         authorized rate of return should, therefore, be to set the rate of return on each source of capital
10         – debt and equity – as close as possible to the actual cost of each source of capital.
11   Q. DOES THE APPROACH TO CALCULATING COST OF EQUITY DIFFER FROM
12         THE APPROACH TO CALCULATING COST OF DEBT?
13   A. Yes. The cost of outstanding debt can be directly determined from its contractual interest rates.
14         Similarly, the cost of debt expected to be issued can be accurately determined from known
15         interest rate indexes for debt of comparable credit quality, such as Moody’s utility bond


       9
         John D. Quackenbush, Cost of Capital and Capital Markets: A Primer for Utility Regulators, Nat’l Ass’n of
         Regul. Util. Comm’s at 10 (2019) (emphasis added) https://bit.ly/3Th1lC8.
      10
         Cal. Pub. Util. Comm’n, Docket No. A 22-04-008 et seq., Reporters’ Transcript: Virtual Proceeding at 65
         (September 12, 2022).

                                       Mark E. Ellis Direct Testimony, on behalf of GIPL
                                                   GPSC Docket No. 44280
                                                             Page 9

                                      PUBLIC DISCLOSURE VERSION
                                                (October 21, 2022 Errata)
 1         indexes, which, in turn, are based on interest rates directly observed in the market. In contrast,
 2         the cost of equity, both existing and to-be-issued, cannot be directly observed and must be
 3         estimated using various models.
 4   Q. WHY IS THE DISTINCTION BETWEEN THE COST OF CAPITAL AND RATE OF
 5         RETURN IMPORTANT?
 6   A. The cost of capital and rate of return (on capital) are entirely different concepts. The rate of
 7         return is a financial performance metric. The cost of capital is an economic concept.
 8         Nonetheless, they are frequently referred to interchangeably in utility regulatory proceedings,
 9         perhaps in part because finance professionals commonly refer to the cost of capital as the
10         expected return (on capital). 11
11             The muddling of the difference between the cost of capital and the rate of return is not just
12         of semantic concern, particularly for the cost of equity, which must be estimated using various
13         models rather than directly observed (like the cost of debt).
14             This confusion between the cost of capital and the return on capital has infiltrated some of
15         the models commonly used in utility cost of capital proceedings to estimate the cost of equity.
16         These models’ apparent influence on regulatory decisions, though, does not make them correct
17         or mean they provide a suitable basis for estimating the cost of equity.
18             Models that rely exclusively on historical or forecast utility rates of return on equity,
19         without reference to utilities’ actual cost of equity, should be rejected outright. Mr. Coyne uses
20         two such models, one relying exclusively on historical authorized ROEs, the other on forecast
21         ROEs. These models incorporate no information about the actual cost of equity and are
22         therefore inherently flawed and produce invalid results. Consistent with the fairness principle
23         described by NARUC, and Mr. Coyne’s own statements, only models that estimate the cost of
24         equity should be used to determine the authorized ROE.


      11
           See, e.g., TIM KOLLER, MARC GOEDHART ET AL., VALUATION, 5TH ED. MCKINSEY & CO. 35 (2010): “The cost of
           capital is the price charged by investors for bearing the risk that the company’s future cash flows may differ
           from what they anticipate when they make the investment. The cost of capital to a company equals the
           minimum return that investors expect to earn from investing in the company. That is why the terms expected
           return to investors and cost of capital are essentially the same. The cost of capital is also called the discount
           rate, because you discount future cash flows at this rate when calculating the present value of an investment, to
           reflect what you will have to pay investors” (emphasis in original).

 1            B.      Multiple, diverse sources of evidence demonstrate that utilities’ authorized
 2                    ROEs far exceed their cost of equity.

 3   Q. DO AUTHORIZED ROES REFLECT THE ACTUAL COST OF EQUITY?
 4   A. No. Substantial, robust evidence suggests that authorized ROEs for nearly all U.S. utilities
 5         exceed their cost of equity. Georgia Power’s ROE follows this national pattern. Below, I
 6         provide three diverse analyses that compellingly demonstrate, individually and collectively,
 7         the substantial gap between authorized ROEs and utilities’ actual cost of equity: expected
8          equity return forecasts produced by investment professionals, utility market-to-book ratios, and
9          the increasing spread between authorized ROEs and interest rates.

10                    1.      Investment firms’ expected return forecasts for the U.S. equity market
11                            as a whole – which is riskier, on average, than utilities – are consistently
12                            lower than utilities’ authorized ROEs.

13   Q. ARE THERE OTHER PUBLIC SOURCES FOR COST OF EQUITY ESTIMATES
14         OUTSIDE UTILITY REGULATORY PROCEEDINGS?
15   A. Utility cost of capital proceedings are not the only purpose for which expected returns on equity
16         are estimated. Investment firms, such as JP Morgan, BlackRock, and T. Rowe Price, regularly
17         publish capital market assumption reports (CMAs) – expected return forecasts for various asset
18         classes. Figure 3 summarizes a survey of U.S. equity market return forecasts published by over
19         thirty firms in 2021.
20            The CMA forecasts shown in Figure 3 are grouped by assumed investment horizon: less
21         than ten years, ten years (the most common), and more than ten years. 12 The average across
22         the longer-term 10-year and more-than-ten-year horizons, 5.7%, is approximately 40% less
23         than the average ROE authorized for regulated utilities throughout the United States in 2021,
24         9.5%. 13 Not a single one of the forty-two expected return forecasts that I reviewed 14 is as high
25         as the average ROE yielded by any of Mr. Coyne’s models.


      12
         Thirty-four CMA reports were reviewed, of which two were excluded due to insufficient data on investment

         (last accessed October 6, 2022). [Hereinafter referred to as “M. Ellis analysis of S&P GMI data.”]
      14
         Some CMAs included forecasts for multiple time horizons, so the number of forecasts exceeds the number of
         reports.

4   utilities than for the market. The fact that authorized utility ROEs are so much higher than the
5   expected returns on the overall market, which is higher risk, is a compelling indicator that
6   authorized ROEs far exceed utility investors’ expected returns, i.e., utilities’ actual cost of
7   equity.

 1                      2.       Market-to-book ratios reveal that utilities’ cost of equity is
 2                               substantially lower than authorized ROEs.

 3   Q. WHAT IS A MARKET-TO-BOOK RATIO?
 4   A. “Market” value refers to the price one must pay to purchase a share of a company’s stock at
 5         any given time. “Book” value refers to the net equity invested in a company. In general, market
 6         value reflects the discounted value of a company’s future cash flows, while book value reflects
 7         the historical investment in the company. The market-to-book ratio (M/B) is a commonly used
 8         financial metric that indicates the amount of shareholder value added in excess of shareholders’
 9         return expectations.
10   Q. HOW DOES A MARKET-TO-BOOK RATIO RELATE TO RETURNS?
11   A. It is a well understood financial principle that a market-to-book ratio greater than 1.0 indicates
12         the company is expected to earn a return on its investment in excess of the actual cost of capital.
13         A positive net present value (NPV), i.e., the value of a company minus its investments, is the
14         signature indicator of a rate of return that exceeds the cost of capital. NPV is equal to
15         investment multiplied by (M/B – 1.0), so M/B exceeding 1.0 indicates that NPV is positive.
16   Q. WHAT DO UTILITY STOCK MARKET-TO-BOOK RATIOS REVEAL ABOUT
17         ROES RELATIVE TO UTILITIES’ COST OF EQUITY?
18   A. It has long been recognized that utilities’ market-to-book (M/B) ratios provide insight into the
19         relationship between authorized return and the true cost of capital. Legendary regulatory
20         economist Alfred Kahn 16 called attention to this phenomenon over fifty years ago in his 1970
21         classic, The Economics of Regulation: Principles and Institutions:

22                           [T]he sharp appreciation in the prices of public utility stocks, to

 2            Another commonly referenced textbook on utility regulation, Kolbe, Read, and Hall’s The
 3        Cost of Capital: Estimating the Rate of Return for Public Utilities, recommends using a M/B
 4        ratio of 1.0 as a “guide for regulators” in setting the cost of capital: 21

 5                         The market-to-book ratio expresses the market value of the
 6                         firm’s outstanding common stock to the book value of its equity.
 7                         If the two are equal the expected return on the book will equal
 8                         the expected return on the market value of the company, which
 9                         in turn will equal the cost of capital for a company of that degree
10                         of risk.


11            Kahn and Kolbe, et al., drew their conclusions from the basic financial concept of net
12        present value (NPV) discussed above. Because utilities trade at a premium to book value (i.e.,
13        invested capital), they have positive NPVs – prima facie evidence that they are earning more
14        than their cost of capital.
