 1   Q. IN PRACTICAL, DOLLARS-AND-CENTS TERMS, WHAT DOES IT MEAN FOR A
 2         UTILITY TO HAVE A MARKET VALUE THAT IS HIGHER THAN ITS BOOK
 3         VALUE?
 4   A. In practical terms, this means that, for every dollar of equity a utility invests, shareholders
 5         receive back not just their investment plus a reasonable return, which would be the case when
 6         M/B = 1.0, but additional value equivalent to their equity investment multiplied by (M/B –
 7         1.0). At current M/B ratios near 2.0, authorized ROEs effectively double the value of utilities’
 8         equity investments, on top of returning their cost of equity. Such high returns are not necessary
 9         to attract capital and needlessly increase customer costs.
10   Q. ARE YOU ABLE TO CALCULATE GEORGIA POWER’S MARKET-TO-BOOK
11         RATIO?
12   A. Georgia Power is not publicly traded, so its market value is not directly observable.
13         Nonetheless, The Southern Company’s market-to-book ratio, averaging 2.82 over the month
14         of September 2022, 22 provides a reasonable estimate.
15   Q. IS MR. COYNE AWARE OF THE RELATIONSHIP BETWEEN THE MARKET-TO-
16         BOOK RATIO AND RETURNS?
17   A Yes. Mr. Coyne has acknowledged that a market-to-book ratio greater than 1.0 necessarily
18         implies that investors’ expected return, i.e., the cost of equity, is less than their authorized
19         return. In a recent cost of capital proceeding in California, Mr. Coyne testified to the following
20         principles:
21                •      “The terms ‘expected return to investors’ and ‘cost of capital’ are essentially
22                       the same.” 23
23                •      “The allowed rate of return should equal the cost of capital,” 24 the NARUC
24                       fairness principle.
25                •      “A typical [investor] expectation is the utility will earn its allowed return
26                       [on book equity].” 25 Mr. Coyne’s Expected Earnings Analysis is predicated
27                       on this assumption, 26 and the Value Line reports from which the EEA draws

14                   necessarily implies a lower return.” 32

15           The logical conclusions from Mr. Coyne’s testimony are that (1) investors’ expected
16   returns on book equity are based on authorized ROEs (as can be seen in Figure 5, which shows the
17   Value Line ROE data used in Mr. Coyne’s EEA; the averages, which range from 10.0% to 10.7%,
18   are in line with the national average ROE authorized for electric utilities in 2020-22, 9.4% 33); (2)
19   investors pay more than book value when they invest in utilities; (3) investors’ expected return on
20   their investment, i.e., the cost of equity, must be less than the authorized ROE; and (4) according
21   to NARUC’s fairness principle, under which the ROE should be set equal to the COE, historical,
22   current, and forecast ROEs, because they systematically exceed the cost of equity, can provide no
23   basis for determining the appropriate authorized ROE.

 1                      3.      Authorized ROEs and interest rates have diverged without a
 2                              corresponding increase in utilities’ risk profile.

 3   Q. WHY DO REGULATORS APPROVE AUTHORIZED ROES IN EXCESS OF
 4         UTILITIES’ ACTUAL COST OF EQUITY?
 5   A. A mathematical model called the Pólya urn can provide insight into why regulators have
 6         continued to approve authorized ROEs in excess of utilities’ actual cost of capital. 34 Historical
 7         return on equity decisions can be thought of as balls in an urn. To decide on a new case, the
 8         regulator draws a ball from the urn. The ball is then replaced, along with a new ball –
 9         representing the current ROE decision – with the same value. This process of sampling-with-
10         replacement-plus-duplication has a self-reinforcing property sometimes called the rich-get-
11         richer or Matthew effect.
12             Of course, this model is over-simplified because regulators look at other information
13         besides past authorized ROEs. The basic model can be modified to include additional balls in
14         the urn representing new information, such as the estimated current cost of equity. Nonetheless,
15         as long as regulators look at, much less rely on, past ROEs, changes in authorized ROEs will
16         lag changes in the current true cost of equity.
17             The basic utility regulatory model and risk profile have not changed significantly for
18         decades, so the utility equity risk premium – the spread of the cost of capital over risk-free
19         government interest rates – has remained stable. The Pólya urn model predicts that, in a market
20         in which interest rates and, assuming a relatively stable utility equity risk premium, utilities’
21         cost of equity have been trending downward for decades, authorized ROEs will consistently
22         exceed the actual cost of equity, and the spread will widen over time.
23             The data confirm the Pólya urn model’s prediction of such a widening spread between
24         authorized ROEs and the actual cost of equity. Figure 6 shows the quarterly average authorized
25         ROE for all U.S. utilities, the 30-year Treasury rate, and their difference. While interest rates
26         have declined steadily since the mid-1980s, authorized ROEs have not kept pace. As a result,
27         the ROE-Treasury spread has more than tripled, from approximately 2.3% in the early 1980s
28         to 7.1% in the two years through September 2022. It can be estimated from the Pólya urn model


      34
           See, e.g., Learning Machines, The Polya Urn Model: A simple simulation of “The Rich get Richer” (Sept. 7,
           2021) https://blog.ephorie.de/the-polya-urn-model-a-simple-simulation-of-the-rich-get-richer.

                                   Mark E. Ellis Direct Testimony, on behalf of GIPL
                                               GPSC Docket No. 44280
                                                        Page 19

                                  PUBLIC DISCLOSURE VERSION
                                           (October 21, 2022 Errata)
1        described above that, even under conservative assumptions, regulators, on average, assign no
2        more than a 25% weight to the current cost of equity and at least 75% to recent ROEs. No
3        evidence suggests that utilities’ risk profile has systematically increased over this period, so
4        setting ROEs higher and higher relative to utilities’ actual cost of equity unnecessarily raises
5        rates and costs to customers.

2    III.       ROE AND CAPITAL STRUCTURE ARE INTERRELATED AND CANNOT BE
3               DETERMINED SEPARATELY.

 4              A.       ROE and capital structure are interrelated through ROE’s impact on cash
 5                       flow.

 6   Q. DOES THE CAPITAL STRUCTURE IMPACT CUSTOMER COSTS?
 7   A. Yes. Capital structure refers to the share of a utility’s investment that is funded by debt and
 8          equity. Because equity generally has a higher cost than debt, assuming no change in authorized
 9          ROE, a higher equity ratio tends to increase customer costs.
10   Q. DOES THE CAPITAL STRUCTURE IMPACT A UTILITY’S CREDIT QUALITY?
11   A. Yes. A primary determinant of a company’s credit quality – its anticipated ability to repay its
12          debts – is the amount of debt outstanding relative to the total amount of capital, both debt and
13          equity, invested in the company. In general, a higher equity ratio tends to improve a utility’s
14          credit quality. Equity ratio is not the only determinant of credit quality, though. As will be
15          explained in more detail below, credit quality is also determined by the amount of cash
16          available to service the debt.


      37
            M. Ellis analysis of S&P GMI data (last accessed October 6, 2022).

 1   Q. PLEASE EXPLAIN THE RELATIONSHIP BETWEEN ROE AND CAPITAL
 2         STRUCTURE.
 3   A. The testimony of Georgia Power’s credit expert, Mr. Steven Fetter, reveals that ROE and
 4         capital structure are inextricably linked.
 5            Mr. Fetter describes the importance of the ratio of funds from operations (FFO) to debt in
 6         rating agencies’ assessments of utility credit quality. 38 FFO and similar cash flow metrics 39
 7         measure the cash available to pay debt interest and principal. What he fails to clarify in his
 8         written testimony is that net income is a key component of FFO. 40 Net income, in turn, is the
 9         product of rate base, equity ratio, and ROE. Consequently, ROE and equity ratio are key
10         determinants of FFO. During cross-examination, Mr. Fetter acknowledged the relationship
11         between capital structure, ROE, and credit quality:
12                        Q. [Ms. Whitfield]: On page 14, you talk about key quantitative
13                           measures. Specifically, you reference FFO to debt and debt
14                           to EBITDA as two core financial ratios that are considered
15                           by credit agencies.

16                        A. [Mr. Fetter]: Yes.

17                        Q. Do both of those ratios include inputs that are affected by
18                           both capital structure and the return on equity?

19                        A. Well, it certainly factors in the equity/debt split because in
20                           one, the debt divides from the bottom and in the other, the
21                           debt is divided into by the EBITDA. As far as whether ROE
22                           gets factored into this, FFO – ROE ends up resulting in
23                           revenues and FFO is a certain measure and EBITDA is an
24                           earnings measure. And so ROE would impact those two
25                           items. 41
26            In the context of the regulatory objective of setting a capital structure that appropriately
27         balances customer and investor interests, as ROE increases, the amount of debt in the capital

      38

1          structure can also increase while still maintaining the utility’s credit quality; similarly, as the
2          ROE declines, the equity ratio would need to increase to maintain the same creditworthiness.
3              Curiously, Mr. Coyne’s capital structure analysis makes no reference whatsoever to any
 4         credit metrics. Instead, he bases his equity ratio recommendation, 56%, solely on a peer
5          comparison. 42
6    Q. HOW SHOULD A UTILITY’S CAPITAL STRUCTURE BE DETERMINED?
7    A. The appropriate capital structure can be determined more rigorously by using the analytical
8          methods and metrics employed by credit rating agencies and as described by Mr. Fetter. The
9          level of debt that can be accommodated in the capital structure will vary with ROE, because
10         funds from operation (FFO), a key determinant of a utility’s creditworthiness, is based on net
11         income, and net income is based on ROE, for any given credit rating and its corresponding
12         FFO/debt ratio.
13             Figure 8 illustrates the relationships between equity ratio, ROE, and FFO/debt. The
14         horizontal axis is the equity ratio; the vertical axis is FFO/debt. The light orange horizontal
15         band represents the range of FFO/debt that corresponds to the utility’s desired credit rating.
16         The dark orange arcing lines correspond to different levels of authorized ROE, with increasing
17         line thickness representing increasing ROE. Holding the equity ratio constant, FFO/debt
18         declines as the ROE is reduced (moving down from a thicker ROE line to a thinner line). But
19         the decline in FFO/debt when ROE is reduced can be reversed by increasing the equity ratio
20         (moving along the thinner ROE line up and to the right). Any number of combinations of ROE
21         and equity ratio can meet the level of FFO/debt needed to maintain the utility’s credit rating.
22         A higher ROE requires less equity to maintain the same FFO/debt and credit rating; a lower
23         ROE can maintain the same FFO/debt and credit rating if it is paired with a higher equity ratio.

 2            Many observers see utilities’ healthy credit ratings and low cost of debt and conclude that
 3        the best way to reduce customer costs is to increase the amount of debt in the capital structure.
 4        As explained above, current authorized ROEs far exceed utilities’ actual cost of equity, so
 5        ample scope exists for the Commission to reduce Georgia Power’s ROE without adversely
 6        affecting its ability to raise equity. At typical utility credit ratings, savings from a lower ROE,
 7        after grossing up for taxes, generally more than make up for the incremental cost of any
 8        additional equity required in the capital structure. For example, based on analysis of data
 9        provided in Moody’s October 2021 Georgia Power credit opinion, 43 every 1.0% reduction in
10        ROE reduces total customer costs by approximately 2.0%, even after accounting for the
11        approximately 2.0% increase in equity ratio needed to maintain Georgia Power’s current cash
12        flow-to-debt ratio. 44
13            Total customer costs can be reduced by decreasing the ROE while increasing the equity
14        ratio to maintain the utility’s creditworthiness (i.e., its cash flow-to-debt) because the trade-off
15        is not one-for-one. Net income accounts for about 50% of GPC’s FFO; other items, such as

     43
        M. Ellis analysis of data provided in GPC data request response STF-NEC-1-1 Attachment AG: Moody’s
        Investors Service, Georgia Power Company: Update to credit analysis at 10; Ex. 9; Ex. 10 (October 13, 2021).
     44
        Moody’s preferred cash flow metric is cash flow from operations (CFO), not FFO, and the analysis is based on
        Moody’s CFO/debt data. The basic definition of CFO is net income + depreciation and amortization + changes
        in working capital, i.e., FFO + changes in working capital. See Corporate Finance Institute, How to Calculate
        FCFE from CFO? (Dec. 16, 2020) https://corporatefinanceinstitute.com/resources/knowledge/accounting/how-
        to-calculate-fcfe-from-cfo/.

                                                  Mark E. Ellis Direct Testimony, on behalf of GIPL
                                                              GPSC Docket No. 44280
                                                                       Page 25

                                   PUBLIC DISCLOSURE VERSION
                                             (October 21, 2022 Errata)
 1         depreciation and amortization, account for the rest. Consequently, a relatively large reduction
 2         in net income due to a sharp cut in ROE would reduce FFO by roughly half as much in
 3         percentage terms. In addition, ROE is grossed-up for taxes, which are not included in FFO, so
 4         the savings to customers from a lower ROE is amplified. Rather than “leverage up,” i.e., reduce
 5         the equity ratio and increase debt, at current ROEs to reduce rates, it is more cost-effective to
 6         use any spare credit capacity to reduce the utility’s ROE, or even to increase the equity ratio if
 7         necessary to maintain a target credit rating, than to increase debt.
 8            Reducing ROE and increasing the equity ratio has the additional benefit of reducing debt-
 9         to-capitalization, another key metric used by rating agencies to assess credit quality. 45

10            B.      Georgia Power’s capital structure proposal does not address the interaction
11                    between ROE and capital structure.

12   Q. DOES GEORGIA POWER’S CAPITAL STRUCTURE PROPOSAL TAKE INTO
13         ACCOUNT THE INTERACTION BETWEEN ROE AND CAPITAL STRUCTURE?
14   A. As Mr. Coyne acknowledged during cross examination, he developed his capital structure
15         recommendation without incorporating the interrelationship between credit quality, ROE, and
16         equity ratio. 46 Neither Mr. Coyne nor Mr. Fetter provides any analysis or calculations
17         demonstrating how FFO/debt interacts with ROE and how that interaction influences GPC’s
18         proposed equity ratio. As a result, how much GPC’s proposed ROE and/or the equity ratio
19         could be reduced to lower customer costs while still maintaining its desired investment-grade
20         credit rating cannot be assessed from the testimony and data GPC has provided.
21            GPC’s analysis should have included a detailed analysis of the relationships between ROE,
22         equity ratio, and creditworthiness and the impact of different combinations of equity ratio and
23         ROE on customers, lenders, and shareholders so that the Commission could examine the ability
24         of Georgia Power to obtain sufficient capital while minimizing customer costs. The
25         Commission should require Georgia Power to provide a detailed analysis of the relationships
26         between ROE, equity ratio, and creditworthiness so that the Commission possesses the facts it
27         needs to come to fact-based conclusions about Georgia Power’s authorized capital structure.

 1   IV.       MR. COYNE EMPLOYS FLAWED MODELS AND ASSUMPTIONS THAT
 2             SYSTEMATICALLY PRODUCE UPWARDLY BIASED ROE ESTIMATES FOR
 3             GEORGIA POWER.

 4             A.      Mr. Coyne’s methodology relies, in part, on conceptually flawed models.

 5   Q. HOW DOES MR. COYNE ESTIMATE GEORGIA POWER’S ROE?
 6   A. Mr. Coyne employs four different models to estimate Georgia Power’s ROE: (1) constant-
 7         growth discounted cash flow model (CG DCF); (2) capital asset pricing model (CAPM); (3)
 8         risk premium analysis (RPA); and (4) expected earnings analysis (EEA). In the CG DCF,
 9         CAPM, and EEA, Mr. Coyne estimates ROEs for a number of proxy group companies and
10         bases his recommended ROEs on the proxy group average.
11   Q. WHAT IS YOUR OVERALL ASSESSMENT OF MR. COYNE’S APPROACH?
12   A. Although two of Mr. Coyne’s models, the CG DCF and CAPM, are widely used by financial
13         professionals and utility cost of capital experts to estimate the cost of equity, his
14         implementations are deeply flawed. They rely on unrealistic, systematically upwardly biased
15         assumptions that invalidate their results. I describe these deficiencies in more detail in Sections
16         IV.C and IV.E below.
17             The other two models, RPA and EEA, while frequently used by utility cost of capital
18         experts, are not commonly used elsewhere in finance outside utility regulatory proceedings,
19         and both suffer a severe, invalidating conceptual flaw.
20   Q. WHAT IS THE INVALIDATING CONCEPTUAL FLAW IN THE RPA AND EEA?
21   A. Both the RPA and EEA confuse the cost of equity and the return on equity, estimating the cost
22         of equity from historic or forecast returns on equity. The RPA consists of a linear regression
23         model of historical quarterly-average authorized ROEs against historical quarterly-average 30-
24         year Treasury yield. Mr. Coyne’s EEA estimates the cost of equity using ROE forecasts
25         provided by investment research firm Value Line. The primary drivers of Value Line’s ROE
26         forecasts for the proxy group companies, in turn, are the companies’ most recent authorized
27         ROEs. 48
28             Both the RPA and EEA are essentially models of past ROE decisions, not the actual cost
29         of equity. By design, therefore, they repeat the historic overearnings arising from the excess

      48
           See, e.g., GPC data request response STF-BAI-1-1 Attachment Coyne F at 5: Value Line, “American Elec.
           Pwr.” (March 11, 2022), fn. E.

 1        ROEs authorized in the past. As explained in Section II.A above, the cost of equity and the
 2        return on equity are two entirely different concepts; there is no basis to assume that they will
 3        or should be equal. In fact, as explained in Section II.B above, authorized ROEs have diverged
 4        dramatically from utilities’ actual cost of equity, as reflected in the disparity between
 5        authorized ROEs and substantially lower forecast returns for the U.S. equity market as a whole,
 6        despite the latter’s higher risk; utility market-to-book ratios exceeding 1.0 for decades; and the
 7        growing divergence between average authorized ROEs and interest rates. Basing a utility’s
 8        authorized ROE on historically authorized ROEs without any reference to the actual cost of
 9        equity, as both the RPA and EEA do, merely perpetuates these errors.
10           A simple calculation illustrates the EEA’s conceptual flaw in equating ROE and COE.
11        ROE is the ratio of earnings to the book value of equity. But investors cannot buy shares at
12        book value; they must pay market value. The market value of the stocks Mr. Coyne chose to
13        include in his peer group tend to trade at a significant premium over book value, currently an
14        average of 2.1x. Mathematically, if investors pay more than book value for the same stream of
15        earnings, their expected return, i.e., the cost of equity, must be less than the ROE calculated
16        using the book value. 49 For companies earning just their cost of equity, the expected return,
17        i.e., the cost of equity, can be estimated by dividing earnings per share (EPS) by equity book
18        value per share (BVPS). 50 EPS can be expressed as the product of ROE and book value per
19        share, so COE can similarly be expressed as ROE divided by the market-to-book ratio. 51 COEs
20        so calculated are dramatically lower than Mr. Coyne’s EEA estimates. For example, Value
21        Line’s forecast ’25-‘27 ROE for Alliant Energy Corporation is 11.0%, 52 and as of September
22        30, 2022, Alliant’s market-to-book ratio was 2.17, 53 so investors’ expected return would be


     49
        A simple example illustrates why this must be true. Suppose one pays $1 for an investment that guarantees a
        payment of $0.10 – a return of $0.010/$1.00 = 10% – every year into perpetuity. If instead the initial cost was
        $2 instead of $1 for the same perpetual stream of $0.10 per year, the return would be $0.10/$2.00 = 5%. If the
        initial investment for the identical cash flow stream is higher, the return is lower.
     50
        Aswath Damodaran, Implied Equity Risk Premium: Principles & Mechanics at 5
        https://pages.stern.nyu.edu/~adamodar/pdfiles/eqnotes/webcasts/ERP/ImpliedERP.pdf (last accessed July 22,
        2022).
     51
        COE = EPS / stock price = ROE x book value per share / stock price = ROE / M/B.

 1        only approximately 11.0/2.17 = 5.07%. 54 As Kahn explained, and Mr. Coyne has
 2        acknowledged (see Section II.B.2 above), M/B ratios greater than 1.0 imply COE is less than
 3        ROE. It is simply mathematically impossible for COE to equal ROE when M/B is not equal to
 4        1.0, yet Mr. Coyne’s RPA and EEA are nonetheless premised on this mathematical
 5        impossibility.
 6           FERC has also recognized the flaws in the RPA and EEA. In Opinion No. 569 (November
 7        2019), FERC rejected the use of both the RPA and EEA to estimate the cost of equity. With
 8        regard to the EEA, FERC explained “that relying on the Expected Earnings model would not
 9        satisfy the requirements of Hope”:

10                         In particular, we find that the record does not support departing
11                         from our traditional use of market-based approaches to
12                         determine base ROE. Under the market-based approach, the
13                         Commission sets a utility’s ROE to equal the estimated return
14                         that investors would require in order to purchase stock in the
15                         utility at its current market price. In Hope, the Supreme Court
16                         explained that “the return to the equity owner should be
17                         commensurate with returns on investments in other enterprises
18                         having corresponding risks.” In order to determine this, we must
19                         analyze the returns that are earned on “investments in other
20                         enterprises having corresponding risks,” but investors cannot
21                         invest in an enterprise at book value and must instead pay the
22                         prevailing market price for an enterprise’s equity. As a result,
23                         the expected return on a utility’s book value does not reflect
24                         “returns on investments in other enterprises” because book value
25                         does not reflect the value of any investment that is available to
26                         an investor in the market, outside of the unlikely situation in
27                         which market value and book value are exactly equal.
28                         Accordingly, we find that relying on the Expected Earnings
29                         model would not satisfy the requirements of Hope. 55

 1                        [T]he Risk Premium model is likely to provide a less accurate
 2                        current cost of equity estimate than the DCF model or CAPM
 3                        because it relies on previous ROE determinations, whose
 4                        resulting ROE may not necessarily be directly determined by a
 5                        market-based method, whereas the DCF and CAPM methods
 6                        apply a market-based method to primary data. For example,
 7                        previous ROE determinations may not involve an explicit
 8                        determination as to whether an ROE is just and reasonable, but
 9                        instead focused on whether to allow an ROE incentive adder or
10                        were approving a preexisting RTO-wide ROE for a new RTO
11                        member. Similarly, many previous ROE determinations used in
12                        the Risk Premium model were the product of rate case
13                        settlements. Such settlements often involve compromises on a
14                        variety of issues present in a rate case, of which the appropriate
15                        ROE is only one. Consequently, such settlements could include
16                        ROEs that are not representative of the market cost of equity
17                        because the ROEs were negotiated above or below that market
18                        cost of equity in order to form an overall settlement package,
19                        together with negotiated outcomes on other issues, that were
20                        acceptable to the parties. 56

 1                        Q. Isn’t it true that two of your models, the risk premium
 2                           analysis, RPA, and the expected earnings analysis, EEA, are

 5                        Q. The RPA is based on historical authorized ROEs; correct?
 6                        A. It’s based on an historic relationship between allowed return
 7                           by regulatory commissions and Treasury yields.
 8                        Q. The EEA is based on forecast ROEs; correct?
 9                        A. Yes. That’s based on a forecast of ROEs. 58

10            Despite his acknowledgement that ROE systematically exceeds COE, Mr. Coyne’s RPA
11         and EEA nonetheless estimate COE based on historical and forecast ROEs – in direct
12         contradiction to the NARUC fairness principle that he has endorsed. For these reasons, the
13         results of Mr. Coyne’s EEA and RPA models should be rejected outright.

14            B.      Mr. Coyne fails to account for differences in equity ratios among the proxy
15                    group members.

16   Q. HOW DOES THE EQUITY RATIO AFFECT THE COST OF EQUITY?
17   A. All else equal, a lower equity ratio tends to raise the cost of equity. This can be understood
18         intuitively. The cash generated by a business is pledged to holders of its debt and equity, with
19         debtholders having first priority. As the equity ratio declines, a smaller share of the cash goes
20         to equity owners. To the extent there is uncertainty in the cash generated, it is amplified by a
21         lower equity ratio, increasing the riskiness of those cash flows. This increased risk is reflected
22         in a higher cost of equity.
23            The companies in Mr. Coyne’s proxy group have different equity ratios, and their average,
24         53%, 59 is different from the 56% he proposes for Georgia Power, so their costs of equity cannot
25         be used to estimate Georgia Power’s under his proposed equity ratio. In addition, Mr. Coyne
26         compares the equity ratios on a book basis; this is incorrect. When determining the cost of
27         capital, the market value of equity should be used. 60

         (September 12, 2022) [edited for clarity].

         possible, you should estimate market values of the elements of the current capital structure” (emphasis added).

 4          C.      Mr. Coyne’s DCF model uses upwardly biased dividend yield calculations and
 5                  unrealistically extrapolates analyst earnings growth forecasts into perpetuity,
 6                  producing economically impossible results.

 7   Q. WHAT IS YOUR ASSESSMENT OF MR. COYNE’S DCF MODEL?
 8   A. Mr. Coyne uses the constant-growth version of the DCF model (CG DCF) in his analysis, with
 9      a growth rate equal to equity analysts’ consensus 3-to-5-year EPS growth rate estimates. The
10      key shortcoming in his implementation of the CG DCF is the assumption that dividends can
11      grow at analysts’ estimated EPS growth rates into perpetuity. This economically impossible
12      assumption upwardly biases his results dramatically.
13   Q. PLEASE DESCRIBE THE CONSTANT-GROWTH DCF MODEL.
14   A. The constant-growth DCF is based on the well-known and widely used mathematical formula
15      for the value of a growing perpetuity stream of cash flows. It assumes a single, constant rate
16      of cash flow growth. In the constant-growth DCF model used by Mr. Coyne and many other
17      modelers, the cash flows are expected dividends, and the perpetuity value formula can be
18      expressed as:
                                                               (1 + 𝑔𝑔)
19                                                 𝑀𝑀0 = 𝐷𝐷0
                                                               (𝑘𝑘 − 𝑔𝑔)
20      where 𝑀𝑀0 refers to the current market value (stock price), 𝐷𝐷0 , the current dividend (typically
21      four times the most recent quarterly payment), 𝑔𝑔, the forecast perpetuity growth rate, and 𝑘𝑘,
22      the cost of equity. Rearranging terms, the cost of equity can be expressed as a function of the
                            𝑀𝑀
23      dividend yield, 𝑑𝑑 ( 𝐷𝐷0 ), and growth rate:
                              0

24                                                𝑘𝑘 = 𝑑𝑑(1 + 𝑔𝑔) + 𝑔𝑔
25          In some implementations of the CG DCF, the first-year dividend yield is calculated by
                                                   𝑔𝑔
26      multiplying the current yield by 1 + 2 , instead of 1 + 𝑔𝑔, to account for the quarterly, not


                                  Mark E. Ellis Direct Testimony, on behalf of GIPL
                                              GPSC Docket No. 44280
                                                       Page 33

                                   PUBLIC DISCLOSURE VERSION
                                            (October 21, 2022 Errata)
 1        annual, payment of dividends. Mr. Coyne uses this approach. 61 Typically, the cost of equity is
 2        estimated for each member of the proxy group, with the mean or median reflecting the cost of
 3        equity for the target company. Mr. Coyne uses the mean.
 4            The general DCF model, which can allow for varying growth rates over time, is a
 5        particularly apt representation of stock returns because its assumptions realistically reflect
 6        several key features of share prices and expected returns. First, the DCF model’s perpetual
 7        cash flow stream assumption mirrors equity’s claim on a firm’s cash flows into perpetuity.
 8        Second, the assumption of steady growth in dividends over time reasonably reflects their much
 9        greater stability relative to other potential measures of profitability, like earnings or cash flow.
10        Third, the resulting single discount rate into perpetuity is consistent with the no-arbitrage
11        principle of finance. If investors expected higher (lower) returns in the future, they would
12        impute that into the price today and bid up (down) the price accordingly, such that near-term
13        and long-term returns roughly equilibrate. 62
14            But the constant growth version of the DCF used by Mr. Coyne is not well-suited for
15        estimating the cost of equity for a utility or any other stock, for two reasons. First, it is not
16        realistic to assume that a utility will maintain its current 3-to-5-year growth rate into perpetuity.
17        At any given time, the 3-to-5-year growth rate will deviate from its long-term trend due to any
18        number of factors, such as weather; economic conditions; new capital projects; regulatory, tax,
19        and other policy changes; and unforeseen events like the Covid-19 pandemic. Second, the
20        results of the CG DCF are particularly sensitive to the perpetuity growth rate assumption. The
21        inaccuracy introduced by assuming a relatively short-term growth rate will be sustained forever
22        invalidates the results of Mr. Coyne’s CG DCF. Mr. Coyne’s use of analyst estimates, a source

        dividend yields as of the starting time period of the model (𝑡𝑡0 ), the common method of using four times the
        most recent quarterly dividend is already slightly conservative relative to a more accurate quarterly model, and
        this adjustment is therefore not necessary.

        Some equity return projections vary with forecast horizon, generally due to a valuation-reversion assumption in

        and remaining at that level afterward. See, e.g., BlackRock Investment Institute, Capital market assumptions
        (2022) https://www.blackrock.com/institutions/en-us/insights/charts/capital-market-assumptions. Whether
        variation in expected equity returns across different forecast horizons can be estimated with any accuracy is a
        subject of ongoing debate among academic and investment professionals. Some forecasters assume no mean
        reversion in their return forecasts. See, e.g., AQR Capital Management, 2014 Capital Market Assumptions for
        Major Asset Classes (2014) https://www.aqr.com/Insights/Research/Alternative-Thinking/2014-Capital-Market-

 1         widely known to be upwardly biased, for his growth rate assumption further invalidates his

 3                    1.      Mr. Coyne’s dividend yield calculations use unnecessarily long trailing
 4                            price histories, introducing upward bias to his estimates.

 5   Q. HOW DOES MR. COYNE CALCULATE THE CURRENT DIVIDEND YIELD?
 6   A. To calculate the dividend yield, Mr. Coyne divides four times the most recent quarterly
 7         dividend by the trailing average share price over 30, 90, and 180 trading days. 63
 8             While it is advisable to use a multi-day average of the share price to reduce the effect of
 9         any day-to-day price fluctuations that are not reflective of investors’ long-term expectations,
10         all three of Mr. Coyne’s averages are unnecessarily long. Because share prices have a general
11         tendency to trend upward over time, the longer the backward-looking averaging period, the
12         lower the share price will tend to be, introducing upward bias into the dividend yield.
13         Averaging the most recent month of data, approximately 21 trading days, better balances the
14         competing objectives of mitigating the potential short-term volatility cited by Mr. Coyne and
15         reflecting current investor expectations.

16                    2.      Mr. Coyne’s perpetuity growth rate is based on analysts’ 3-to-5-year
17                            growth rate forecasts, producing economically impossible results.

18   Q. HOW DOES MR. COYNE ESTIMATE EACH PEER UTILITY’S PERPETUITY
19         GROWTH RATE?
20   A. While estimating the current dividend yield is fairly straightforward (although, as just
21         explained, the potential for bias exists even there), estimating the perpetuity growth rate is
22         more subjective. Mr. Coyne uses analysts’ consensus 3-to-5-year estimated earnings-per-share
23         (EPS) growth rate provided by Value Line, Thomson First Call (as reported by Yahoo!
24         Finance), 64 and Zacks. 65

 1            The DCF is a model of dividends, not earnings, so it would be preferable to use explicit
 2         dividend-per-share (DPS) forecasts. While some analysts, such as Value Line, provide DPS
 3         forecasts, they are less common, so EPS forecast are typically used instead.
 4   Q. IS IT REASONABLE TO ASSUME ANALYSTS’ CONSENSUS GROWTH RATES
 5         INTO PERPETUITY IN A DCF MODEL?
 6   A. No. There are several problems with using analysts’ estimates for the perpetuity growth rate.
 7         A wealth of academic research over decades has found that analyst forecasts tend to be
 8         optimistic. 66 Several other observations and analyses demonstrate the unreasonableness of
 9         using analysts’ 3-to-5-year EPS growth rate estimates for the perpetuity dividend growth rate
10         in the constant-growth DCF model.
11   Q. WHAT IS YOUR FIRST OBSERVATION OR ANALYSIS THAT DEMONSTRATES
12         THE UNREASONABLENESS OF USING ANALYSTS’ 3-TO-5-YEAR EPS GROWTH
13         RATE ESTIMATES FOR THE PERPETUITY DIVIDEND GROWTH RATE IN THE
14         CG DCF?
15   A. It is economically impossible for analyst earnings growth forecasts to be sustained even for
16         one decade, much less into perpetuity. Figure 10 compares the forecast aggregate earnings of
17         the U.S. publicly-traded companies for which analysts provide EPS growth forecasts 67 to
18         forecast U.S. GDP. 68 Currently, these companies’ combined earnings are equal to roughly 8%
19         of U.S. GDP. Yet if analysts’ growth projections were correct, they would exceed total U.S.
20         GDP in just six years.

         See, e.g., Marc Goedhart, Rishi Raj, Abjishek Saxena, Equity analysts: Still too bullish, McKinsey Quarterly
         (Apr. 2010), https://www mckinsey.com/business-functions/strategy-and-corporate-finance/our-insights/equity-
         analysts-still-too-bullish. For a more recent example, see also Stefano Cassella, Benjamin Golez, Huseyin
         Gulen, Peter Kelly, Horizon Bias and the Term Structure of Equity Returns (Nov. 2021),
         https://ssrn.com/abstract=3328970.

 4                                   Consensus long-term earnings growth estimates routinely
 5                                   exceed sustainable GDP growth. The current consensus growth
 6                                   rate for earnings on the S&P 500, according to the Zacks

 1   Q. WHAT            IS    YOUR         SECOND       OBSERVATION            OR       ANALYSIS   THAT
 2         DEMONSTRATES THE UNREASONABLENESS OF USING ANALYSTS’ 3-TO-5-
 3         YEAR EPS GROWTH RATE ESTIMATES FOR THE PERPETUITY DIVIDEND
 4         GROWTH RATE IN THE CG DCF?
 5   A. Mr. Coyne is correct that “[o]ver the long term … dividend growth can only be sustained by
 6         earnings growth,” 71 and EPS and DPS do tend to have similar growth rates over extended,
 7         multi-decade periods of time. Nonetheless, EPS growth rates are poor proxies for DPS growth
 8         over the 3-to-5-year horizon of analysts’ EPS forecasts. This is observed in both forecast and
 9         historical data.
10             Few analysts provide both DPS and EPS projections, but Value Line, one source of Mr.
11         Coyne’s growth estimates, does provide both EPS and DPS growth forecasts, and its EPS and
12         DPS projections vary considerably. Figure 11 compares Value Line’s forecast EPS and DPS
13         growth rates for the members of Mr. Coyne’s proxy group. On average, the DPS growth rate
14         is lower, 5.5% vs 6.1% for EPS; EPS overestimates expected dividend growth, introducing
15         upward bias into Mr. Coyne’s DCF results. More importantly, as shown in Figure 11, a cross-
16         plot of Value Line’s EPS and DPS growth rate estimates, the correlation between the two sets
17         of numbers is low (R2 coefficient of 0.23).

 2           In both the historical and projected data, the average growth rate for EPS is higher than for
 3        DPS, seemingly contradicting Mr. Coyne’s assertion that “earnings per share [and] dividends
 4        per share … grow at the same constant rate.” 74 But EPS and DPS do, in fact, grow at roughly
 5        the same compound rate over the long term. 75 Nonetheless, analysts’ 3-to-5-year forecast
 6        horizons are not long-term, and simply because earnings are more volatile than dividends, the
 7        figure reported for EPS growth over analysts’ 3-to-5-year forecast period will tend to be higher
 8        than DPS, as both the historical growth rates in Figure 12 and the forecast growth rates in
 9        Figure 11 demonstrate.
10           This is due to the mathematical relationship between compound, or geometric, and
11        arithmetic average growth rates. 76 An illustrative example demonstrates the principle. Suppose
12        both EPS and DPS both grow at a compound average growth rate (CAGR) of 5% over 25
13        years, but they take different paths to get there. DPS grows at the same 5% every year, or

     73
        M. Ellis analysis of data from S&P Dow Jones Indices, S&P 500 Earning and Estimate Report (September 30,
        2022) https://www.spglobal.com/spdji/en/documents/additional-material/sp-500-eps-est.xlsx.
     74
        2022 Rate Case Hr’g Tr. Vol. 1 at 654.
     75
        “Compound” refers to the single, constant growth rate at which an initial value would need to grow to reach a
        final value. Importantly, the initial value could follow any number of different growth paths to achieve the same
        compound growth rate. EPS and DPS can have the same compound growth rate over an extended, multi-decade
        period, but have very different growth rates over the intervening 5-year periods.
     76
        See, e.g., Educba, Difference Between Geometric vs. Arithmetic Mean, https://www.educba.com/geometric-
        mean-vs-arithmetic-mean/ (last accessed Oct. 20, 2022).

                                    Mark E. Ellis Direct Testimony, on behalf of GIPL
                                                GPSC Docket No. 44280
                                                         Page 40

                                       PUBLIC DISCLOSURE VERSION
                                               (October 21, 2022 Errata)
 1         27.6% over each 5-year period. Annual EPS growth varies between -5% and +15% over each
 2         5-year period, say 15% in the first 5 years, followed by -5%, 0%, 6%, and 10% over the next
 3         four 5-year periods. The average of the DPS growth rates over each of the 5-year periods is
 4         5.0%, but the average of the EPS growth rates is 5.2%. The effect is relatively small, but it is
 5         another source of upward bias introduced by using EPS growth rates as a proxy for DPS
 6         growth.
 7             For the two reasons observed in both forecast and historical data – overestimation on
 8         average and low correlation – EPS is a poor proxy for DPS, and it is inappropriate to assume
 9         that analysts’ relatively short-term EPS growth estimates reasonably reflect investors’ DPS
10         growth expectations into perpetuity.
11   Q. WHAT IS YOUR THIRD OBSERVATION OR ANALYSIS THAT DEMONSTRATES
12         THE UNREASONABLENESS OF USING ANALYSTS’ 3-TO-5-YEAR EPS GROWTH
13         RATE ESTIMATES FOR THE PERPETUITY DIVIDEND GROWTH RATE IN THE
14         CG DCF?
15   A. EPS growth rate horizons are likely not compatible with the CG DCF’s forecast horizon. The
16         starting time periods of analysts’ estimates are not specified with precision. S&P explains of
17         its estimates: 77

18                             Long Term Growth Rate (LTG) is a compound annual growth
19                             rate based on current and projected EPS values provided directly
20                             by the analysts. … Most analysts define LTG as an estimated
21                             average rate of earnings growth for the next 3-5 years. The exact
22                             time frame differs from broker to broker. Since the analysts
23                             providing LTG may differ from the analysts providing fiscal
24                             year estimates and the variation in time periods of 3-5 years, it
25                             is not possible to reconcile LTG with fiscal year estimates.


26         The starting points for Yahoo! Finance’s estimates are similarly unknown: 78

 1                       [A]s most analysts do not provide the basis of the calculation of
 2                       their growth rates, the estimates collected are assumed to include
 3                       a combination of past and future years with at least one future
 4                       period included, and are calculated on a compounded annual
 5                       growth rate (CAGR) basis.


 6        Value Line does specify the starting point and forecast horizon of its estimates. Nonetheless,
 7        Value Line’s growth rate forecast horizons are virtually certain not to be consistent with the
 8        dividend yield used in the CG DCF, i.e., the end of the last trading day of the share price
 9        averaging period. The starting period for the Value Line growth estimates used by Mr. Coyne,
10        for example, is ’19-’21, the midpoint of which, June 2020, is almost three years before the first
11        dividend payment in his DCF model. 79
12           EPS can vary significantly from one year to the next, typically much more than the annual
13        variation in DPS. Without knowing the forecast period, it is not possible to determine whether
14        the estimate reflects the growth rate over the three to five years, much less the rate into
15        perpetuity, from the starting point of the CG DCF. Following a year of poor performance, for
16        example, expected growth would be elevated, potentially significantly above what could be
17        sustained long-term.
18           The incompatibility of the growth estimates can be clearly observed in the data itself. For
19        example, Value Line’s growth rate for Edison International is reported as “not meaningful”
20        because the company reported a loss in 2018, the first year of its growth forecast period.
21        Yahoo! Finance’s and Zacks’s growth rates are both positive, presumably because the starting
22        point of their growth rate forecasts are years in which Edison International reported a profit. 80

 1   Q. WHAT            IS   YOUR        FOURTH           OBSERVATION                OR      ANALYSIS           THAT
 2         DEMONSTRATES THE UNREASONABLENESS OF USING ANALYSTS’ 3-TO-5-
 3         YEAR EPS GROWTH RATE ESTIMATES FOR THE PERPETUITY DIVIDEND
 4         GROWTH RATE IN THE CG DCF?
 5   A. The expected returns produced by a CG DCF model assuming DPS grows into perpetuity at
 6         analysts’ 3-to-5-year EPS growth rates are inconsistent with analysts’ own expected return
 7         forecasts.
 8            In addition to their EPS and DPS growth rates, Value Line publishes a variety of other
 9         forecasts, including for share prices. 81 These forecasts can be used to estimate Value Line’s
10         own expected return for each company, which can be compared to the CG DCF results using
11         Value Line’s dividend yield and EPS growth rate forecasts in Mr. Coyne’s model. 82 Figure 13
12         compares the results of the two models. The horizontal axis is the COE estimated using Mr.
13         Coyne CG DCF and Value Line’s yield and EPS growth rate assumptions; the vertical axis is
14         COE implied by Value Line’s own dividend and price forecasts.
15            On average, the CG DCF COE estimates (9.6%) are significantly higher than the COE
16         implied by Value Line’s price and dividend forecast (8.1%). More importantly, there is
17         virtually no correlation between the two sets of model results (R2 = 0.0). A CG DCF COE
18         based on Value Line’s dividend yield and EPS growth bears almost no resemblance to the COE
19         implied in its own dividend and price forecast. The analysts that provide 3-to-5-year EPS
20         growth forecasts clearly do not assume those rates apply to dividends or will be sustained into
21         perpetuity, as Mr. Coyne does. For Mr. Coyne’s proxy group, Value Line assumes DPS will
22         grow more slowly, on average, than EPS beyond its forecast horizon.

 2   Q. WHAT IS YOUR FIFTH OBSERVATION OR ANALYSIS THAT DEMONSTRATES
 3         THE UNREASONABLENESS OF USING ANALYSTS’ 3-TO-5-YEAR EPS GROWTH
 4         RATE ESTIMATES FOR THE PERPETUITY DIVIDEND GROWTH RATE IN THE
 5         CG DCF?
 6   A. Analyst earnings (and, by assumption, dividend) growth forecasts tend to be higher than the
 7         companies’ long-term historical results, additional evidence that analysts’ 3-to-5-year growth
 8         forecasts are not sustainable into perpetuity and are therefore unreasonable assumptions in a
 9         CG DCF model.

11         (1992-2022) DPS compound annual growth rates (CAGR). On average, forecast rates are
12         approximately 4% higher, in both nominal (as reported) and real (inflation-adjusted) terms,
13         than the historical average. The ~4% difference between historical and forecast growth
14         highlights the unreasonableness of assuming analysts’ estimates into perpetuity. 84

 2   Q. GIVEN THE NUMEROUS SHORTCOMINGS OF ANALYSTS’ FORECASTS,
 3         SHOULD THEY BE USED AT ALL IN DCF MODELS?
 4   A. Discounted cash flow models can be a robust approach to estimating expected returns and are
 5         widely used throughout finance. The key shortcoming of the constant-growth version of the
 6         DCF model – assuming a relatively short-term growth rate into perpetuity – can be easily
 7         remedied by assuming that analysts’ estimated growth rates apply only for a limited period,
 8         after which they converge toward a market- or sector-average terminal growth rate in a multi-
 9         stage DCF model (MS DCF), which I describe in more detail in IV.D below. Despite the
10         various deficiencies in analysts’ estimates even in the short-term, they are viewed as the best
11         available estimates of near-term investor expectations. That said, relatively little weight should
12         be placed on them in estimating the cost of equity, and the MS DCF model can weight them
13         more appropriately.


      85
           M. Ellis analysis of S&P GMI and U.S. Bureau of Labor Statistics (BLS) data (last accessed October 2, 2022);
           Ex. JMC-4.

                               PUBLIC DISCLOSURE VERSION
                                       (October 21, 2022 Errata)
 1                 3.      Mr. Coyne’s flawed DCF results should be disregarded.

 2   Q. WHAT IS YOUR CONCLUSION REGARDING MR. COYNE’S DCF RESULTS?
 3   A. Mr. Coyne’s DCF model results should be disregarded completely. While, in principle, a
 4      constant-growth DCF model could be used to estimate the cost of equity, Mr. Coyne’s
 5      implementation is deeply flawed. Its dividend yield calculations use excessively long historical
 6      price data series, introducing upward bias into the results. More critically, Mr. Coyne’s DCF
 7      unrealistically assumes analysts’ EPS growth estimates are valid for forecasting dividends into
 8      perpetuity. Several analyses demonstrate the invalidity of this assumption: the economic
 9      impossibility of sustaining EPS growth forecasts even for one decade, much less into
10      perpetuity; the low correlation between analysts’ EPS and DPS growth forecasts; the unknown
11      starting period for analyst growth forecasts and therefore likely inconsistency with the DCF
12      model’s starting period; the inconsistency between the CG DCF results and analysts’ own
13      implied expected return estimates; and the wide disparity between analyst forecasts and
14      utilities’ long-term historical DPS growth rates.

15         D.      The Multi-Stage DCF should be used instead of the CG DCF because it allows
16                 more realistic cash flow projections, yielding more accurate results.

17                 1.      The multi-stage DCF model enhances the CG DCF by allowing
18                         different dividend growth rates over time.

19   Q. WHAT IS THE MULTI-STAGE DCF MODEL?
20   A. The multi-stage DCF model (MS DCF) enhances the CG DCF by allowing different dividend
21      growth rates over time. As we saw previously, analysts’ estimated 3-to-5-year growth rates are
22      too high to be sustained in perpetuity, and may be biased. But analyst estimates should not be
23      ignored completely. Analysts’ estimated 3-to-5 year growth rates provide useful information
24      about the relative expected growth across companies. Over the long term though, it is
25      reasonable to assume investors expect growth rates, in real terms, to revert to their long-term
26      historical trends. The MS DCF explicitly models different growth rates over time.
27         The MS DCF can incorporate any number of stages. For equity valuation, a three-stage
28      model is commonly used, in which the initial stage uses analysts’ estimates over their 3-to-5-
29      year forecast horizon, and the terminal stage uses the long-term real historical growth rate plus
30      current long-term inflation expectations. In between lies a transition phase, typically 5 to 15

                              Mark E. Ellis Direct Testimony, on behalf of GIPL
                                          GPSC Docket No. 44280
                                                   Page 46

                                          PUBLIC DISCLOSURE VERSION
                                                      (October 21, 2022 Errata)
 1         years, in which the growth rate is the simple average of the initial and terminal rates. The MS
 2         DCF model can be expressed as:
                         1+𝑔𝑔           1+𝑔𝑔    𝑡𝑡1          1+𝑔𝑔   𝑡𝑡1 1+𝑔𝑔             1+𝑔𝑔2 𝑡𝑡2         1+𝑔𝑔     𝑡𝑡1   1+𝑔𝑔   𝑡𝑡2 1+𝑔𝑔
 3              1 = 𝑑𝑑 𝑘𝑘−𝑔𝑔1 �1 − � 1+𝑘𝑘1� � + 𝑑𝑑 � 1+𝑘𝑘1�                 2
                                                                                �1 − �        � � + 𝑑𝑑 � 1+𝑘𝑘1� � 1+𝑘𝑘2�                3
                                1                                      𝑘𝑘−𝑔𝑔2            1+𝑘𝑘                                       𝑘𝑘−𝑔𝑔3

 4         where 𝑑𝑑 is the current dividend yield; 𝑔𝑔1 , 𝑔𝑔2 , and 𝑔𝑔3 are the initial, transition, and terminal
 5         growth rates, respectively (where 𝑔𝑔2 = �(1 + 𝑔𝑔1 )(1 + 𝑔𝑔3 ) − 1); 86 𝑡𝑡1 and 𝑡𝑡2 are the initial and
 6         transition stage durations; and 𝑘𝑘 is the cost of equity such that the equation is true. Substantial
 7         precedent exists for the MS DCF model, in both its two- and three-stage forms, in both
 8         corporate finance and regulatory contexts. 87
 9              In my implementation of the MS DCF, I assume an initial growth stage of three years – the
10         low end of analysts’ EPS growth rate forecast horizon, to mitigate the effect of their upward
11         bias – and a 10-year transition. To account for the quarterly distribution of dividends, I convert
12         the reported rates to quarterly and multiply the number of periods in the initial and transition
13         phases by 4. 88 The dividend yield is the most recent quarterly dividend divided by the average
14         price over December 2021.

15                        2.        The MS DCF’s initial growth rate can be estimated from analysts’ EPS
16                                  growth forecasts.

17   Q. HOW DO YOU ESTIMATE THE INITIAL GROWTH RATE FOR THE MS DCF?
18   A. I use an average of analysts’ EPS growth forecasts from S&P Global Market Intelligence (S&P
19         GMI), Yahoo! Finance, and Zacks.

20                        3.        The MS DCF’s terminal growth rate can be estimated from expected
21                                  inflation, based on utilities’ long-term historical dividend growth.

22   Q. HOW DO YOU ESTIMATE THE TERMINAL GROWTH RATE FOR THE MS DCF?
23   A. The terminal growth rate is intended to reflect a sector-wide dividend growth rate toward which
24         all stocks in the peer group are expected to converge over the long term. Figure 15 shows real

8             For the market as a whole, long-term real DPS growth has tracked GDP per capita, about
 9        1.9% per year. 90 At any given time, some sectors grow faster, some slower. The technology
10        and healthcare industries, for example, have sustained DPS growth rates higher than the market
11        average for decades. Utilities are a mature industry, though, and end-use demand for electricity,
12        gas, and water has grown more slowly than GDP for decades, so it is not unreasonable for
13        utility companies’ per-share dividend growth to lag the market as whole. The long-term track
14        record of essentially zero real dividend growth further highlights the unreasonableness of Mr.
15        Coyne’s assumption that analyst growth forecasts can be sustained into perpetuity.

 1   Q. HOW DO YOU ESTIMATE EXPECTED LONG-TERM INFLATION?
 2   A. For expected long-term inflation, I use Treasury-TIPS spreads. TIPS are Treasury Inflation-
 3         Protected Securities, which provide investors a return equivalent to inflation plus the quoted
 4         TIPS yield. The difference in yield between Treasurys and TIPS of equal maturity is a current
 5         measure of the market’s forward-looking inflation expectation over the life of the bonds.
 6             The MS DCF uses inflation for the terminal, not initial or transition, growth rate, so
 7         inflation into perpetuity is estimated at the end of the transition phase, not from today. I use
 8         the expected inflation, 𝑖𝑖𝑙𝑙𝑙𝑙 , rate over the period from 20 to 30 years from now, as implied by
 9         the difference in the 30-year and 20-year Treasury-TIPS spreads:

 2   Q. DO YOU USE THE MS DCF ELSEWHERE IN YOUR ANALYSIS?
 3   A. Yes. I use it as one of two methods to estimate the market risk premium for the CAPM.

 4             E.        Mr. Coyne makes unrealistic, upwardly biased assumptions for all three
 5                       inputs into his CAPM.

 6   Q. WHAT IS THE CAPITAL ASSET PRICING MODEL?
 7   A. Mr. Coyne’s analysis incorporates another well-known COE model, the capital asset pricing
 8         model (CAPM). It estimates the cost of equity, 𝑘𝑘, from the formula:
 9                                                  𝑘𝑘 = 𝑟𝑟𝑓𝑓 + 𝛽𝛽�𝑟𝑟𝑚𝑚 − 𝑟𝑟𝑓𝑓 �
10         where 𝑟𝑟𝑓𝑓 is the risk-free rate (typically a long-term U.S. Treasury), 𝑟𝑟𝑚𝑚 is the expected return
11         on the market, and 𝛽𝛽 is a measure of risk of the company in question relative to the market.
12         The market risk premium (MRP), the difference between the market return and the risk-free
13         rate, 𝑟𝑟𝑚𝑚 − 𝑟𝑟𝑓𝑓 , reflects the additional return investors require as compensation for taking on
14         equity market risk. The CAPM is a simple model of the fundamental financial risk-reward
15         trade-off: investors demand higher returns as risk increases.


      93
           S&P GMI; Refinitiv via Yahoo! Finance (https://finance.yahoo.com/); Zacks (https://www.zacks.com/) (last
           accessed October 2, 2022).

 1                  1.     The source of Mr. Coyne’s risk-free rate assumption has a long and
 2                         widely known track record of upwardly biased interest rate forecasts.

3    Q. WHAT RISK-FREE RATE DOES MR. COYNE USE IN HIS CAPM?
4    A. Mr. Coyne uses the 30-year Treasury (T30). Because the models estimate the expected return
5       on equity, which is a claim on cash flows into perpetuity, the longest-term rate available should
6       be used. The 30-year Treasury is the longest-term risk-free rate, so the T30 is an appropriate
7       term. Mr. Coyne calculates his CAPM COE estimates using both recent historical average and
8       forecast Treasury rates.
 9   Q. DO YOU HAVE ANY CONCERNS WITH MR. COYNE’S RISK-FREE RATE
10      ESTIMATES?
11   A. Yes, I have two concerns. First, as with the average price calculation for the dividend yield in
12      his DCF model, Mr. Coyne’s historical T30 averaging period, 30 days, is too long. Curiously,
13      Mr. Coyne provides no explanation for the use of different averaging periods in the two
14      different models. One month of trading data, typically 20-22 days, is sufficient. Nonetheless,
15      the difference in trailing averaging periods for interest rates generally does not introduce any
16      bias in the results because, unlike stock prices, which tend to exhibit an upward trend over
17      time, daily movements interest rates are random.
18   Q. WHAT IS YOUR SECOND CONCERN WITH MR. COYNE’S RISK-FREE RATE
19      ESTIMATES?
20   A. My second concern involves his use of a forecast rate. Using a forecast rate creates
21      inconsistencies with the time horizon of the DCF, which is estimated as of today (or, more
22      precisely, as of the end of the trailing price averaging period). The mathematical formula for
23      the present value of a periodic time series upon which the DCF is based discounts the stream
24      of future cash flows to a “time zero” one period before the first payment. The resulting discount
25      rate is as of that time zero. The first payment in the DCF model is typically assumed to occur
26      one time step from today; therefore, the rate determined by the DCF model is as of today.
27      Using an interest rate expected at some future date in the CAPM produces a COE as of that
28      future date, not today, and that COE is not directly comparable to the DCF’s COE.
29          Ignoring the consistency concern, even if we did want to use a forecast rate, in general,
30      commonly available interest rate forecasts are no better predictors of future interest rates than
31      the current market rate. Most importantly, though, I am particularly concerned about Mr.

 7            More intuitively, markets are forward-looking. If investors expect interest rates to rise,
 8        their expectations will be incorporated into current yields. Consider the alternative. Suppose
 9        an investor expects the yield on the 30-year Treasury to rise from its current ~3% to 4% over
10        the next six months. There is an inverse relationship between a bond’s value and a bond’s
11        yield; when the yield rises, the value falls, and vice versa. An investor who expects bond yields
12        to rise would not buy a bond today, because to do so would be to invest expecting a loss; better
13        not to buy the bond at all. But market participants do buy at the current 3%, implying that the

 4         experts. Reviewing the academic research, Roger Morin, author of the frequently cited
 5         practitioner text, New Regulatory Finance, concludes, “The literature suggests that on balance,
 6         the bond market is very efficient in that it is difficult to consistently forecast interest rates with

14                     2.      Mr. Coyne cherry-picks his beta calculation methodology, ignoring the
15                             wide variety of valid potential approaches and best practice for

17   Q. HOW DOES MR. COYNE ESTIMATE THE BETA IN HIS CAPM MODEL?
18   A. Mr. Coyne uses beta estimates from two different financial data providers, Value Line and
19         Bloomberg. He uses Value Line’s betas as reported. Mr. Coyne overrides Bloomberg’s default
20         settings and uses only a subset of the beta estimates Bloomberg provides, such that the
21         calculation methodology and resulting beta estimates he reports for Bloomberg are very similar
22         to Value Line’s.
23             Importantly, Mr. Coyne selects two nearly identical beta calculation methodologies among
24         the multitude of methodologies commonly used by practitioners and academics, specifically
25         the two that currently tend to produce the highest results.
26   Q. HOW DO BLOOMBERG AND VALUE LINE ESTIMATE THEIR BETAS?
27   A. Both Bloomberg and Value Line estimate beta from the slope of a linear regression model of
28         a stock’s returns against the returns of the market overall. Both calculate “raw” betas from a


     102
           ROGER A. MORIN, NEW REGULATORY FINANCE, PUB. UTIL. REPORTS at 172 (2006).
     103
           See, e.g., DANIEL KAHNEMAN, OLIVIER SIBONY, CASS SUNSTEIN, NOISE: A FLAW IN HUMAN JUDGMENT,

 1         regression of trailing weekly, price-only returns of the stock in question against the
 2         corresponding market returns. Both also report “Blume-adjusted” betas – a weighted-average
 3         of approximately 2/3 of the raw beta and 1/3 of the market average of 1.0 – to correct for an
 4         empirically observed tendency for betas, on average, to regress toward the market mean over
 5         time that I discuss in more detail below. 104 Other details of their respective methodologies
 6         differ, which is why their beta estimates are not exactly the same even after Mr. Coyne’s
 7         modifications to Bloomberg’s defaults. Figure 19 summarizes the key elements of Value
 8         Line’s and Bloomberg’s beta estimation methodologies.

1    Q. WHY DO BLOOMBERG AND OTHER FINANCIAL DATA PROVIDERS USE
2       DIFFERENT BETA CALCULATION METHODOLOGIES, PROVIDE MULTIPLE
3       ESTIMATES, AND ALLOW USERS TO OVERRIDE THEIR DEFAULTS?
4    A. Beta is intended to be a forward-looking measure of relative risk, so it is inherently uncertain.
5       It cannot be measured directly (like an interest rate) and is usually estimated from historical
 6      data, as the slope of the regression of the returns of a stock against the returns of the market
 7      over a recently-ended historical period. Estimates based on historical data generally reasonably
 8      reflect future expectations, because most companies’ risk profiles change slowly over time.
 9      Slowly changing risk profiles prove particularly true for the relatively stable and predictable
10      utility sector.
11          But if a dramatic change in the market or in individual stocks occurs, as in the pandemic-
12      related market turmoil of early 2020, that change will influence the beta estimate for as long
13      as the period of change is included in the trailing data used in the beta calculation, even if
14      investors’ risk perceptions have returned to their level prior to the dramatic change. Analysts
15      should always examine whether the change in market conditions was temporary or is sustained.
16      Mr. Coyle’s analysis suffers from his failure to examine whether the pandemic-related change
17      in market conditions was temporary or has been sustained.
18   Q. HOW CAN WE DETERMINE WHETHER THE CHANGE IN INVESTORS’ RISK
19      PERCEPTIONS WAS TEMPORARY OR HAS BEEN SUSTAINED?
20   A. Determining whether the change in investors’ risk perception was temporary or has been
21      sustained is typically done by examining how betas calculated using different amounts of
22      trailing data and returns calculated at different frequencies – for example, daily, weekly, or
23      monthly – have changed over time.
24   Q. IS THE NEED TO ESTIMATE BETA USING DIFFERENT METHODOLOGIES
25      WELL KNOWN?
26   A. Yes. Utility cost of capital expert witnesses Michael Vilbert and Bente Villadsen have written
27      about the trade-offs of different methodologies, highlighting the need to consider shorter
28      calculation intervals in the wake of abrupt disruptions such as was experienced first during and
29      then immediately after the pandemic-driven bout of market turmoil in early 2020:


                              Mark E. Ellis Direct Testimony, on behalf of GIPL
                                          GPSC Docket No. 44280
                                                   Page 58

 1                        The choices for the interval for the return data and the length of
 2                        the beta estimation window involve trade-offs between
 3                        obtaining more observations through the choice of a longer
 4                        window and/or more frequent return data, ensuring that no
 5                        structural change has occurred during the estimation window,
 6                        and avoiding problems due to insufficient trading activity. …
 7                        Balancing these considerations, economists typically
 8                        recommend estimating beta using daily, weekly, or monthly
 9                        returns over the most recent 2- to 5-year period, with weekly
10                        being the more common, except if there are reasons to think that
11                        the industry might be subject to recent changes in systematic risk
12                        so that the use of a more recent data window is desirable. 111


13            The need to examine beta using different calculation methodologies is also reflected in data
14         providers’ offerings. As explained previously, Bloomberg allows users to easily override its
15         default beta calculation parameters. S&P GMI, in addition to reporting betas calculated using
16         1 and 3 years of trailing data, provides its users with spreadsheet models that allow them to
17         modify all of its beta calculation parameters.
18            In using both Value and Bloomberg betas, Mr. Coyne implicitly acknowledges that no
19         single beta calculation methodology necessarily reflects current investor expectations. But the
20         differences in the Value Line and (modified) Bloomberg beta methodologies he uses are
21         immaterial. Their different market indexes – NYSE Composite for Value Line, S&P 500 for
22         Bloomberg – are nearly perfectly correlated (0.96 correlation 112), and their slightly different
23         Blume adjustment weights yield differences in beta of 2% or less across the range of typical
24         raw utility betas (0.4-1.0). Mr. Coyne’s chosen methodologies hardly reflect the wide range of
25         ways beta could be calculated, each of which could produce dramatically different results. As
26         Nobel laureate Fischer Black, one of the pioneers of empirical testing of beta and the CAPM,
27         famously admonished, “Watch out for data mining!” 113 – running an analysis several different
28         ways but reporting only the outcomes that support one’s conclusions.

 1   Q. WHICH METHODOLOGICAL DIFFERENCES ACCOUNT FOR THE MOST
 2         VARIATION IN BETA ESTIMATES?
 3   A. The largest potential sources of variation in beta estimates arise from their trailing return
 4         history duration, return calculation frequency, and Blume adjustment parameters.
 5   Q. HOW DOES THE DURATION OF TRAILING RETURN HISTORY AFFECT BETA
 6         ESTIMATES?
 7   A. Following bouts of high market volatility, such as was experienced in the early days of the
 8         pandemic in February and March 2020, betas will be affected as long as the trailing history
 9         includes the volatile period, even if market conditions have stabilized. For example, S&P GMI
10         reports unadjusted betas calculated from both 1 and 3 years of daily returns. 114 As of July 27,
11         2022, the average 1-year beta for the proxy group, which does not include the volatile period,
12         was 0.28; the average 3-year beta, which does include the volatile period, was 0.72. 115 For
13         comparison, on the same day, Yahoo! Finance’s and Zacks’s group average 5-year unadjusted
14         betas using monthly returns were 0.45. 116
15             Figure 21 plots the raw beta for the entire utility sector using 1, 2, and 5 years of weekly
16         returns from June 1926 through May 2022. At any given time, beta can be very sensitive to the
17         trailing history used. As of the end of August 2022, the betas using the 1-, 2-, and 5-year trailing
18         histories were 0.51, 0.47, and 0.71, respectively.

 2            In general, betas calculated using longer return histories tend to be the more stable over
 3         time, as the effect of any short-term period of volatility is reduced by longer surrounding
 4         periods of stability. Because utility cost of capital proceedings seek a relatively long-term
 5         estimate, it might be tempting to conclude that a longer trailing history is preferred in
 6         estimating a utility’s beta for purposes of authorizing an ROE. As Vilbert and Villadsen
 7         astutely recommend, though, “if there are reasons to think that the industry might be subject to
 8         recent changes in systematic risk … the use of a more recent data window is desirable.” 118 The
 9         current 5-year beta of 0.71, driven by the market turmoil of early 2020, is higher than its
10         historical average since 1955 of approximately 0.55. The 1- and 2-year betas, at 0.51 and 0.47,
11         respectively, are in-line with their long-term historical averages. After the brief period of
12         unusual market volatility in early 2020 not seen since the World War II era, investor
13         perceptions of utility risk quickly settled to their pre-pandemic levels. Because of their shorter
14         trailing histories, the 1- and 2-year betas are more reflective of current investor sentiment.
15            Figure 21 also betrays Mr. Coyne’s claim that “the recent movement in betas captures a
16         trend that began five years ago but has been accelerated by the recent market conditions” and

 1         that “[t]he substantial increase in Beta coefficients for the proxy group companies represents a

 3         market and is not just COVID-19 related.” 119 Regardless of the duration of the trailing return
 4         used, utility sector betas were in a decade-plus-long decline before the pandemic. Once the
 5         early-pandemic period of market turmoil fell out of the trailing historical data, as with the 1-
 6         and 2-year betas, betas returned to their pre-pandemic levels.
 7             The sharp declines in the 1- and 2-year betas in Figure 21 make it clear that Mr. Coyne’s
 8         elevated 5-year weekly betas are not valid indicators of current investor expectations but purely
 9         artifacts of the inclusion of a transitory and short-term market anomaly.
10   Q. HOW DOES THE FREQUENCY OF RETURN CALCULATION AFFECT BETA
11         ESTIMATES?
12   A. Figure 22 plots the raw beta for the entire utility sector using 5 years of monthly, weekly, and
13         daily returns from June 1926 through May 2022. As with the trailing history, at any given time,
14         beta can be very sensitive to the return calculation frequency used. As of the end of August
15         2022, the betas using the monthly, weekly, and daily trailing histories were 0.50, 0.71, and
16         0.66, respectively. In general, betas tend to be more stable at higher return calculation
17         frequencies. For any given trailing history duration (e.g., 1, 2, or 5 years), shorter return
18         frequencies generate more data for use in the beta calculation: weekly returns generate
19         approximately four times more data than monthly; daily approximately five times more than
20         weekly. As the return frequency increases, any extreme data points are averaged with a larger
21         number of “typical” data points, which tends to mitigate abrupt changes in beta over time.
22             This finding would tend to recommend using shorter return frequencies, due to their greater
23         stability over time. But the choice of return frequency should reflect the time horizon of the
24         analysis in which the CAPM-derived cost of equity will be used. Utility cost of capital
25         proceedings seek to estimate a cost of equity that applies over a multi-year period. This
26         consideration recommends a longer calculation frequency.

2    Q. HOW DO OTHER BETA CALCULATION PARAMETERS IMPACT THE
3          VARIATION IN BETA ESTIMATES?
4    A. Even a beta calculation parameter as seemingly arbitrary as the day of the week on which
5          weekly returns are calculated can materially affect the beta estimate. Figure 23 shows the 5-
6          year trailing beta, i.e., raw Value Line-equivalent, with returns calculated on each weekday.
7          Currently, Friday yields the highest beta, 0.78, but simply changing the calculation day to
 8         Tuesday reduces the beta to 0.54, 31% lower. This day-of the-week return calculation effect is
 9         only partially mitigated by averaging multiple utilities. The stock prices of individual
10         companies within the sector tend to move together on any given day, so weekly returns
11         calculated on the same day will tend to be similar.
12             This finding highlights the deficiency in Mr. Coyne’s analysis in failing to examine
13         alternative beta calculation methodologies and the potential arbitrariness of his selected
14         methodologies. It also further highlights the need for caution in using the mechanically
15         calculated betas provided by Value Line, Bloomberg, or other financial data providers,
16         particularly betas calculated using weekly returns, without examining how they’ve changed
17         over time or comparing them to long-term historical averages.
