 2   Q. YOU LISTED THE BLUME ADJUSTMENT AS A THIRD SOURCE OF VARIATION
 3         IN BETA ESTIMATES. WHAT IS THE ORIGIN OF THE BLUME ADJUSTMENT?
 4   A. The Blume adjustment is based on an analysis conducted by Wharton professor Marshall
 5         Blume in the early 1970s. Analyzing beta-sorted portfolios, he found a tendency for betas, on
 6         average, to regress toward the market average beta, 1.0, from one time period to the next.122
 7         Based on this finding, some providers of beta estimates report adjusted betas that are a
 8         weighted average of the raw estimate and the market mean. The most common weighting is
 9         2/3 on the raw beta, 1/3 on the market beta (1.0): 123
                                                                      2              1
10                                                 𝛽𝛽𝑎𝑎𝑎𝑎𝑎𝑎𝑎𝑎𝑎𝑎𝑎𝑎𝑎𝑎𝑎𝑎 = 3 𝛽𝛽𝑟𝑟𝑟𝑟𝑟𝑟 + 3.

11            For stocks with raw betas below 1.0, like most utilities historically, the effect of the
12         adjustment is to increase the beta one-third of the way toward 1.0. For example, a stock with a
13         raw beta of 0.4 would have an adjusted beta of 2/3 x 0.4 + 1/3 = 0.6. For its adjusted beta,

         Marshall E. Blume, On the Assessment of Risk, 26:1 J. Finance at 1-10 (1971)
         https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/10.1111/j.1540-6261.1971.tb00584.x.
     123
         The 2/3 and 1/3 weights are based on the regression coefficients Blume presented in his original paper, which
         regressed betas in one period against betas in the previous period.

 1         Bloomberg uses the common 2/3 and 1/3 weights. Value Line’s weights are similar: 0.67 and
 2         0.35, respectively. Value Line also rounds to the nearest 0.05. 124
 3             As Vilbert and Villadsen note, “analysts have different views on whether to use raw or
 4         adjusted betas,” 125 hence the reporting of unadjusted betas by Bloomberg, S&P GMI, Yahoo!
 5         Finance, and Zacks.
 6   Q. IS THE BLUME ADJUSTMENT VALID FOR UTILITIES?
 7   A. No, it is not. The Blume adjustment is based on an observation of the tendency of betas, on
 8         average, to regress toward 1.0. But not every stock exhibits this tendency. Blume did not
 9         investigate whether and how this tendency might vary across stocks with different
10         characteristics.
11             Rutgers professor Richard Michelfelder investigated the validity of the beta adjustment
12         specifically for utility stocks and found no evidence of the average tendency observed by
13         Blume. 126 This can be clearly seen in Figure 24, which shows the same 7-year monthly beta
14         used by Blume in his original analysis for the entire utility sector going back to 1926. Since
15         the 1950s, the beta for the utility sector as a whole has tended to regress toward 0.50-0.60, not
16         1.0. 127
                                    [Remainder of page intentionally left blank.]

         Value Line, “Value Line’s Estimation of Beta,” personal correspondence (October 6, 2021).

         INDUSTRIES, ACADEMIC PRESS at 80 (2017).

         One might ask whether the utility sector average reflects the tendency of individual utility stocks. Betas are
         additive, so a tendency for individual utility stocks to regress toward 1.0, on average, would be reflected in the
         industry beta. Blume used the same logic to extrapolate from the portfolios he analyzed to individual stocks. See
         Eugene F. Fama and Kenneth R. French, The Capital Asset Pricing Model: Theory and Evidence, 18:3 J.
         Economic Perspectives 25, 31 (2004), https://pubs.aeaweb.org/doi/pdfplus/10.1257/0895330042162430.

 2            Blume speculated as to why betas, on average, tend to regress toward 1.0 over time.129
 3         High-beta firms tend to be newer and smaller; as they mature and grow, they become more
 4         risk-averse. In contrast, low-beta firms tend to run out of low-risk investment opportunities
 5         and must accept more risk to stay in business. Neither of these causal explanations applies to
 6         utility operating companies, like Georgia Power and the publicly traded members of the GPC
 7         proxy group. They are large and mature, and their investments tend to have consistently low
 8         risk profiles over time. These attributes combine to keep utilities’ betas sustainably and
 9         significantly below 1.0.
10            Empirical analysis specifically investigating utility betas, grounded in sound economic
11         reasoning, demonstrates that utility betas do not have a tendency to regress toward the market
12         average and therefore should not be Blume-adjusted. 130

 1   Q. WHAT ARE YOUR CONCLUSIONS ABOUT MR. COYNE’S CHOICE OF BETA
 2         ESTIMATION METHODOLOGY?
 3   A. The variation in the three most recent beta estimates in Figure 21 suggests we should not
 4         unthinkingly use the most recent trailing betas from Value Line, Bloomberg, or any other data
 5         provider. It’s important to keep in mind that all methodologies are intended to produce
 6         estimates of investors’ future expectations. The elevated current 5-year betas used by Mr.
 7         Coyne are artifacts of arbitrary choices of calculation period; there is no reason to believe they
 8         reflect investors’ current long-term expectations.
 9             The seven-week bout of market volatility in early 2020 was an anomaly, and investor
10         perceptions of utility risk quickly returned to their pre-pandemic levels. The 5-year return
11         history used in Mr. Coyne’s beta estimates does not accurately reflect current investor
12         sentiment.
13             The betas used by Mr. Coyne are inflated because they are (1) Blume-adjusted and (2)
14         incorporate an anomalous period that does not reflect investors’ current, long-term
15         expectations.

16                      3.       Mr. Coyne estimates his market risk premium using a constant-growth
17                               DCF model with the same flawed perpetuity growth assumption as his
18                               proxy group DCF analysis.

19   Q. HOW DOES MR. COYNE ESTIMATE THE THIRD ASSUMPTION IN THE CAPM,
20         THE MARKET RISK PREMIUM?
21   A. Mr. Coyne estimates the market risk premium (MRP) using the same constant-growth
22         discounted cash flow model he uses for the proxy group in his DCF analysis. As detailed in
23         Section IV.C.2 above, Mr. Coyne’s fatally flawed implementation of the CG DCF erroneously
24         assumes analysts’ 3-to-5-year EPS growth estimates can be sustained into perpetuity, a
25         deficiency of which he is aware. This assumption is invalid for several reasons, perhaps the
26         most compelling of which is that it is simply economically impossible for the market to sustain
27         analysts’ forecast growth rates for even a decade, much less forever. Since 1926, U.S. stock
28         market dividend growth has averaged 4.9% (1.9% in real terms) 131 – roughly 40% of the
29         12.4%-14.2% range assumed by Mr. Coyne. 132 The market has never sustained 12.4% dividend

 1      growth for even seven years, much less into perpetuity – certainly raising a legitimate “concern
 2      that short term growth rates may not be sustainable over the longer-term.” Mr. Coyne
 3      nonetheless ignores his own advice and uses the constant growth DCF to estimate his CAPM
 4      MRP. Consequently, Mr. Coyne’s MRP results beggar belief: at 11.8%-12.5%, they are
 5      roughly 2.5 times the long-term historical average of approximately 5%.

 6                  4.      Mr. Coyne’s flawed CAPM results should be disregarded.

 7   Q. WHAT IS YOUR OVERALL ASSESSMENT OF THE CAPM AND MR. COYNE’S
 8      IMPLEMENTATION OF IT?
 9   A. The CAPM is conceptually sound and one of the most widely used COE models in corporate
10      finance. But Mr. Coyne’s implementation choices – a forecast, not current, risk-free interest
11      rate; a cherry-picked adjusted beta that is not reflective of current market conditions or utilities’
12      long term risk profile; and a flawed MRP model – yields systematically upwardly-biased
13      results. His flawed CAPM results should be disregarded.

14          F.      Implementing the CAPM with more rigorously estimated assumptions
15                  produces substantially lower COE estimates.

16   Q. PLEASE EXPLAIN YOUR IMPLEMENTATION OF THE CAPM.
17   A. There are three components to the CAPM: the risk-free rate, beta, and the market risk premium.
18      For the risk-free rate I use the current, not forecast, 30-year Treasury rate.
19          As explained above in Section IV.E.2, beta is the most subjective of the three CAPM
20      parameters, and no single calculation methodology produces the most reasonable estimates in
21      all market conditions. Based on my review of the latest research literature, I use the betas
22      provided by Yahoo! Finance, calculated using five years of monthly returns. Five-year monthly
23      betas are consistent with cost of capital proceedings’ objective of estimating a multi-year cost
24      of equity, and their current values strike a reasonable balance between current market
25      sentiment and the long-term historical average for utilities.
26          I estimate the market risk-premium from the average of the long-term historical trend and
27      a forward-looking estimate calculated using the MS DCF.


                               Mark E. Ellis Direct Testimony, on behalf of GIPL
                                           GPSC Docket No. 44280
                                                    Page 68

                               PUBLIC DISCLOSURE VERSION
                                        (October 21, 2022 Errata)
 1                 1.      The risk-free rate, one of the three CAPM inputs, should be estimated
 2                         from the current, not forecast, interest rate.

 3   Q. HOW DO YOU ESTIMATE THE RISK-FREE RATE?
4    A. Like Mr. Coyne, I use the 30-year Treasury. I use the most recent full-month average 30-year
5       Treasury rate, for September 2022, of 3.56%. As explained above in Section IV.E.1, current
6       market interest rates provide an unbiased estimate of future rates and are generally superior to
7       publicly available “expert” forecasts.

 8                 2.      Betas calculated using five years of monthly returns are consistent with
 9                         the objective of a cost of capital proceeding and strike an appropriate
10                         balance between recent market conditions and utilities’ long-term
11                         historical risk profile.

12   Q. HOW DO YOU ESTIMATE BETA?
13   A. As explained in Section IV.E.2 above, no single, widely used approach to estimating beta
14      exists. Beta estimates can vary substantially depending, in particular, on the historical trailing
15      period used, return calculation frequency, and adjustment for long-term trend reversion. I use
16      5-year monthly betas such as those provided by Yahoo! Finance and Zacks and commonly
17      used in academic studies. Five-year betas using monthly returns are consistent with cost of
18      capital proceedings’ objective of estimating a multi-year expected cost of equity. The current
19      average of 0.48 for the GPC proxy group, shown in Figure 20, strikes an appropriate balance
20      between the long-term historical range 0.5-0.6 and current subdued investor perceptions of
21      risk, as reflected in 1-year betas using daily returns of approximately 0.4.

22                 3.      The market risk premium estimate should reflect both current
23                         market conditions and the long-term historical trend.

24   Q. WHY CAN’T WE JUST LOOK UP THE MRP LIKE, SAY, WE CAN LOOK UP A
25      STOCK PRICE OR INTEREST RATE?
26   A. The market risk premium is the difference between investors’ expectations of future stock
27      returns and the risk-free (interest) rate. While interest rates are directly observable, expected
28      future market returns are not. The MRP must therefore be estimated. A historical average is a
29      useful check for reasonableness, but the cliché, “past performance is no guarantee of future
30      results,” applies. Instead, analysts use a variety of models and input assumptions to estimate
31      the MRP.

                                          GPSC Docket No. 44280
                                                   Page 69

 1   Q. HOW DO YOU ESTIMATE THE MARKET RISK PREMIUM?
 2   A. I use the average of two methods, one historical, the other forward-looking.

         Total bond return is the monthly interest (the yield divided by 12) plus any capital gain or loss, estimated as the

         regression model is used to adjust the long-term Treasury data to estimate the 20-year yield.

 1         analysis, though, the premium is reduced by the current difference in the inflation-adjusted 20-
 2         and 30-year Treasurys (TIPS), 0.08%, for a 30-year real MRP of 4.75%. Adjusted for the 30-
 3         year inflation rate estimated from the Treasury-TIPS spread, 2.24%, the nominal MRP is
 4         4.86%.
 5   Q. HOW DO YOU ESTIMATE THE FORWARD-LOOKING MRP?
 6   A. I apply the same multi-stage DCF model I use for the GPC proxy group to the market as a
 7         whole, represented by the S&P 500 Index, and subtract the current 30-year Treasury.
 8   Q. HOW DO YOU ESTIMATE THE CURRENT DIVIDEND YIELD FOR THE S&P 500
 9         INDEX?
10   A. I use the same methodology I use for the proxy group members: the most recent dividend paid,
11         through September 30, 2022, divided by the average price of the index over the month of
12         September 2022. The current annualized yield is 1.73%. 136
13   Q. HOW DO YOU ESTIMATE THE INITIAL GROWTH RATE FOR THE S&P 500
14         INDEX?
15   A. I use the weekly estimate provided in S&P’s weekly S&P 500 earnings and estimate report. I
16         use this source because it is publicly available, well-known, frequently updated, and produced
17         by the party with the most intimate knowledge of the index. As of September 30, 2022, S&P’s
18         estimate for the S&P 500 Index’s 3-to-5-year growth rate is 13.6%. 137
19   Q. HOW DO YOU ESTIMATE THE TERMINAL GROWTH RATE FOR THE S&P 500
20         INDEX?
21   A. Many analysts incorrectly assume long-term dividend growth equal to nominal GDP growth.
22         Historically, per-share payout growth, whether measured as dividends or dividends plus net
23         share buybacks, has tracked GDP per capita. 138 I assume a terminal growth rate based on
24         forecast real long-term per-capita GDP plus the current market forecast for long-term inflation,
25         estimated as described in Section IV.D.3.

 2         adjusted forecasts from three government agencies: the Congressional Budget Office
 3         (CBO), 139 the Energy Information Administration (EIA), 140 and the Social Security
 4         Administration (SSA). 141 I use the compound annual growth rate from 2042 to remove any
 5         near-term transitory effects, such as post-Covid economic recovery, and to align with the time
 6         period used to estimate long-term inflation (years 21 through 30 from today).
 7            TIPS payouts are tied to CPI, so the Treasury-TIPS spread provides a forecast of consumer
 8         price inflation. In contrast, real GDP forecasts are deflated by the GDP deflator, which reflects
 9         the prices of all domestic expenditures, including by businesses and government. For
10         consistency with the CPI forecast derived from the Treasury-TIPS spread, which reflects only
11         the prices paid by consumers, I use each agency’s nominal GDP forecast deflated by its CPI

 1         per-capita nominal GDP growth rates is 3.64%. I use the market-implied long-term inflation

 7   Q. WHAT IS YOUR FORWARD-LOOKING MRP?

 9         side equity return forecasts summarized in Figure 3, which average 5.9% over the 10+-year
10         horizon. Subtracting the current T30, 3.57%, gives an MRP of 2.87%.
11   Q. AND YOUR COMBINED MRP?
12   A. The average of my historical (4.86%) and forward-looking (2.87%) MRPs is 3.86%.

 1         monthly average utility bond yields for September 2022, 2/3 x 5.61% + 1/3 x 5.28% = 5.50%
 2         – higher than my CAPM result of 5.41%. 147
 3            But a utility COE estimate – an expected return on equity – cannot be directly compared
 4         to the corresponding utility bond yield. Reported bond yields are yields to maturity, assuming
 5         no default. Default risk for bonds with a Baa1 rating reduces their expected returns by
 6         approximately 0.46%. Similarly, bonds are not as liquid as stocks and attract a liquidity
 7         premium of approximately 0.33%. 148 Deducting the default and liquidity premia from the Baa1
 8         utility bond yield to maturity reduces it to approximately 4.74%, 1.24% and 0.67% lower than
 9         my MS DCF and CAPM COE estimates, respectively.
10            There is no hard-and-fast rule for what the spread of equity returns over the corresponding
11         cost of debt should be. My MS DCF and CAPM results are consistent with my forward-looking
12         MRP and beta estimates, third-party forecasts for total market returns, and the widely
13         recognized low risk profile of utilities.

14            G.      Mr. Coyne’s flotation cost adjustment is not analytically sound and is
15                    therefore unwarranted.

16   Q. MR. COYNE ADDS A FLOTATION COST ADJUSTMENT TO HIS ROE MODEL
17         RESULTS. IS A FLOTATION COST ADJUSTMENT WARRANTED?
18   A. No. A flotation cost adjustment is not warranted. Mr. Coyne cites Roger Morin’s New
19         Regulatory Finance in support of adding a flotation cost adjustment, 149 but the application of
20         the flotation cost adjustment provided in Morin’s book is conceptually flawed. The math relied
21         on by Mr. Coyne for his recommendation shows that shareholders make money from the
22         issuance of new stock, so flotation cost adjustments are especially inappropriate.

         way between A and Baa.

 1             Mr. Morin’s flotation cost adjustment assumes that the market price of the equity issued is
 2         equal to book value. 150 But, as shown in Figure 4 above, the average market-to-book ratio of
 3         the GPC proxy group is 2.1. As implied by the very formula provided by Mr. Morin, when the
 4         market price is greater than book value, new equity issuance is accretive to existing
 5         shareholders – that is, a share of the proceeds of the new issuance accrues to them. 151
 6             Morin derives the flotation cost adjustment from the formula for the extended DCF model
 7         under the assumption of continuous external stock financing:
                                                          𝐷𝐷

 9         where 𝑏𝑏 is the earnings retention ratio, 𝑟𝑟 is ROE, 𝑠𝑠 is the annual rate of new equity issuance
                                                                                                                𝐵𝐵
10         (expressed as a share of existing book equity), and 𝑣𝑣 is the accretion factor, equal to 1 − 𝑃𝑃 . He
                                                                     𝐵𝐵                                              𝐵𝐵
11         then adjusts 𝑃𝑃 for flotation costs, 𝑓𝑓, so that 1 − 𝑃𝑃(1−𝑓𝑓). 152 But as long as (1 − 𝑓𝑓) exceeds 𝑃𝑃 ,

12         i.e., the reciprocal of the market-to-book ratio, existing shareholders gain from new share
13         issuance. Mr. Coyne estimates flotation costs to be 2.47%, so 1 − 𝑓𝑓 = 0.98, much greater than
14         the reciprocal of the GPC proxy group average M/B ratio, 1/2.1 = 0.48.
15   Q. UNDER THE NARUC FAIRNESS PRINCIPLE, ROE SHOULD EQUAL THE
16         ACTUAL COST OF EQUITY. THIS WOULD IMPLY THAT THE MARKET-TO-
17         BOOK RATIO EQUALS 1.0, IN WHICH CASE NEW EQUITY ISSUANCE WOULD
18         NOT BE ACCRETIVE. IF THE COMMISSION ADHERES TO THE NARUC
19         FAIRNESS PRINCIPLE, SHOULD ROE BE ADJUSTED FOR FLOTATION COSTS?
20   A. No. Even if the M/B ratio is equal to 1.0, there is no reason to adjust the ROE for new equity
21         issuance costs.
22             Purchasers of the newly issued equity know that a portion of proceeds will cover
23         transaction costs, and the offering price reflects this information. There is no need to
24         compensate new investors for issuance costs, which they knowingly pay when they purchase
25         new shares, with an adjustment to the ROE. Similarly, existing shareholders know that the
26         company may issue new equity over time. These costs, and their potential effect on expected

 3             Mr. Coyne’s flotation cost adjustment is small and is overwhelmed by the imprecision in
 4         his models. At 0.08%, it amounts to less than 2% of the 8.99%-13.55% range of Mr. Coyne’s
 5         base ROE results. 153 To add a flotation cost adjustment is akin to the elementary school student,
 6         who, upon being asked what they learned during a field trip to the science museum, proudly
 7         says, “Earth is 4.5 billion years and [glancing at a clock] six hours and forty-three minutes

11   V.        GEORGIA POWER’S ROE AND EQUITY RATIO SHOULD OPTIMALLY
12             BALANCE CUSTOMER AND INVESTOR INTERESTS.

13             A.      COE model results must be adjusted for differences in equity ratio.

14   Q. IN SECTION IV.B ABOVE, YOU DISCUSSED ADJUSTING COE MODEL RESULTS
15         FOR DIFFERENCES IN EQUITY RATIOS AMONG THE PROXY GROUP
16         MEMBERS. HOW DO YOU DO THAT?
17   A. The MS DCF and CAPM yield levered costs of equity. To account for differences in capital
18         structure among the proxy group members, the COE results are unlevered to estimate the cost
19         of equity assuming 100% equity financing. 154 The unlevered cost of equity, 𝑘𝑘𝑢𝑢 , is typically
20         expressed as an adjustment to beta in the CAPM:
21                                              𝑘𝑘𝑢𝑢 = 𝑟𝑟𝑓𝑓 + 𝛽𝛽𝑢𝑢 �𝑟𝑟𝑚𝑚 − 𝑟𝑟𝑓𝑓 �, 155
22         where the unlevered beta, 𝛽𝛽𝑢𝑢 , is expressed in terms of the levered equity beta, 𝛽𝛽𝑒𝑒 :


     153
         2022 Rate Case Hr’g Tr. Vol. 1 at 668 (Sept. 28, 2022).
     154
         The unlevered cost of equity differs from the weighted average cost of capital (“WACC”). The unlevered cost
         of capital assumes 100% equity financing; the WACC assumes the company’s current capital structure. While
         under the Modigliani and Miller theorem of capital structure independence, the cost of capital should be the
         same regardless of capital structure, the WACC typically overstates the unlevered cost of equity because the
         expected return on corporate debt is lower than the yield due to default and liquidity risk.
     155
         See, e.g., ASWATH DAMODARAN, DAMODARAN ON VALUATION: SECURITY ANALYSIS FOR INVESTMENT AND
         CORPORATE FINANCE, WILEY 2ND ED. at 129 (2006).

     156
         Unlevered beta is sometimes adjusted for taxes (the “Hamada” adjustment). As explained in Damondaran in
         Valuation, when the capital structure is constant over time, as it is with utilities, then the value of tax shields
         tracks the value of operating assets. Thus, the risk of tax shields will mirror the risk of operating assets and have
         the same discount rate, i.e., the unlevered cost of equity. Id. at 790-93.

 2             The variation in the unlevered COE estimates is much lower than in the levered COEs,

 6         vary considerably, though, from 42% to 74%, which introduces variation in their levered costs
 7         of equity. This variation due to differences in equity ratios makes levered COEs an
 8         inappropriate basis for determining the ROE of a target company, like Georgia Power, which
 9         will likely have a different equity ratio.

 1   height of the dot on the vertical axis,       % – well above the           % required to maintain
 2   Georgia Power’s current Baa1 credit rating. Either or both of Georgia Power’s proposed ROE
 3   and equity ratio can be reduced to lower customer costs while still satisfying investor demands.
 4      The lower of the two ROE arcs represents Georgia Power’s 4.78% unlevered (100%
 5   equity) COE. As discussed previously, the corresponding levered ROE will increase as the
 6   equity ratio declines. To maintain Georgia Power’s current Baa1 rating would require an equity
 7   ratio of 59.2%, slightly higher than GPC’s proposal, and an ROE of 5.62%. If instead a higher
 8   A3 credit rating is targeted, the equity ratio and ROE would need to be 61.7% and 5.54%,
 9   respectively. The trade-off, in terms of customer costs, associated with these higher equity
10   ratios will be quantified below.
11      The two white dots immediately below GPC’s proposal illustrate scenarios in which an
12   equity ratio constraint is imposed, here equal to GPC’s proposed 56%. To maintain target credit
13   ratings of Baa1 or A3, the ROE would need to be increased to 7.18% and 8.36%, respectively.
14   The trade-off, in terms of customer costs, associated with an equity ratio constraint will also
15   be quantified below.

 4   Q. HOW WOULD AN OPTIMIZED ROE AND EQUITY RATIO IMPACT CUSTOMER
 5         COSTS?
 6   A. Figure 29 compares the revenue requirement (pre-tax) weighted average rate of return for the
 7         five scenarios depicted in Figure 28. The ROE optimized for a Baa1 rating, 5.62%, is 49%
 8         lower than Mr. Coyne’s proposed ROE of 11.00% (line 2). Even with the higher 59.2% equity
 9         ratio, after grossing up for taxes, the revenue requirement ROR, 5.92%, is 40% lower than
10         GPC’s 9.83% (lines 6-7). If a higher A3 credit rating is targeted, the additional cost of the
11         required higher 61.7% equity ratio is almost completely offset by the lower levered ROE of
12         5.54%, 162 and the revenue requirement ROR increases only slightly to 5.95%.
13             Figure 29 also shows the average annual total revenue requirement under each of the five
14         scenarios (line 8). Based on analysis of Georgia Power’s general rate case filings, its proposed
15         combined rate of return for both debt and equity, the latter grossed up for taxes, accounts for

     162
           It is assumed that there is no change in the cost of debt at the higher A1 credit rating over the 2023-25 period.
           New issuance accounts for approximately 18% of Georgia Power’s net long-term debt at the end of 2025. The
           difference in yield between A3- and Baa1-rated utility bonds is approximately 0.11%. The change in interest
           costs would therefore be approximately 18% x 0.11% = 0.02% by the end of the forecast period. M. Ellis
           analysis of data in Ex. APA SPA ADH MBR-3, Schedule 3, Workpaper 4.

                                     Mark E. Ellis Direct Testimony, on behalf of GIPL
                                                 GPSC Docket No. 44280
                                                          Page 81

                                    PUBLIC DISCLOSURE VERSION
                                             (October 21, 2022 Errata)
 1         more than 25% of its revenue requirement. 163 The total revenue requirement under GPC’s
 2         proposal averages $9.14 billion per year from 2023 through 2025. Optimized ROEs and equity
 3         ratios, under either the Baa1 or A3 target credit rating, reduce total customer costs by over 11%
 4         (line 10) or more than $1.0 billion per year (line 9).
 5             An equity ratio constraint would impose fairly substantial costs on customers, increasing
 6         the revenue requirement ROR to 6.96% and 7.84% under Baa1 and A3 credit rating targets,
 7         respectively – an increase of nearly 100 and 200 basis points relative to the corresponding
 8         minimum-cost scenarios. While total customer costs are lower than under GPC’s proposal,
 9         they are only 50% to 70% of the reduction without an equity ratio constraint. These results
10         further demonstrate the flaw in the “leverage up” (reduce the equity ratio) argument described
11         in Section III.A above. It is much more cost effective to use any spare credit capacity to reduce
12         ROE than to increase debt.

 5   Q. WHAT ARE YOUR ROE AND EQUITY RATIO RECOMMENDATIONS FOR
 6         GEORGIA POWER?
 7   A. I recommend the A3 target credit rating scenario for Georgia Power, 5.54% ROE and 61.7%
 8         equity ratio, for several reasons. Both the A3 and Baa1 scenarios are consistent with the
 9         Supreme Court’s guidance provided in Hope Natural Gas to set “just and reasonable rates”
10         through a “balancing of the investor and the consumer interests.” In contrast, Mr. Coyne’s
11         recommendation and the two equity ratio-constrained scenarios are overly generous to
12         investors at the expense of customers and therefore fail the Hope standard.
13             The A3 and Baa1 scenarios have the same estimated cost to customers over the 2023-25
14         timeframe. This is largely because the analysis assumes the improved credit rating does not
15         materially reduce Georgia Power’s average cost of debt during the 2023-25 timeframe. Beyond
16         2025, though, the average cost of debt would be materially lower under the A3 scenario,
17         reducing customer costs relative to the Baa1 scenario. Figure 30 summarizes my COE analysis
18         and ROE and equity ratio recommendations.

 1   Q. IF       THE       COMMISSION              ADOPTS        YOUR         ROE       AND       EQUITY         RATIO
 2         RECOMMENDATIONS,                    HOW        WOULD          THE        SOUTHERN            COMPANY’S
 3         SHAREHOLDERS BE AFFECTED?
 4   A. ROEs in excess of the cost of equity resembles a zero-sum game, with the interests of
 5         shareholders and customers in direct opposition. If the Commission adopted my recommended
 6         ROE and equity ratio, I would expect The Southern Company share price to experience a one-
 7         time downward adjustment as investors reset their expectations about future returns. Southern
 8         would still be able to access the equity markets, because Georgia Power’s ROE, based on its
 9         actual cost of equity, i.e., investors’ expected return, 166 would be sufficient to satisfy investors’
10         demands. Investors who purchase shares after the one-time adjustment would earn returns
11         comparable to the return current Southern shareholders expect.


     166
           “Cost of equity” and “expected return on equity” are synonymous. See supra footnote 11.

                                      Mark E. Ellis Direct Testimony, on behalf of GIPL
                                                  GPSC Docket No. 44280
                                                           Page 84

                                    PUBLIC DISCLOSURE VERSION
                                                (October 21, 2022 Errata)
 1            I said that ROE above the cost of equity resembles a zero-sum game between shareholders
 2         and customers. It is not quite zero-sum between these two parties, though, because there is a
 3         third party: the tax authorities. ROE is grossed-up for taxes; to the extent ROE is reduced, taxes
 4         are also reduced. In aggregate, a lower ROE provides approximately one-third more benefit to
 5         customers than shareholders lose in foregone profit. 167 As shown in Figure 31, of the $1.04-
 6         billion reduction in revenue requirement return under the A3 scenario (Figure 29, line 9), the
 7         decline in Georgia Power’s net income accounts for $740 million; $190 million is foregone
 8         taxes, of which approximately $150 million (77.5%) is Federal. 168

12                        Ratemaking is indeed but one species of price-fixing. The fixing

 1                          that the value is reduced does not mean that the regulation is
 2                          invalid. It does, however, indicate that “fair value” is the end
 3                          product of the process of ratemaking, not the starting point, as
 4                          the Circuit Court of Appeals held. The heart of the matter is that
 5                          rates cannot be made to depend upon “fair value” when the value
 6                          of the going enterprise depends on earnings under whatever rates
 7                          may be anticipated. 169

                                                           SUMMARY
Mark E. Ellis is a former utility executive now working as an independent consultant and
testifying expert in finance and economics in utility regulatory proceedings.
Before establishing his own consultancy, Mark led the strategy function at Sempra Energy
(parent of SDG&E and SoCalGas) for fifteen years. Previously, he worked as a consultant in
McKinsey’s energy practice, in international project development for ExxonMobil, and in
industrial demand-side management for Southern California Edison. He has an MS from MIT’s
Technology and Policy Program, where he focused on utility policy and conducted research in
the MIT Energy Lab, and a BS in mechanical engineering from Harvard.

 1   I.      INTRODUCTION

 2   Q. PLEASE STATE YOUR NAME AND PROFESSIONAL AFFILIATION.
 3   A. My name is Mark E. Ellis. I am an economic and financial consultant. My business address is
 4        8595 Nottingham Place, La Jolla, CA 92037.
 5   Q. ON WHOSE BEHALF ARE YOU TESTIFYING?
 6   A. I am testifying on behalf of Georgia Interfaith Power and Light (GIPL).
 7   Q. DO YOU CERTIFY UNDER PENALTY OF PERJURY THAT, TO THE BEST OF
 8        YOUR      KNOWLEDGE,           THE     TESTIMONY          YOU     WILL    GIVE     IN   THIS
 9        PROCEEDING IS TRUE AND CORRECT?
10   A. Yes.
11   Q. PLEASE SUMMARIZE YOUR EDUCATION AND PROFESSIONAL WORK
12        EXPERIENCE.
13   A. I graduated from Harvard University with a Bachelor of Science in Mechanical and Materials
14        Sciences and Engineering and from the Massachusetts Institute of Technology with a Master
15        of Science in Technology and Policy.
16           I have over 25 years of professional experience in the energy industry. Before starting my
17        consulting practice in 2020, I led the strategy function at Sempra Energy for fifteen years. My
18        responsibilities included developing and implementing the enterprise-wide cost of capital
19        estimation process. This critical corporate finance function entailed thorough and ongoing
20        research of the academic and practitioner literature on the historical cost of capital and the
21        various cost of capital estimation methodologies and models; creating a process to estimate,
22        quarterly, the forward-looking, risk-adjusted cost of capital for Sempra’s portfolio of
23        companies spanning a variety of geographies and lines of business; and calibrating the results
24        against historical data and reputable, objective third-party estimates.
25           Previously, I held various positions in strategy, project development, and engineering with
26        McKinsey, ExxonMobil, Southern California Edison, and Sanyo Electric. Last year, I provided
27        expert testimony on behalf of The Utility Reform Network (TURN) before the California
28        Public Utilities Commission in PG&E’s application (A.21-20-04-23) for a $7.5-billion wildfire
29        cost securitization. Earlier in 2022, I provided expert testimony on behalf of The Protect Our
30        Communities Foundation (PCF) before the California Public Utilities Commission in the three


                                Mark E. Ellis Direct Testimony, on behalf of GIPL
                                            GPSC Docket No. 44280
                                                      Page 1

                                    PUBLIC DISCLOSURE VERSION
                                             (October 21, 2022 Errata)
1          California utilities’ application (A.21-08-013 et seq.) to suspend the Cost of Capital
2          Mechanism (CCM). I am currently working on or have completed other utility regulatory cases
3          in New Hampshire, Hawaii, Georgia, and Wisconsin. Attachment MEE-1 contains more detail
4          on my background.

 5             A.      Summary of Conclusions

 6   Q. WHAT IS THE PURPOSE OF YOUR TESTIMONY IN THIS PROCEEDING?
 7   A. I have been asked by GIPL to assess Georgia Power Company’s (GPC) test year 2023 cost of
 8         capital application to analyze and calculate the return on equity (ROE) and equity ratio that are
 9         (1) adequate to ensure that GPC, from 2023 to 2025, earns a return on its investments
10         “commensurate with returns on investments in other enterprises having corresponding risk,”
11         and (2) “sufficient to assure confidence in the financial integrity of the enterprise, so as to
12         maintain its credit and to attract capital.” 1
13   Q. GENERALLY, WHAT CONCLUSIONS DO YOU REACH FROM YOUR ANALYSIS
14         AND CALCULATIONS?
15   A. The Commission can substantially reduce the authorized ROE requested by Georgia Power,
16         and thereby customer costs (by a billion dollars per year), while still enabling Georgia Power
17         to attract debt and equity investment capital, fairly compensate investors for risk, and maintain
18         Georgia Power’s current credit rating.
19   Q. WHAT ARE YOUR RECOMMENDED AUTHORIZED ROE AND EQUITY RATIO?
20   A. I recommend an authorized return on equity (ROE) equal to Georgia Power’s cost of equity
21         (COE) and paired with an equity ratio that, together with my recommended ROE, minimizes
22         customer costs while maintaining Georgia Power’s financial integrity, fairly compensating
23         debt and equity investors for risk, and improving Georgia Power’s credit rating. My
24         recommended authorized ROE is 5.54% at an equity ratio of 61.7%.


       1
           Fed. Power Comm’n v. Hope Nat. Gas Co., 320 U.S. 591, 603 (1944) (attached as MEE-2).

                                   Mark E. Ellis Direct Testimony, on behalf of GIPL
                                               GPSC Docket No. 44280

 1            B.       Summary of Findings

 2                     1.         Georgia Power’s cost of capital testimony employs flawed models and
 3                                assumptions that systematically produce upwardly biased ROE
 4                                estimates.

 5   Q. THERE               IS     A     SIGNIFICANT           DIFFERENCE               BETWEEN    YOUR
 6        RECOMMENDATIONS AND GEORGIA POWER’S RECOMMENDED ROE AND
 7        EQUITY RATIO. HOW DO YOU EXPLAIN THAT?
 8   A. The divergence arises from differences in both the models and input assumptions used to
 9        determine our respective ROE and equity recommendations.
10            Georgia Power’s cost of capital expert, Mr. James Coyne, uses four models in developing
11        his recommended return on equity (ROE). Two of those models, the Risk Premium Analysis
12        (RPA) and Expected Earnings Analysis (EEA), both suffer from a severe, invalidating
13        conceptual flaw: they are based on utilities’ authorized or forecast return on equity, not their
14        actual cost of equity (COE), or the “the return to the equity owner … commensurate with
15        returns on investments in other enterprises having corresponding risks.” 2 Thus, these two
16        models are not commonly used in finance outside of utility regulatory proceedings because
17        they do not actually estimate the cost of equity. These models are akin to developing a nutrition
18        recommendation based on what people actually eat, not on what they should eat to maintain a
19        healthy weight.
20            The other two models used by Mr. Coyne – the discounted cash flow model (DCF) and
21        capital asset pricing model (CAPM) – are widely used throughout finance to estimate the cost
22        of capital. But Mr. Coyne’s implementations of each suffer from numerous flaws which bias
23        his results upward.
24            In implementing the DCF, Mr. Coyne uses unnecessarily long trailing price histories to
25        calculate the dividend yield, which bias his figures upward, and he assumes demonstrably
26        unrealistic, economically impossible long-term dividend growth rates.
27            In implementing the CAPM, he uses an interest rate forecast long and widely known to be
28        systematically upwardly biased. He cherry-picks a beta calculation methodology that does not
29        reflect current investor risk perceptions and applies the “Blume” adjustment that is not valid


      2
          Id. (emphasis added).

                                    Mark E. Ellis Direct Testimony, on behalf of GIPL
                                                GPSC Docket No. 44280
                                                          Page 3

                                   PUBLIC DISCLOSURE VERSION
                                            (October 21, 2022 Errata)
 1       for utilities, both of which upwardly bias his results. He fails to examine other, more robust
 2       beta estimation methodologies, investigate whether the pandemic-related changes in market
 3       conditions and investor perceptions of utility risk were temporary or have been sustained, or
 4       compare his results to the long-term history of utility betas.
 5           Mr. Coyne’s chosen methodology does not reflect the wide range of ways beta could be
 6       estimated, each of which could produce dramatically different results. As Nobel laureate
 7       Fischer Black, one of the pioneers of empirical testing of beta and the CAPM, famously
 8       admonished, “Watch out for data mining!” 3 – running an analysis several different ways but
 9       reporting only the outcomes that support one’s conclusions.
10           To estimate the CAPM market risk premium, Mr. Coyne uses the same flawed
11       implementation of the DCF used for his proxy group, again producing an economically
12       impossible result.
13           Finally, Mr. Coyne fails to adjust his ROE estimates for differences in equity ratio among
14       the proxy group members, and between the proxy group average and Georgia Power.
15           Mr. Coyne’s capital structure recommendation is similarly deficient. While the testimony
16       of Georgia Power’s credit expert, Mr. David M. Fetter, refers to key metrics used in assessing
17       credit quality, Mr. Coyne does not provide any analysis or calculations demonstrating how
18       ROE affects those metrics, and how the impact of ROE on those metrics influences his
19       proposed equity ratio. Regulators in other states have authorized ROEs that are substantially
20       lower than those requested by Georgia Power, with comparable or lower equity ratios, without
21       adversely impacting their utilities’ credit ratings, suggesting the Commission can substantially
22       reduce the ROE requested by Georgia Power, and thereby customer costs, while maintaining
23       its credit rating.


     3
         Fischer Black, Beta and Return, 20(1) J. Portfolio Management 8 (1993) (Available at hhtps://jpm.pm-
         research.com/content/20/1/8.)

 1                    2.      More rigorous, fact-based analysis of Georgia Power’s COE and credit
 2                            metrics yields a recommended ROE roughly 50% lower and an equity
 3                            ratio slightly higher than Georgia Power’s proposal.

 4   Q. PLEASE SUMMARIZE THE MAIN FINDINGS OF YOUR ROE ANALYSES FOR
 5        GEORGIA POWER.
 6   A. Like Mr. Coyne, I use the DCF and CAPM, two models that are widely used throughout
 7        finance, to estimate the cost of capital. Unlike Mr. Coyne, I am careful to use realistic and
 8        rigorously supported assumptions about long-term dividend growth rates, current interest rates,
 9        and risk profiles and premia.
10            As I explain in Section III.A below, the equity ratio required to maintain any given level
11        of credit quality depends on the ROE. Consequently, the ROE and equity ratio must be
12        determined jointly. Instead of Mr. Coyne’s crude equity ratio peer group comparison, which
13        does not consider the most important metrics of credit quality, I model the inter-relationships
14        between key credit metrics, ROE, and equity ratio to arrive at the optimal equity ratio that
15        minimizes customer costs while meeting the return and credit quality requirements of both
16        equity and debt investors.
17            Figure 1 summarizes the key findings of my review of Mr. Coyne’s analysis, the
18        modifications required to correct its deficiencies, the resulting COE estimates, and my ROE
19        and equity ratio recommendations. More rigorous, fact-based, and accurate analyses result in
20        a substantially lower recommendation for Georgia Power’s ROE: 5.54%, roughly one half of
21        Mr. Coyne’s recommended 11.00%, and a slightly higher equity ratio, 61.7% vs. Mr. Coyne’s
22        56%.
23            Based on my analysis of Georgia Power’s general rate case filings, its proposed combined
24        rate of return on both debt and equity, grossed up for taxes, accounts for more than 25% of its
25        revenue requirement. 4 My recommended ROE and equity ratio would reduce Georgia Power
26        customer costs by approximately 11%, over $1 billion, per year.

 1              C.      Organization of testimony

 2   Q. HOW IS YOUR TESTIMONY ORGANIZED?
 3   A. First, I review a few key conceptual issues related to the cost of capital and capital structure.
4          Next, I provide a detailed assessment of Mr. Coyne’s cost of equity estimation methodology
5          and implementation. For the DCF and CAPM, the two of Mr. Coyne’s four cost of equity
6          models that are valid, I explain various modifications to his methodology and assumptions to
7          correct for the deficiencies in Mr. Coyne’s analyses and then provide the resulting COE
8          estimates. I then apply the capital structure concepts reviewed in the first part of my testimony
9          to determine the optimal equity ratio that minimizes customer costs while satisfying the
10         demands of equity and debt investors. Finally, I estimate the potential savings to customers
11         from adopting my recommended ROE and equity ratio instead of Mr. Coyne’s proposal.
12   II.        CONFUSION BETWEEN THE RATE OF RETURN ON CAPITAL AND COST OF
13              CAPITAL HAS LED TO EXCESSIVE AUTHORIZED RETURNS.

14              A.      Rate of return on capital and cost of capital are not the same: rate of return
15                      on capital is a financial performance metric, whereas cost of capital is the
16                      measure of economic cost described in the Hope case.

17   Q. WHAT IS THE OBJECTIVE IN SETTING A UTILITY’S AUTHORIZED RATE OF
18         RETURN?
19   A. The authorized rate of return is the amount of money, expressed as a percentage of capital
20         invested, that a utility is allowed to recover in customer rates to compensate debt and equity
21         investors for assuming the risks of investing in the utility
22              The Supreme Court provides the guiding ratemaking principles in its 1944 Hope Natural
23         Gas case, in which it directs ratemakers to arrive at “just and reasonable rates” by a “balancing
24         of the investor and the consumer interests.” 6 Consumer interests are straightforward: they want
25         to minimize costs and rates. Investors include holders of both the utility’s debt and equity. A
26         utility’s debt investors expect to receive their contractual interest payments. Debtholders
27         therefore have a “legitimate concern with the financial integrity of the company whose rates
28         are being regulated,” 7 i.e., the utility’s credit quality and anticipated ability to fulfill its


     6
         320 U.S. 591, 603.
     7
         Id.

                                  Mark E. Ellis Direct Testimony, on behalf of GIPL
                                              GPSC Docket No. 44280
                                                        Page 7

                                      PUBLIC DISCLOSURE VERSION
                                               (October 21, 2022 Errata)
1              obligations to debtholders. Equity investors expect a fair return, defined in Hope as
 2             “commensurate with returns on investments in other enterprises having corresponding risks.” 8
 3   Q. HOW DOES THE RATE OF RETURN ON CAPITAL DIFFER FROM THE COST OF
 4             CAPITAL?
5    A. The rate of return on capital, often shortened to “rate of return,” is an accounting metric of
 6             financial performance, calculated by dividing the value returned to investors – e.g., interest,
 7             net income – by the amount of capital invested. The cost of capital is the return investors expect
 8             on their investment. It is referred to as a “cost” because it reflects what investors demand in
 9             return for assuming the risk of the investment and, therefore, what companies must pay for that
10             investment. The rate of return on each form of capital, whether calculated retrospectively or
11             estimated prospectively, may or may not equal its respective cost of capital.
12   Q. HOW DOES THE AUTHORIZED RATE OF RETURN ON CAPITAL DIFFER FROM
13             THE RATE OF RETURN AND THE COST OF CAPITAL?
14   A. The authorized rate of return refers to a specific use of this accounting metric to determine a
15             utility’s revenue requirement and customer rates. According to long-established ratemaking
16             principles, which will be explained in more detail below, the authorized rate of return should
17             be set equal to the cost of capital. Figure 2 summarizes the differences between these three sets
18             of metrics for debt, equity, and the combined return.

 2   Q. WHAT PRINCIPLES DO YOU USE TO DETERMINE YOUR RECOMMENDED
 3         AUTHORIZED RATE OF RETURN?
 4   A. As the National Association of Regulatory Utility Commissioners (NARUC) has explained,
 5         “Fundamental financial concepts demonstrate that the fair rate of return to use in ratemaking
 6         for a utility is its cost of capital in order to achieve the proper balance between customers and
 7         investors.” 9 Mr. Coyne has also acknowledged, in ROE testimony in other jurisdictions, “that
 8         the allowed rate of return should equal the cost of capital.” 10 The objective in setting a utility’s
 9         authorized rate of return should, therefore, be to set the rate of return on each source of capital
10         – debt and equity – as close as possible to the actual cost of each source of capital.
11   Q. DOES THE APPROACH TO CALCULATING COST OF EQUITY DIFFER FROM
12         THE APPROACH TO CALCULATING COST OF DEBT?
13   A. Yes. The cost of outstanding debt can be directly determined from its contractual interest rates.
14         Similarly, the cost of debt expected to be issued can be accurately determined from known
15         interest rate indexes for debt of comparable credit quality, such as Moody’s utility bond


       9
         John D. Quackenbush, Cost of Capital and Capital Markets: A Primer for Utility Regulators, Nat’l Ass’n of
         Regul. Util. Comm’s at 10 (2019) (emphasis added) https://bit.ly/3Th1lC8.
      10
         Cal. Pub. Util. Comm’n, Docket No. A 22-04-008 et seq., Reporters’ Transcript: Virtual Proceeding at 65
         (September 12, 2022).

                                       Mark E. Ellis Direct Testimony, on behalf of GIPL
                                                   GPSC Docket No. 44280
                                                             Page 9

                                      PUBLIC DISCLOSURE VERSION
                                                (October 21, 2022 Errata)
 1         indexes, which, in turn, are based on interest rates directly observed in the market. In contrast,
 2         the cost of equity, both existing and to-be-issued, cannot be directly observed and must be
 3         estimated using various models.
 4   Q. WHY IS THE DISTINCTION BETWEEN THE COST OF CAPITAL AND RATE OF
 5         RETURN IMPORTANT?
 6   A. The cost of capital and rate of return (on capital) are entirely different concepts. The rate of
 7         return is a financial performance metric. The cost of capital is an economic concept.
 8         Nonetheless, they are frequently referred to interchangeably in utility regulatory proceedings,
 9         perhaps in part because finance professionals commonly refer to the cost of capital as the
10         expected return (on capital). 11
11             The muddling of the difference between the cost of capital and the rate of return is not just
12         of semantic concern, particularly for the cost of equity, which must be estimated using various
13         models rather than directly observed (like the cost of debt).
14             This confusion between the cost of capital and the return on capital has infiltrated some of
15         the models commonly used in utility cost of capital proceedings to estimate the cost of equity.
16         These models’ apparent influence on regulatory decisions, though, does not make them correct
17         or mean they provide a suitable basis for estimating the cost of equity.
18             Models that rely exclusively on historical or forecast utility rates of return on equity,
19         without reference to utilities’ actual cost of equity, should be rejected outright. Mr. Coyne uses
20         two such models, one relying exclusively on historical authorized ROEs, the other on forecast
21         ROEs. These models incorporate no information about the actual cost of equity and are
22         therefore inherently flawed and produce invalid results. Consistent with the fairness principle
23         described by NARUC, and Mr. Coyne’s own statements, only models that estimate the cost of
24         equity should be used to determine the authorized ROE.


      11
           See, e.g., TIM KOLLER, MARC GOEDHART ET AL., VALUATION, 5TH ED. MCKINSEY & CO. 35 (2010): “The cost of
           capital is the price charged by investors for bearing the risk that the company’s future cash flows may differ
           from what they anticipate when they make the investment. The cost of capital to a company equals the
           minimum return that investors expect to earn from investing in the company. That is why the terms expected
           return to investors and cost of capital are essentially the same. The cost of capital is also called the discount
           rate, because you discount future cash flows at this rate when calculating the present value of an investment, to
           reflect what you will have to pay investors” (emphasis in original).

 1            B.      Multiple, diverse sources of evidence demonstrate that utilities’ authorized
 2                    ROEs far exceed their cost of equity.

 3   Q. DO AUTHORIZED ROES REFLECT THE ACTUAL COST OF EQUITY?
 4   A. No. Substantial, robust evidence suggests that authorized ROEs for nearly all U.S. utilities
 5         exceed their cost of equity. Georgia Power’s ROE follows this national pattern. Below, I
 6         provide three diverse analyses that compellingly demonstrate, individually and collectively,
 7         the substantial gap between authorized ROEs and utilities’ actual cost of equity: expected
8          equity return forecasts produced by investment professionals, utility market-to-book ratios, and
9          the increasing spread between authorized ROEs and interest rates.

10                    1.      Investment firms’ expected return forecasts for the U.S. equity market
11                            as a whole – which is riskier, on average, than utilities – are consistently
12                            lower than utilities’ authorized ROEs.

13   Q. ARE THERE OTHER PUBLIC SOURCES FOR COST OF EQUITY ESTIMATES
14         OUTSIDE UTILITY REGULATORY PROCEEDINGS?
15   A. Utility cost of capital proceedings are not the only purpose for which expected returns on equity
16         are estimated. Investment firms, such as JP Morgan, BlackRock, and T. Rowe Price, regularly
17         publish capital market assumption reports (CMAs) – expected return forecasts for various asset
18         classes. Figure 3 summarizes a survey of U.S. equity market return forecasts published by over
19         thirty firms in 2021.
20            The CMA forecasts shown in Figure 3 are grouped by assumed investment horizon: less
21         than ten years, ten years (the most common), and more than ten years. 12 The average across
22         the longer-term 10-year and more-than-ten-year horizons, 5.7%, is approximately 40% less
23         than the average ROE authorized for regulated utilities throughout the United States in 2021,
24         9.5%. 13 Not a single one of the forty-two expected return forecasts that I reviewed 14 is as high
25         as the average ROE yielded by any of Mr. Coyne’s models.

3   prospectively – than the market, and therefore investors have lower return expectations for
4   utilities than for the market. The fact that authorized utility ROEs are so much higher than the
5   expected returns on the overall market, which is higher risk, is a compelling indicator that
6   authorized ROEs far exceed utility investors’ expected returns, i.e., utilities’ actual cost of
7   equity.

 1                      2.       Market-to-book ratios reveal that utilities’ cost of equity is
 2                               substantially lower than authorized ROEs.

 3   Q. WHAT IS A MARKET-TO-BOOK RATIO?
 4   A. “Market” value refers to the price one must pay to purchase a share of a company’s stock at
 5         any given time. “Book” value refers to the net equity invested in a company. In general, market
 6         value reflects the discounted value of a company’s future cash flows, while book value reflects
 7         the historical investment in the company. The market-to-book ratio (M/B) is a commonly used
 8         financial metric that indicates the amount of shareholder value added in excess of shareholders’
 9         return expectations.
10   Q. HOW DOES A MARKET-TO-BOOK RATIO RELATE TO RETURNS?
11   A. It is a well understood financial principle that a market-to-book ratio greater than 1.0 indicates
12         the company is expected to earn a return on its investment in excess of the actual cost of capital.
13         A positive net present value (NPV), i.e., the value of a company minus its investments, is the
14         signature indicator of a rate of return that exceeds the cost of capital. NPV is equal to
15         investment multiplied by (M/B – 1.0), so M/B exceeding 1.0 indicates that NPV is positive.
16   Q. WHAT DO UTILITY STOCK MARKET-TO-BOOK RATIOS REVEAL ABOUT
17         ROES RELATIVE TO UTILITIES’ COST OF EQUITY?
18   A. It has long been recognized that utilities’ market-to-book (M/B) ratios provide insight into the
19         relationship between authorized return and the true cost of capital. Legendary regulatory
20         economist Alfred Kahn 16 called attention to this phenomenon over fifty years ago in his 1970
21         classic, The Economics of Regulation: Principles and Institutions:

22                           [T]he sharp appreciation in the prices of public utility stocks, to
23                           one and half and then two times their book value during this
24                           period, reflected … a growing recognition that the companies in
25                           question were in fact being permitted to earn considerably more
26                           than their cost of capital. … The source of the discrepancy
27                           between market and book value has been that commissions have
28                           been allowing r’s [returns on equity] in excess of k [market cost
29                           of equity]; if instead they had set r equal to k, or proceeded at


      16

           dies at 93, Cornell Chronicle (December 27, 2010) https://news.cornell.edu/stories/2010/12/alfred-kahn-father-
           airline-deregulation-dies-93.

 2                                 value … would have disappeared, or would never have arisen. 17

 6        financial crisis, has exceeded 1.5 since 1995.

 5                         The market-to-book ratio expresses the market value of the
 6                         firm’s outstanding common stock to the book value of its equity.
 7                         If the two are equal the expected return on the book will equal
 8                         the expected return on the market value of the company, which
 9                         in turn will equal the cost of capital for a company of that degree
10                         of risk.

13        invested capital), they have positive NPVs – prima facie evidence that they are earning more
14        than their cost of capital.

 1   Q. IN PRACTICAL, DOLLARS-AND-CENTS TERMS, WHAT DOES IT MEAN FOR A
 2         UTILITY TO HAVE A MARKET VALUE THAT IS HIGHER THAN ITS BOOK
 3         VALUE?
 4   A. In practical terms, this means that, for every dollar of equity a utility invests, shareholders
 5         receive back not just their investment plus a reasonable return, which would be the case when
 6         M/B = 1.0, but additional value equivalent to their equity investment multiplied by (M/B –
 7         1.0). At current M/B ratios near 2.0, authorized ROEs effectively double the value of utilities’
 8         equity investments, on top of returning their cost of equity. Such high returns are not necessary
 9         to attract capital and needlessly increase customer costs.
10   Q. ARE YOU ABLE TO CALCULATE GEORGIA POWER’S MARKET-TO-BOOK
11         RATIO?
12   A. Georgia Power is not publicly traded, so its market value is not directly observable.
13         Nonetheless, The Southern Company’s market-to-book ratio, averaging 2.82 over the month
14         of September 2022, 22 provides a reasonable estimate.
15   Q. IS MR. COYNE AWARE OF THE RELATIONSHIP BETWEEN THE MARKET-TO-
16         BOOK RATIO AND RETURNS?
17   A Yes. Mr. Coyne has acknowledged that a market-to-book ratio greater than 1.0 necessarily
18         implies that investors’ expected return, i.e., the cost of equity, is less than their authorized
19         return. In a recent cost of capital proceeding in California, Mr. Coyne testified to the following
20         principles:
21                •      “The terms ‘expected return to investors’ and ‘cost of capital’ are essentially
22                       the same.” 23
23                •      “The allowed rate of return should equal the cost of capital,” 24 the NARUC
24                       fairness principle.
25                •      “A typical [investor] expectation is the utility will earn its allowed return
26                       [on book equity].” 25 Mr. Coyne’s Expected Earnings Analysis is predicated
27                       on this assumption, 26 and the Value Line reports from which the EEA draws

 1                   its input assumptions explicitly state that the reports’ ROE forecasts are
 2                   based on allowed returns. 27
 3               •   “[I]nvestors can’t buy shares at book value … They [investors] can only
 4                   buy them [shares of utility stocks] at market value.” 28
 5               •   “[M]arket-to-book values [of] electric and gas utilities all hover in around
 6                   2.0; although there is variation between companies. It’s been a long-term
 7                   trend towards increasing the market-to-book ratio for utilities.” 29
 8               •   It “would be highly unusual” to find “any company in [Mr. Coyne’s proxy
 9                   group] sample that does not have a market value greater than the book
10                   value.” 30
11               •   “When market-to-book ratio is greater than 1.0 that means that investors are
12                   willing to pay more than book value for the same stream of cash flows.” 31
13               •   “[W]hen an investor pays more for the same stream of cash flows, that
14                   necessarily implies a lower return.” 32

15           The logical conclusions from Mr. Coyne’s testimony are that (1) investors’ expected
16   returns on book equity are based on authorized ROEs (as can be seen in Figure 5, which shows the
17   Value Line ROE data used in Mr. Coyne’s EEA; the averages, which range from 10.0% to 10.7%,
18   are in line with the national average ROE authorized for electric utilities in 2020-22, 9.4% 33); (2)
19   investors pay more than book value when they invest in utilities; (3) investors’ expected return on
20   their investment, i.e., the cost of equity, must be less than the authorized ROE; and (4) according
21   to NARUC’s fairness principle, under which the ROE should be set equal to the COE, historical,
22   current, and forecast ROEs, because they systematically exceed the cost of equity, can provide no
23   basis for determining the appropriate authorized ROE.

 1                      3.      Authorized ROEs and interest rates have diverged without a
 2                              corresponding increase in utilities’ risk profile.

 3   Q. WHY DO REGULATORS APPROVE AUTHORIZED ROES IN EXCESS OF
 4         UTILITIES’ ACTUAL COST OF EQUITY?
 5   A. A mathematical model called the Pólya urn can provide insight into why regulators have
 6         continued to approve authorized ROEs in excess of utilities’ actual cost of capital. 34 Historical
 7         return on equity decisions can be thought of as balls in an urn. To decide on a new case, the
 8         regulator draws a ball from the urn. The ball is then replaced, along with a new ball –
 9         representing the current ROE decision – with the same value. This process of sampling-with-
10         replacement-plus-duplication has a self-reinforcing property sometimes called the rich-get-
11         richer or Matthew effect.
12             Of course, this model is over-simplified because regulators look at other information
13         besides past authorized ROEs. The basic model can be modified to include additional balls in
14         the urn representing new information, such as the estimated current cost of equity. Nonetheless,
15         as long as regulators look at, much less rely on, past ROEs, changes in authorized ROEs will
16         lag changes in the current true cost of equity.
17             The basic utility regulatory model and risk profile have not changed significantly for
18         decades, so the utility equity risk premium – the spread of the cost of capital over risk-free
19         government interest rates – has remained stable. The Pólya urn model predicts that, in a market
20         in which interest rates and, assuming a relatively stable utility equity risk premium, utilities’
21         cost of equity have been trending downward for decades, authorized ROEs will consistently
22         exceed the actual cost of equity, and the spread will widen over time.
23             The data confirm the Pólya urn model’s prediction of such a widening spread between
24         authorized ROEs and the actual cost of equity. Figure 6 shows the quarterly average authorized
25         ROE for all U.S. utilities, the 30-year Treasury rate, and their difference. While interest rates
26         have declined steadily since the mid-1980s, authorized ROEs have not kept pace. As a result,
27         the ROE-Treasury spread has more than tripled, from approximately 2.3% in the early 1980s
28         to 7.1% in the two years through September 2022. It can be estimated from the Pólya urn model


      34
           See, e.g., Learning Machines, The Polya Urn Model: A simple simulation of “The Rich get Richer” (Sept. 7,
           2021) https://blog.ephorie.de/the-polya-urn-model-a-simple-simulation-of-the-rich-get-richer.

                                   Mark E. Ellis Direct Testimony, on behalf of GIPL
                                               GPSC Docket No. 44280
                                                        Page 19

                                  PUBLIC DISCLOSURE VERSION
                                           (October 21, 2022 Errata)
1        described above that, even under conservative assumptions, regulators, on average, assign no
2        more than a 25% weight to the current cost of equity and at least 75% to recent ROEs. No
3        evidence suggests that utilities’ risk profile has systematically increased over this period, so
4        setting ROEs higher and higher relative to utilities’ actual cost of equity unnecessarily raises
5        rates and costs to customers.

2    III.       ROE AND CAPITAL STRUCTURE ARE INTERRELATED AND CANNOT BE
3               DETERMINED SEPARATELY.

 4              A.       ROE and capital structure are interrelated through ROE’s impact on cash
 5                       flow.

 6   Q. DOES THE CAPITAL STRUCTURE IMPACT CUSTOMER COSTS?
 7   A. Yes. Capital structure refers to the share of a utility’s investment that is funded by debt and
 8          equity. Because equity generally has a higher cost than debt, assuming no change in authorized
 9          ROE, a higher equity ratio tends to increase customer costs.
10   Q. DOES THE CAPITAL STRUCTURE IMPACT A UTILITY’S CREDIT QUALITY?
11   A. Yes. A primary determinant of a company’s credit quality – its anticipated ability to repay its
12          debts – is the amount of debt outstanding relative to the total amount of capital, both debt and
13          equity, invested in the company. In general, a higher equity ratio tends to improve a utility’s
14          credit quality. Equity ratio is not the only determinant of credit quality, though. As will be
15          explained in more detail below, credit quality is also determined by the amount of cash
16          available to service the debt.

 1   Q. PLEASE EXPLAIN THE RELATIONSHIP BETWEEN ROE AND CAPITAL
 2         STRUCTURE.
 3   A. The testimony of Georgia Power’s credit expert, Mr. Steven Fetter, reveals that ROE and
 4         capital structure are inextricably linked.
 5            Mr. Fetter describes the importance of the ratio of funds from operations (FFO) to debt in
 6         rating agencies’ assessments of utility credit quality. 38 FFO and similar cash flow metrics 39
 7         measure the cash available to pay debt interest and principal. What he fails to clarify in his
 8         written testimony is that net income is a key component of FFO. 40 Net income, in turn, is the
 9         product of rate base, equity ratio, and ROE. Consequently, ROE and equity ratio are key
10         determinants of FFO. During cross-examination, Mr. Fetter acknowledged the relationship
11         between capital structure, ROE, and credit quality:
12                        Q. [Ms. Whitfield]: On page 14, you talk about key quantitative
13                           measures. Specifically, you reference FFO to debt and debt
14                           to EBITDA as two core financial ratios that are considered
15                           by credit agencies.

16                        A. [Mr. Fetter]: Yes.

17                        Q. Do both of those ratios include inputs that are affected by
18                           both capital structure and the return on equity?

19                        A. Well, it certainly factors in the equity/debt split because in
20                           one, the debt divides from the bottom and in the other, the
21                           debt is divided into by the EBITDA. As far as whether ROE
22                           gets factored into this, FFO – ROE ends up resulting in
23                           revenues and FFO is a certain measure and EBITDA is an
24                           earnings measure. And so ROE would impact those two
25                           items. 41
26            In the context of the regulatory objective of setting a capital structure that appropriately
27         balances customer and investor interests, as ROE increases, the amount of debt in the capital

1          structure can also increase while still maintaining the utility’s credit quality; similarly, as the
2          ROE declines, the equity ratio would need to increase to maintain the same creditworthiness.
3              Curiously, Mr. Coyne’s capital structure analysis makes no reference whatsoever to any
 4         credit metrics. Instead, he bases his equity ratio recommendation, 56%, solely on a peer
5          comparison. 42
6    Q. HOW SHOULD A UTILITY’S CAPITAL STRUCTURE BE DETERMINED?
7    A. The appropriate capital structure can be determined more rigorously by using the analytical
8          methods and metrics employed by credit rating agencies and as described by Mr. Fetter. The
9          level of debt that can be accommodated in the capital structure will vary with ROE, because
10         funds from operation (FFO), a key determinant of a utility’s creditworthiness, is based on net
11         income, and net income is based on ROE, for any given credit rating and its corresponding
12         FFO/debt ratio.
13             Figure 8 illustrates the relationships between equity ratio, ROE, and FFO/debt. The
14         horizontal axis is the equity ratio; the vertical axis is FFO/debt. The light orange horizontal
15         band represents the range of FFO/debt that corresponds to the utility’s desired credit rating.
16         The dark orange arcing lines correspond to different levels of authorized ROE, with increasing
17         line thickness representing increasing ROE. Holding the equity ratio constant, FFO/debt
18         declines as the ROE is reduced (moving down from a thicker ROE line to a thinner line). But
19         the decline in FFO/debt when ROE is reduced can be reversed by increasing the equity ratio
20         (moving along the thinner ROE line up and to the right). Any number of combinations of ROE
21         and equity ratio can meet the level of FFO/debt needed to maintain the utility’s credit rating.
22         A higher ROE requires less equity to maintain the same FFO/debt and credit rating; a lower
23         ROE can maintain the same FFO/debt and credit rating if it is paired with a higher equity ratio.

 2            Many observers see utilities’ healthy credit ratings and low cost of debt and conclude that
 3        the best way to reduce customer costs is to increase the amount of debt in the capital structure.
 4        As explained above, current authorized ROEs far exceed utilities’ actual cost of equity, so
 5        ample scope exists for the Commission to reduce Georgia Power’s ROE without adversely
 6        affecting its ability to raise equity. At typical utility credit ratings, savings from a lower ROE,
 7        after grossing up for taxes, generally more than make up for the incremental cost of any
 8        additional equity required in the capital structure. For example, based on analysis of data
 9        provided in Moody’s October 2021 Georgia Power credit opinion, 43 every 1.0% reduction in
10        ROE reduces total customer costs by approximately 2.0%, even after accounting for the
11        approximately 2.0% increase in equity ratio needed to maintain Georgia Power’s current cash
12        flow-to-debt ratio. 44
13            Total customer costs can be reduced by decreasing the ROE while increasing the equity
14        ratio to maintain the utility’s creditworthiness (i.e., its cash flow-to-debt) because the trade-off
15        is not one-for-one. Net income accounts for about 50% of GPC’s FFO; other items, such as

     43

 1         depreciation and amortization, account for the rest. Consequently, a relatively large reduction
 2         in net income due to a sharp cut in ROE would reduce FFO by roughly half as much in
 3         percentage terms. In addition, ROE is grossed-up for taxes, which are not included in FFO, so
 4         the savings to customers from a lower ROE is amplified. Rather than “leverage up,” i.e., reduce
 5         the equity ratio and increase debt, at current ROEs to reduce rates, it is more cost-effective to
 6         use any spare credit capacity to reduce the utility’s ROE, or even to increase the equity ratio if
 7         necessary to maintain a target credit rating, than to increase debt.
 8            Reducing ROE and increasing the equity ratio has the additional benefit of reducing debt-
 9         to-capitalization, another key metric used by rating agencies to assess credit quality. 45

10            B.      Georgia Power’s capital structure proposal does not address the interaction
11                    between ROE and capital structure.

12   Q. DOES GEORGIA POWER’S CAPITAL STRUCTURE PROPOSAL TAKE INTO
13         ACCOUNT THE INTERACTION BETWEEN ROE AND CAPITAL STRUCTURE?
14   A. As Mr. Coyne acknowledged during cross examination, he developed his capital structure
15         recommendation without incorporating the interrelationship between credit quality, ROE, and
16         equity ratio. 46 Neither Mr. Coyne nor Mr. Fetter provides any analysis or calculations
17         demonstrating how FFO/debt interacts with ROE and how that interaction influences GPC’s
18         proposed equity ratio. As a result, how much GPC’s proposed ROE and/or the equity ratio
19         could be reduced to lower customer costs while still maintaining its desired investment-grade
20         credit rating cannot be assessed from the testimony and data GPC has provided.
21            GPC’s analysis should have included a detailed analysis of the relationships between ROE,
22         equity ratio, and creditworthiness and the impact of different combinations of equity ratio and
23         ROE on customers, lenders, and shareholders so that the Commission could examine the ability
24         of Georgia Power to obtain sufficient capital while minimizing customer costs. The
25         Commission should require Georgia Power to provide a detailed analysis of the relationships
26         between ROE, equity ratio, and creditworthiness so that the Commission possesses the facts it
27         needs to come to fact-based conclusions about Georgia Power’s authorized capital structure.
