 7   Q. WHAT IS YOUR OVERALL ASSESSMENT OF THE CAPM AND MR. COYNE’S
 8      IMPLEMENTATION OF IT?
 9   A. The CAPM is conceptually sound and one of the most widely used COE models in corporate
10      finance. But Mr. Coyne’s implementation choices – a forecast, not current, risk-free interest
11      rate; a cherry-picked adjusted beta that is not reflective of current market conditions or utilities’
12      long term risk profile; and a flawed MRP model – yields systematically upwardly-biased
13      results. His flawed CAPM results should be disregarded.

14          F.      Implementing the CAPM with more rigorously estimated assumptions
15                  produces substantially lower COE estimates.

16   Q. PLEASE EXPLAIN YOUR IMPLEMENTATION OF THE CAPM.
17   A. There are three components to the CAPM: the risk-free rate, beta, and the market risk premium.
18      For the risk-free rate I use the current, not forecast, 30-year Treasury rate.
19          As explained above in Section IV.E.2, beta is the most subjective of the three CAPM
20      parameters, and no single calculation methodology produces the most reasonable estimates in
21      all market conditions. Based on my review of the latest research literature, I use the betas
22      provided by Yahoo! Finance, calculated using five years of monthly returns. Five-year monthly
23      betas are consistent with cost of capital proceedings’ objective of estimating a multi-year cost
24      of equity, and their current values strike a reasonable balance between current market
25      sentiment and the long-term historical average for utilities.
26          I estimate the market risk-premium from the average of the long-term historical trend and
27      a forward-looking estimate calculated using the MS DCF.


                               Mark E. Ellis Direct Testimony, on behalf of GIPL
                                           GPSC Docket No. 44280
                                                    Page 68

                               PUBLIC DISCLOSURE VERSION
                                        (October 21, 2022 Errata)
 1                 1.      The risk-free rate, one of the three CAPM inputs, should be estimated
 2                         from the current, not forecast, interest rate.

 3   Q. HOW DO YOU ESTIMATE THE RISK-FREE RATE?
4    A. Like Mr. Coyne, I use the 30-year Treasury. I use the most recent full-month average 30-year
5       Treasury rate, for September 2022, of 3.56%. As explained above in Section IV.E.1, current
6       market interest rates provide an unbiased estimate of future rates and are generally superior to
7       publicly available “expert” forecasts.

 8                 2.      Betas calculated using five years of monthly returns are consistent with
 9                         the objective of a cost of capital proceeding and strike an appropriate
10                         balance between recent market conditions and utilities’ long-term
11                         historical risk profile.

12   Q. HOW DO YOU ESTIMATE BETA?
13   A. As explained in Section IV.E.2 above, no single, widely used approach to estimating beta
14      exists. Beta estimates can vary substantially depending, in particular, on the historical trailing
15      period used, return calculation frequency, and adjustment for long-term trend reversion. I use
16      5-year monthly betas such as those provided by Yahoo! Finance and Zacks and commonly
17      used in academic studies. Five-year betas using monthly returns are consistent with cost of
18      capital proceedings’ objective of estimating a multi-year expected cost of equity. The current
19      average of 0.48 for the GPC proxy group, shown in Figure 20, strikes an appropriate balance
20      between the long-term historical range 0.5-0.6 and current subdued investor perceptions of
21      risk, as reflected in 1-year betas using daily returns of approximately 0.4.

22                 3.      The market risk premium estimate should reflect both current
23                         market conditions and the long-term historical trend.

24   Q. WHY CAN’T WE JUST LOOK UP THE MRP LIKE, SAY, WE CAN LOOK UP A
25      STOCK PRICE OR INTEREST RATE?
26   A. The market risk premium is the difference between investors’ expectations of future stock
27      returns and the risk-free (interest) rate. While interest rates are directly observable, expected
28      future market returns are not. The MRP must therefore be estimated. A historical average is a
29      useful check for reasonableness, but the cliché, “past performance is no guarantee of future
30      results,” applies. Instead, analysts use a variety of models and input assumptions to estimate
31      the MRP.

 5   Q. HOW DO YOU ESTIMATE THE FORWARD-LOOKING MRP?
 6   A. I apply the same multi-stage DCF model I use for the GPC proxy group to the market as a
 7         whole, represented by the S&P 500 Index, and subtract the current 30-year Treasury.
 8   Q. HOW DO YOU ESTIMATE THE CURRENT DIVIDEND YIELD FOR THE S&P 500
 9         INDEX?
10   A. I use the same methodology I use for the proxy group members: the most recent dividend paid,
11         through September 30, 2022, divided by the average price of the index over the month of
12         September 2022. The current annualized yield is 1.73%. 136
13   Q. HOW DO YOU ESTIMATE THE INITIAL GROWTH RATE FOR THE S&P 500
14         INDEX?
15   A. I use the weekly estimate provided in S&P’s weekly S&P 500 earnings and estimate report. I
16         use this source because it is publicly available, well-known, frequently updated, and produced
17         by the party with the most intimate knowledge of the index. As of September 30, 2022, S&P’s
18         estimate for the S&P 500 Index’s 3-to-5-year growth rate is 13.6%. 137
19   Q. HOW DO YOU ESTIMATE THE TERMINAL GROWTH RATE FOR THE S&P 500
20         INDEX?
21   A. Many analysts incorrectly assume long-term dividend growth equal to nominal GDP growth.
22         Historically, per-share payout growth, whether measured as dividends or dividends plus net
23         share buybacks, has tracked GDP per capita. 138 I assume a terminal growth rate based on
24         forecast real long-term per-capita GDP plus the current market forecast for long-term inflation,
25         estimated as described in Section IV.D.3.

 1         monthly average utility bond yields for September 2022, 2/3 x 5.61% + 1/3 x 5.28% = 5.50%
 2         – higher than my CAPM result of 5.41%. 147
 3            But a utility COE estimate – an expected return on equity – cannot be directly compared
 4         to the corresponding utility bond yield. Reported bond yields are yields to maturity, assuming
 5         no default. Default risk for bonds with a Baa1 rating reduces their expected returns by
 6         approximately 0.46%. Similarly, bonds are not as liquid as stocks and attract a liquidity
 7         premium of approximately 0.33%. 148 Deducting the default and liquidity premia from the Baa1
 8         utility bond yield to maturity reduces it to approximately 4.74%, 1.24% and 0.67% lower than
 9         my MS DCF and CAPM COE estimates, respectively.
10            There is no hard-and-fast rule for what the spread of equity returns over the corresponding
11         cost of debt should be. My MS DCF and CAPM results are consistent with my forward-looking
12         MRP and beta estimates, third-party forecasts for total market returns, and the widely
13         recognized low risk profile of utilities.

14            G.      Mr. Coyne’s flotation cost adjustment is not analytically sound and is
15                    therefore unwarranted.

16   Q. MR. COYNE ADDS A FLOTATION COST ADJUSTMENT TO HIS ROE MODEL
17         RESULTS. IS A FLOTATION COST ADJUSTMENT WARRANTED?
18   A. No. A flotation cost adjustment is not warranted. Mr. Coyne cites Roger Morin’s New
19         Regulatory Finance in support of adding a flotation cost adjustment, 149 but the application of
20         the flotation cost adjustment provided in Morin’s book is conceptually flawed. The math relied
21         on by Mr. Coyne for his recommendation shows that shareholders make money from the
22         issuance of new stock, so flotation cost adjustments are especially inappropriate.


     147
         The ratings of Moody’s A- and Baa-rated utility bond indexes are comparable to Moody’s A2 and Baa2 ratings,
         respectively. The intermediate ratings are A3 and Baa1, so Georgia Power’s Baa1 rating falls two-thirds of the
         way between A and Baa.
     148
         Wolfgang Bühler and Monika Trapp, Time-Varying Credit Risk and Liquidity Premia in Bond and CDS
         Markets, CFR Working Papers 09-13, University of Cologne, Centre for Financial Research at 37 (2008),
         https://www.fdic.gov/analysis/cfr/2008/april/trapp-buhler.pdf.
     149
         2022 Rate Case Hr’g Tr. Vol. 1 at 669 (Sept. 28, 2022).

                                            (October 21, 2022 Errata)
 1             Mr. Morin’s flotation cost adjustment assumes that the market price of the equity issued is
 2         equal to book value. 150 But, as shown in Figure 4 above, the average market-to-book ratio of
 3         the GPC proxy group is 2.1. As implied by the very formula provided by Mr. Morin, when the
 4         market price is greater than book value, new equity issuance is accretive to existing
 5         shareholders – that is, a share of the proceeds of the new issuance accrues to them. 151
 6             Morin derives the flotation cost adjustment from the formula for the extended DCF model
 7         under the assumption of continuous external stock financing:
                                                          𝐷𝐷
 8                                                  𝑘𝑘 = 𝑃𝑃1 + 𝑏𝑏𝑏𝑏 + 𝑠𝑠𝑠𝑠,

 9         where 𝑏𝑏 is the earnings retention ratio, 𝑟𝑟 is ROE, 𝑠𝑠 is the annual rate of new equity issuance
                                                                                                                𝐵𝐵
10         (expressed as a share of existing book equity), and 𝑣𝑣 is the accretion factor, equal to 1 − 𝑃𝑃 . He
                                                                     𝐵𝐵                                              𝐵𝐵
11         then adjusts 𝑃𝑃 for flotation costs, 𝑓𝑓, so that 1 − 𝑃𝑃(1−𝑓𝑓). 152 But as long as (1 − 𝑓𝑓) exceeds 𝑃𝑃 ,

12         i.e., the reciprocal of the market-to-book ratio, existing shareholders gain from new share
13         issuance. Mr. Coyne estimates flotation costs to be 2.47%, so 1 − 𝑓𝑓 = 0.98, much greater than
14         the reciprocal of the GPC proxy group average M/B ratio, 1/2.1 = 0.48.
15   Q. UNDER THE NARUC FAIRNESS PRINCIPLE, ROE SHOULD EQUAL THE
16         ACTUAL COST OF EQUITY. THIS WOULD IMPLY THAT THE MARKET-TO-
17         BOOK RATIO EQUALS 1.0, IN WHICH CASE NEW EQUITY ISSUANCE WOULD
18         NOT BE ACCRETIVE. IF THE COMMISSION ADHERES TO THE NARUC
19         FAIRNESS PRINCIPLE, SHOULD ROE BE ADJUSTED FOR FLOTATION COSTS?
20   A. No. Even if the M/B ratio is equal to 1.0, there is no reason to adjust the ROE for new equity
21         issuance costs.
22             Purchasers of the newly issued equity know that a portion of proceeds will cover
23         transaction costs, and the offering price reflects this information. There is no need to
24         compensate new investors for issuance costs, which they knowingly pay when they purchase
25         new shares, with an adjustment to the ROE. Similarly, existing shareholders know that the
26         company may issue new equity over time. These costs, and their potential effect on expected

 3             Mr. Coyne’s flotation cost adjustment is small and is overwhelmed by the imprecision in
 4         his models. At 0.08%, it amounts to less than 2% of the 8.99%-13.55% range of Mr. Coyne’s
 5         base ROE results. 153 To add a flotation cost adjustment is akin to the elementary school student,

11   V.        GEORGIA POWER’S ROE AND EQUITY RATIO SHOULD OPTIMALLY
12             BALANCE CUSTOMER AND INVESTOR INTERESTS.

13             A.      COE model results must be adjusted for differences in equity ratio.

14   Q. IN SECTION IV.B ABOVE, YOU DISCUSSED ADJUSTING COE MODEL RESULTS
15         FOR DIFFERENCES IN EQUITY RATIOS AMONG THE PROXY GROUP
16         MEMBERS. HOW DO YOU DO THAT?
17   A. The MS DCF and CAPM yield levered costs of equity. To account for differences in capital
18         structure among the proxy group members, the COE results are unlevered to estimate the cost
19         of equity assuming 100% equity financing. 154 The unlevered cost of equity, 𝑘𝑘𝑢𝑢 , is typically
20         expressed as an adjustment to beta in the CAPM:
21                                              𝑘𝑘𝑢𝑢 = 𝑟𝑟𝑓𝑓 + 𝛽𝛽𝑢𝑢 �𝑟𝑟𝑚𝑚 − 𝑟𝑟𝑓𝑓 �, 155
22         where the unlevered beta, 𝛽𝛽𝑢𝑢 , is expressed in terms of the levered equity beta, 𝛽𝛽𝑒𝑒 :


     153
         2022 Rate Case Hr’g Tr. Vol. 1 at 668 (Sept. 28, 2022).
     154
         The unlevered cost of equity differs from the weighted average cost of capital (“WACC”). The unlevered cost
         of capital assumes 100% equity financing; the WACC assumes the company’s current capital structure. While
         under the Modigliani and Miller theorem of capital structure independence, the cost of capital should be the
         same regardless of capital structure, the WACC typically overstates the unlevered cost of equity because the
         expected return on corporate debt is lower than the yield due to default and liquidity risk.
     155
         See, e.g., ASWATH DAMODARAN, DAMODARAN ON VALUATION: SECURITY ANALYSIS FOR INVESTMENT AND
         CORPORATE FINANCE, WILEY 2ND ED. at 129 (2006).

                                              GPSC Docket No. 44280
                                                       Page 76

 8         where 𝐷𝐷 and 𝐸𝐸 refer to debt and equity, respectively. Best practice is to use market, not book,
 9         values for both debt and equity as market reflects investors’ actual exposure; they buy and sell
10         securities at market value, not book. 157 Market values for the debt carried by the proxy group
11         members are not readily available, though, so book value is assumed.

     156
         Unlevered beta is sometimes adjusted for taxes (the “Hamada” adjustment). As explained in Damondaran in
         Valuation, when the capital structure is constant over time, as it is with utilities, then the value of tax shields
         tracks the value of operating assets. Thus, the risk of tax shields will mirror the risk of operating assets and have
         the same discount rate, i.e., the unlevered cost of equity. Id. at 790-93.
     157
         See, e.g., TIM KOLLER, MARC GOEDHART ET AL., VALUATION, 5TH ED. MCKINSEY & CO. 35 at 204 (2010):
         “Where possible, you should estimate market values of the elements of the current capital structure” (emphasis
         added).

 2             The variation in the unlevered COE estimates is much lower than in the levered COEs,

 6         vary considerably, though, from 42% to 74%, which introduces variation in their levered costs
 7         of equity. This variation due to differences in equity ratios makes levered COEs an
 8         inappropriate basis for determining the ROE of a target company, like Georgia Power, which
 9         will likely have a different equity ratio.

 1         more than 25% of its revenue requirement. 163 The total revenue requirement under GPC’s

 3         ratios, under either the Baa1 or A3 target credit rating, reduce total customer costs by over 11%

 6         the revenue requirement ROR to 6.96% and 7.84% under Baa1 and A3 credit rating targets,

 8         minimum-cost scenarios. While total customer costs are lower than under GPC’s proposal,
 9         they are only 50% to 70% of the reduction without an equity ratio constraint. These results
10         further demonstrate the flaw in the “leverage up” (reduce the equity ratio) argument described
11         in Section III.A above. It is much more cost effective to use any spare credit capacity to reduce
12         ROE than to increase debt.

 5   Q. WHAT ARE YOUR ROE AND EQUITY RATIO RECOMMENDATIONS FOR
 6         GEORGIA POWER?
 7   A. I recommend the A3 target credit rating scenario for Georgia Power, 5.54% ROE and 61.7%
 8         equity ratio, for several reasons. Both the A3 and Baa1 scenarios are consistent with the
 9         Supreme Court’s guidance provided in Hope Natural Gas to set “just and reasonable rates”
10         through a “balancing of the investor and the consumer interests.” In contrast, Mr. Coyne’s
11         recommendation and the two equity ratio-constrained scenarios are overly generous to
12         investors at the expense of customers and therefore fail the Hope standard.
13             The A3 and Baa1 scenarios have the same estimated cost to customers over the 2023-25
14         timeframe. This is largely because the analysis assumes the improved credit rating does not
15         materially reduce Georgia Power’s average cost of debt during the 2023-25 timeframe. Beyond
16         2025, though, the average cost of debt would be materially lower under the A3 scenario,
17         reducing customer costs relative to the Baa1 scenario. Figure 30 summarizes my COE analysis
18         and ROE and equity ratio recommendations.

 1   Q. IF       THE       COMMISSION              ADOPTS        YOUR         ROE       AND       EQUITY         RATIO
 2         RECOMMENDATIONS,                    HOW        WOULD          THE        SOUTHERN            COMPANY’S
 3         SHAREHOLDERS BE AFFECTED?
 4   A. ROEs in excess of the cost of equity resembles a zero-sum game, with the interests of
 5         shareholders and customers in direct opposition. If the Commission adopted my recommended
 6         ROE and equity ratio, I would expect The Southern Company share price to experience a one-
 7         time downward adjustment as investors reset their expectations about future returns. Southern
 8         would still be able to access the equity markets, because Georgia Power’s ROE, based on its
 9         actual cost of equity, i.e., investors’ expected return, 166 would be sufficient to satisfy investors’
10         demands. Investors who purchase shares after the one-time adjustment would earn returns
11         comparable to the return current Southern shareholders expect.


     166
           “Cost of equity” and “expected return on equity” are synonymous. See supra footnote 11.

                                                  GPSC Docket No. 44280

                                    PUBLIC DISCLOSURE VERSION
                                                (October 21, 2022 Errata)
 1            I said that ROE above the cost of equity resembles a zero-sum game between shareholders
 2         and customers. It is not quite zero-sum between these two parties, though, because there is a
 3         third party: the tax authorities. ROE is grossed-up for taxes; to the extent ROE is reduced, taxes
 4         are also reduced. In aggregate, a lower ROE provides approximately one-third more benefit to
 5         customers than shareholders lose in foregone profit. 167 As shown in Figure 31, of the $1.04-
 6         billion reduction in revenue requirement return under the A3 scenario (Figure 29, line 9), the
 7         decline in Georgia Power’s net income accounts for $740 million; $190 million is foregone
 8         taxes, of which approximately $150 million (77.5%) is Federal. 168

12                        Ratemaking is indeed but one species of price-fixing. The fixing

          Georgia Power’s marginal tax rate is approximately 25%. The tax gross-up is 1/(1 – 25%) = 11/3. See Ex. APA-

     168
         Georgia corporate income tax rate of 5.75%, Federal, 21%. After deducting state taxes, the net Federal rate is (1

 1                          that the value is reduced does not mean that the regulation is
 2                          invalid. It does, however, indicate that “fair value” is the end
 3                          product of the process of ratemaking, not the starting point, as
 4                          the Circuit Court of Appeals held. The heart of the matter is that
 5                          rates cannot be made to depend upon “fair value” when the value
 6                          of the going enterprise depends on earnings under whatever rates
 7                          may be anticipated. 169


 8         The impact on Southern’s stock price should not factor at all into the Commission’s
 9         determination of the appropriate ROE and equity ratio.

                                                           SUMMARY
Mark E. Ellis is a former utility executive now working as an independent consultant and
testifying expert in finance and economics in utility regulatory proceedings.
Before establishing his own consultancy, Mark led the strategy function at Sempra Energy
(parent of SDG&E and SoCalGas) for fifteen years. Previously, he worked as a consultant in
McKinsey’s energy practice, in international project development for ExxonMobil, and in
industrial demand-side management for Southern California Edison. He has an MS from MIT’s
Technology and Policy Program, where he focused on utility policy and conducted research in
the MIT Energy Lab, and a BS in mechanical engineering from Harvard.
